It does not assert the result for every matrix of rank at most two.

\section{Matrices with one exceptional column or
row}\label{sec:one-special}

\subsection{Statement}\label{special-statement}

Let \(A\) be a nonnegative doubly stochastic matrix of order \(n\ge2\)
with \(n-1\) identical columns. Then

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-1}
\operatorname{per}(A)^2\ge c_n\operatorname{per}(AA^T),
\qquad
\operatorname{per}(A)^2\ge c_n\operatorname{per}(A^TA),
\qquad c_n=\frac{n!}{n^n}.
\end{equation}

Consequently Gyires's inequality holds for this family:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-2}
4\operatorname{per}(A)^2
\ge c_n\left(\operatorname{per}(AA^T)+\operatorname{per}(A^TA)
+2\operatorname{per}(A^2)\right).
\end{equation}

Equality occurs exactly at \(A=J_n\), where every entry of \(J_n\) is
\(1/n\). The same conclusions hold for matrices with \(n-1\) identical
rows.

The first Gram inequality follows from Proposition
\ref{prop:fb-two-column}. The second has the elementary proof below.

\subsection{Parametrization and the first Gram
inequality}\label{special-parametrization-and-the-first-gram-inequality}

Move the special column to the first position. The permanent and both
Gram permanents are unchanged by this column relabeling; the final
Cauchy--Schwarz step can be applied to the original matrix as well.
Every such matrix has columns

\[
u,\ v,\ldots,v,\qquad
u_i\ge0,\quad \sum_i u_i=1,\quad
v_i=\frac{1-u_i}{m},\quad m=n-1.
\]

There are at most two column types. Proposition \ref{prop:fb-two-column}
gives \[
\operatorname{per}(AA^T)\le\operatorname{per}(A),\qquad\operatorname{per}(A)\ge c_n.
\] Therefore \(c_n\operatorname{per}(AA^T)\le\operatorname{per}(A)^2\).
If \(A\ne J_n\), the strict permanent lower bound in that proposition
makes this strong inequality strict. The second Gram inequality needs a
separate argument, since \(A\) can have more than two row types.

\subsection{An exact formula for the other Gram
permanent}\label{special-an-exact-formula-for-the-other-gram-permanent}

Put

\[
a=\sum_i u_i^2,\qquad
b=u\cdot v=\frac{1-a}{m},\qquad
c=\|v\|^2=\frac{n-2+a}{m^2}.
\]

Let \(E_m\) be the \(m\times m\) all-ones matrix. Then

\[
A^TA=
\begin{pmatrix}
a&b\mathbf1^T\\
b\mathbf1&cE_m
\end{pmatrix}.
\]

In its permanent, either the first row uses the first column, or it uses
one of the other \(m\) columns. Counting the two cases gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-3}
\operatorname{per}(A^TA)
=m!\,c^{m-1}(ac+mb^2).
\end{equation}

Let

\[
Q(u)=\sum_i u_i\prod_{j\ne i}(1-u_j)
\]

be the probability of exactly one success among independent Bernoulli
variables with parameters \(u_1,\ldots,u_n\). The permanent is

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-4}
\operatorname{per}(A)=\frac{m!}{m^m}Q(u).
\end{equation}

After substituting \eqref{eq:e-3} and \eqref{eq:e-4}, the second inequality in \eqref{eq:e-1} is
equivalent to

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-5}
Q(u)^2\ge R_n(a),
\end{equation}

where

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-6}
R_n(a)=
n^{1-n}(n-2+a)^{n-2}\bigl(n-1-na+na^2\bigr),
\qquad \frac1n\le a\le1.
\end{equation}

At \(a_0=1/n\),

\[
R_n(a_0)=g_n^2,\qquad
g_n=\left(\frac{n-1}{n}\right)^{n-1}.
\]

\subsection{Two elementary lower bounds for the one-success
probability}\label{special-two-elementary-lower-bounds-for-the-one-success-probability}

First, the product inequality

\[
\prod_{j\ne i}(1-u_j)\ge1-\sum_{j\ne i}u_j=u_i
\]

gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-7}
\boxed{Q(u)\ge a.}
\end{equation}

For the second bound, initially suppose every \(u_i<1\). Weighted
AM--GM, with weights \(u_i\), gives

\[
\log\left(\sum_i\frac{u_i}{1-u_i}\right)
\ge-\sum_i u_i\log(1-u_i).
\]

Using \(Q=\prod_i(1-u_i)\sum_i u_i/(1-u_i)\),

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-8}
\log Q\ge\sum_i(1-u_i)\log(1-u_i).
\end{equation}

Let \(f(x)=(1-x)\log(1-x)\), continuously extended by \(f(1)=0\). Since
\(f''(x)=1/(1-x)\ge1\), strong convexity at \(1/n\) gives

\[
\sum_i f(u_i)\ge nf(1/n)+\frac12\sum_i(u_i-1/n)^2
=\log g_n+\frac12(a-1/n).
\]

Therefore

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-9}
\boxed{Q(u)^2\ge g_n^2e^{a-1/n}.}
\end{equation}

Both bounds extend to boundary points by continuity.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The range
\(1/n\le a\le1/2\)}{The range 1/n to 1/2}}\label{special-the-range-1nle-ale12}

Write

\[
d=n-2+a,\qquad K=n-1-na+na^2.
\]

Differentiating \eqref{eq:e-6},

\[
\frac{d}{da}\log R_n(a)
=\frac{n-2}{d}+\frac{n(2a-1)}K\le1
\]

throughout this range. Integrating from \(1/n\) gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-10}
R_n(a)\le g_n^2e^{a-1/n}\le Q(u)^2.
\end{equation}

For \(n>2\) and \(a>1/n\), the first inequality is strict. For \(n=2\),
this range consists only of the uniform endpoint.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The range
\(1/2\le a\le1\)}{The range 1/2 to 1}}\label{special-the-range-12le-ale1}

A direct algebraic simplification gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-11}
\frac d{da}\log\frac{R_n(a)}{a^2}
=
\frac{(a-1)\left(n(n-2)a^2+na+2(n-1)(n-2)\right)}
{a(n-2+a)(n-1-na+na^2)}
\le0.
\end{equation}

Thus it is enough to show \(R_n(1/2)\le1/4\). At the endpoint,

\[
R_n(1/2)
=\left(1-\frac3{2n}\right)^{n-2}
\left(\frac34-\frac1n\right).
\]

For completeness, set \(x=3/(2n)\) and \(k=n-2\). The nonnegative
binomial series gives

\[
(1-x)^{-k}\ge1+kx+\frac{k(k+1)}2x^2
\ge1+\frac{2k}{n}=3-\frac4n.
\]

The second inequality follows from

\[
\frac{(k+1)x}{2}=\frac{3(n-1)}{4n}\ge\frac13
\]

for \(n\ge2\); the case \(k=0\) is equality directly. Hence
\(R_n(1/2)\le1/4\), with strict inequality for \(n>2\).

Combining this with \eqref{eq:e-11} and \eqref{eq:e-7},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e-12}
R_n(a)\le a^2\le Q(u)^2.
\end{equation}

This proves the second Gram inequality in every order.

\subsection{Equality and Gyires's
inequality}\label{special-equality-and-the-original-gyires-inequality}

The preceding bounds are strict unless \(a=1/n\). By \(\sum_i u_i=1\),
that condition forces \(u_i=1/n\) for all \(i\), so \(A=J_n\).

Lemma \ref{lem:permanent-cs}, applied in the original indexing, gives \[
\operatorname{per}(A^2)\le\sqrt{\operatorname{per}(AA^T)\operatorname{per}(A^TA)}
\le\frac12\left(\operatorname{per}(AA^T)+\operatorname{per}(A^TA)\right).
\]

Applying both inequalities in \eqref{eq:e-1} now proves \eqref{eq:e-2}, with equality only
at \(J_n\). Transposition proves the corresponding statement when
\(n-1\) rows are identical.

The identical-column hypothesis permits the exceptional column to have any number of distinct entries.
It still excludes general rank-two matrices.


\section{All rank-two matrices in orders two through fifteen}\label{sec:rank-two-finite}

The four-block condition restricts the number of distinct rows and columns.
A general matrix of rank at most two can have more row and column types.
The next result covers all such doubly stochastic matrices in a finite range of orders.
Its proof combines an analytic reduction with exact integer polynomial certificates.

\begin{theorem}[Finite-order rank-two theorem]\label{thm:rank-two-finite}
Let $2\le n\le15$, let $A\in\Omega_n$, and suppose $\operatorname{rank}(A)\le2$.
Set $d=\per(A)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-contractions}
\per(AA^\T)\le d,\qquad \per(A^\T A)\le d,\qquad \per(A^2)\le d.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-margin}
\Delta_n(A)\ge4d(d-c_n)\ge0,
\qquad c_n\per(AA^\T)\le d^2.
\end{equation}
Equality in Gyires's inequality, or in the last strong inequality, holds exactly when $A=J_n$.
\end{theorem}

The contraction itself can have other equality cases.
For example, a direct sum of two uniform stochastic blocks has rank two and satisfies $AA^\T=A$.
The order-one case is immediate.
Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-two-finite} makes no claim for a general rank-two matrix of order greater than fifteen.

\subsection{A representation of every rank-two matrix}

A rank-one doubly stochastic matrix is $J_n$.
Indeed, its rows are proportional, and their common row sum makes them equal.
The column sums then determine every entry.
Suppose that $A$ is nonuniform and has rank at most two.
Its row space has dimension at most two.
The row-sum functional is nonzero on this space, so its intersection with the row-sum-one hyperplane has affine dimension at most one.
The rows therefore lie on a line in the probability simplex.

Let $b^{(1)}$ and $b^{(0)}$ be the two endpoint rows of their convex hull.
Write row $i$ as $x_i b^{(1)}+(1-x_i)b^{(0)}$, where $0\le x_i\le1$.
Both endpoints occur, so $t:=\sum_i x_i$ satisfies $0<t<n$.
The column sums give
\[
t b^{(1)}_j+(n-t)b^{(0)}_j=1.
\]
Set $y_j=t b^{(1)}_j$.
Then $0\le y_j\le1$ and $\sum_jy_j=t$.
This gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-representation}
A_{ij}=\frac{x_i y_j}{t}+\frac{(1-x_i)(1-y_j)}{n-t},
\qquad x,y\in[0,1]^n,\quad \sum_i x_i=\sum_j y_j=t.
\end{equation}
Conversely, these conditions make~\eqref{eq:r2-representation} a nonnegative doubly stochastic matrix of rank at most two.
Each summand has rank at most one.
The row sum is $x_i+(1-x_i)=1$, and the column sum follows in the same way.

For fixed $n,t$, define
\[
\theta=\frac tn,\quad \sigma=\theta(1-\theta)>0,
\quad u=x-\theta\mathbf1,\quad v=y-\theta\mathbf1.
\]
Then $\sum_i u_i=\sum_jv_j=0$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-centered}
A=J_n+\frac{uv^\T}{n\sigma}.
\end{equation}
Let $e_k$ denote the elementary symmetric polynomial of degree $k$, with $e_0=1$.
Put
\[
w_k=\frac{k!(n-k)!}{n!\sigma^k},
\qquad \kappa_y=\frac{\sum_jv_j^2}{n\sigma}.
\]
Since $\sum_j y_j^2\le\sum_jy_j=t$, $0\le\kappa_y\le1$.

Expanding the permanent by the rows and columns that use the rank-one term gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-cross-expansion}
S(x,y):=\frac{\per(A)}{c_n}
=\sum_{k=0}^n w_k e_k(u)e_k(v).
\end{equation}
For fixed sets of $k$ rows and $k$ columns, there are $k!$ matchings using that term.
The other rows and columns contribute $(n-k)!/n^{n-k}$.
The factor $(n\sigma)^{-k}$ and division by $c_n$ give the displayed coefficient.

The zero sums of $u,v$ make the cross terms with $J_n$ vanish.
Thus
\[
AA^\T=J_n+\frac{\kappa_y}{n\sigma}uu^\T,
\]
and the same expansion yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-gram-expansion}
Q(x,y):=\frac{\per(AA^\T)}{c_n}
=\sum_{k=0}^n w_k e_k(u)^2\kappa_y^k.
\end{equation}
The zeroth term is one and the first term is zero in both expansions.
Every term in~\eqref{eq:r2-gram-expansion} is nonnegative.

\subsection{Separate convexity in the probability laws}

Let $P_x$ be the law of a sum of independent Bernoulli variables with parameters $x_1,\ldots,x_n$.
Its probability generating polynomial is
\[
F_{P_x}(z)=\sum_{j=0}^nP_x(j)z^j=\prod_i(1-x_i+x_i z).
\]
At fixed $n,t$, the identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-law-transform}
\prod_i\bigl(1+(x_i-\theta)z\bigr)
=(1-\theta z)^n F_{P_x}\!\left(1+\frac{z}{1-\theta z}\right)
\end{equation}
shows that every $e_k(u)$ is a linear functional of $P_x$.
To verify the identity, multiply each factor on the right by its factor $1-\theta z$.
The resulting factor is $1+(x_i-\theta)z$.
The right side is therefore a polynomial despite its rational notation.
Also, $\sum_jv_j=0$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-kappa-linear}
\kappa_y=-\frac{2e_2(v)}{n\sigma}.
\end{equation}
Thus $\kappa_y$ is linear in the second probability law at fixed mean.

Consider the contraction deficit $E(P_x,P_y)=Q(x,y)-S(x,y)$.
For fixed $P_y$, it is a nonnegative weighted sum of squares of linear functionals of $P_x$, minus a linear functional.
It is therefore convex in $P_x$.
For fixed $P_x$, it is one plus a sum of nonnegative multiples of $\kappa_y^k$, $k\ge2$, minus a linear functional of $P_y$.
The powers are convex for $\kappa_y\ge0$, so it is convex in $P_y$ as well.
These conclusions remain valid on the convex hull of the fixed-mean laws, because~\eqref{eq:r2-kappa-linear} stays nonnegative there.
Only separate convexity is needed.

\begin{lemma}[Convex fixed-mean extremizer]\label{lem:r2-convex-extremizer}
Fix $t\in[0,n]$.
A continuous convex functional of a Poisson-binomial law with mean $t$ attains its maximum at parameters whose values in $(0,1)$ are all equal.
Here convexity means convexity on a set containing the convex hull of the admissible laws.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The parameter set $\{x\in[0,1]^n:\sum_i x_i=t\}$ is compact.
The law depends continuously on the parameters, so a maximum exists.
Among all maximizers, choose one that maximizes $\sum_i x_i^2$.
Suppose two unequal parameters $p,q$ lie in $(0,1)$.
Keep $s=p+q$ fixed and vary $z=pq$.
Their generating factor is
\[
(1-p+pw)(1-q+qw)=(1-s)+sw+z(1-w)^2.
\]
The full law is affine in $z$.
Its feasible interval is $\max(0,s-1)\le z\le s^2/4$.
The current $z$ lies strictly between the endpoints.
The upper endpoint makes the parameters equal, and the lower endpoint makes one parameter zero or one.

The objective is convex in $z$.
Write the current point as a strict convex combination of the two endpoints.
Both endpoint values are at most the global maximum, while convexity bounds the current maximum by their weighted average.
Both endpoint values must therefore equal the maximum.
Moving to the lower endpoint preserves the objective and strictly increases $p^2+q^2=s^2-2z$.
This contradicts the secondary maximization.
Thus all interior parameters are equal.
\end{proof}

The linear case is the classical fixed-mean reduction used in the four-block argument~\cite{hoeffding56}.
The proof above gives the convex version required here.
Maximize $E$ over the two compact parameter sets at fixed $n,t$.
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:r2-convex-extremizer} to one law and then to the other.
Each replacement preserves the global maximum.
The maximum is therefore attained by two profiles of the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-profiles}
\begin{aligned}
x&=(1^a,q^m,0^{n-a-m}),&q&=\frac{t-a}{m},\\
y&=(1^c,r^\ell,0^{n-c-\ell}),&r&=\frac{t-c}{\ell}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
An exponent here specifies the number of repeated entries.
The integer indices satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-overlap}
a,c\ge0,\quad m,\ell\ge1,\quad a+m,c+\ell\le n,
\quad L:=\max(a,c)\le t\le R:=\min(a+m,c+\ell).
\end{equation}
Deterministic laws are represented by endpoints, as shown next.

\subsection{Complete coverage of the profiles and endpoints}

The profile $(a,m)=(0,n)$ makes $x_i=t/n$ for every $i$.
It gives $u=0$ and $A=J_n$ regardless of $y$.
The same holds for the corresponding profile in the second slot.
Those profiles can be omitted because $S=Q=1$.

A deterministic law with mean $h\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}$ has $h$ parameters equal to one and the rest zero.
It is the endpoint $t=h$ of the profile $(a,m)=(h,1)$.
A nondeterministic partner has a support interval with $h$ in its interior.
Its integer upper endpoint is at least $h+1$, so its overlap with $[h,h+1]$ has positive length.
Two deterministic laws with the same mean can both use $(h,1)$.
Thus every required pair has a representation with $L<R$ or is already a uniform case.

There is also a direct check for any discarded singleton overlap $L=R$.
Both profiles then occur at endpoints, so both parameter vectors are binary with $L$ ones.
Up to row and column permutations, their matrix is
$\operatorname{diag}(J_L,J_{n-L})$.
Its Gram matrix is itself, so $S=Q$.
An overlap with $L>R$ is infeasible.

For $L<R$, the interval endpoints with $0<t<n$ are included by the same formulas or continuity.
The original representation of a nonuniform matrix already has $0<t<n$.
The values $t=0,n$ therefore omit no such matrix.
For example, as $t\to0$, each $x_i,y_j\le t$, so the first term of~\eqref{eq:r2-representation} tends to zero and the second tends to $1/n$.
The limit at $n$ follows by complementing the parameters.

Permuting $x$ or $y$ independently permutes rows or columns of $A$.
Both $\per(A)$ and $\per(AA^\T)$ are invariant under these operations.
The certificate retains every ordered pair of profiles.
It does not assume symmetry of the contraction under exchange of $x$ and $y$.

\subsection{Homogeneous integer certificates}

Fix an admissible pair with $L<R$.
For $U,V\ge0$ with $U+V>0$, put
\[
T=LU+RV,\quad W=(n-L)U+(n-R)V,\quad
D=TW,\quad t=\frac{T}{U+V},
\]
and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-K}
K=\ell D-n\bigl((L-c)U+(R-c)V\bigr)
                 \bigl((\ell+c-L)U+(\ell+c-R)V\bigr).
\end{equation}
For $0<t<n$, $D>0$ and
\[
\sigma=\frac{D}{n^2(U+V)^2},\qquad \kappa_y=\frac{K}{\ell D}.
\]
The second identity follows from
$\sum_j y_j(1-y_j)=\ell r(1-r)=(t-c)(\ell+c-t)/\ell$
and $\sum_jv_j^2=n\sigma-\sum_jy_j(1-y_j)$.

Let $X_k$ be the elementary symmetric polynomial of degree $k$ in the following $n$ linear forms:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-linear-forms}
\begin{array}{ll}
mW,&a\text{ copies},\\
\bigl(n(L-a)-mL\bigr)U+\bigl(n(R-a)-mR\bigr)V,
   &m\text{ copies},\\
-mT,&n-a-m\text{ copies}.
\end{array}
\end{equation}
They are precisely the quantities $nm(U+V)(x_i-\theta)$.
Define $Y_k$ by replacing $(a,m)$ with $(c,\ell)$.
Thus
\[
e_k(u)=\frac{X_k}{(nm(U+V))^k},\qquad
e_k(v)=\frac{Y_k}{(n\ell(U+V))^k}.
\]
Set
\begin{align}
\mathcal C
 &=\sum_{k=0}^n k!(n-k)!(m\ell)^{n-k}X_kY_kD^{n-k},
 \label{eq:r2-C}\\
\mathcal G
 &=\sum_{k=0}^n k!(n-k)!m^{2(n-k)}\ell^{n-k}
      X_k^2K^kD^{2(n-k)}.
 \label{eq:r2-G}
\end{align}
Every coefficient is an integer.
Substitution into~\eqref{eq:r2-cross-expansion} and~\eqref{eq:r2-gram-expansion} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-denominators}
S=\frac{\mathcal C}{n!m^n\ell^nD^n},\qquad
Q=\frac{\mathcal G}{n!m^{2n}\ell^nD^{2n}}.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{align}
S-Q&=\frac{\mathcal H}{n!m^{2n}\ell^nD^{2n}},
&\mathcal H&:=m^n\mathcal C D^n-\mathcal G,
\label{eq:r2-H}\\
S^2-Q&=\frac{\mathcal S}{(n!)^2m^{2n}\ell^{2n}D^{2n}},
&\mathcal S&:=\mathcal C^2-n!\ell^n\mathcal G.
\label{eq:r2-strong-numerator}
\end{align}
The denominators are positive on the relevant parameter range.
The polynomial $\mathcal C$ has degree $2n$.
Both deficit numerators have degree $4n$.
Nonnegative coefficients of $U^{4n-j}V^j$ therefore prove their nonnegativity for all $U,V\ge0$.
This proves inequalities on whole mean intervals.
The same signs are the Bernstein coefficient signs on $[L,R]$, because the basis conversion divides only by positive binomial coefficients.

\subsection{The exhaustive finite calculation}

The program \nolinkurl{rank2_bernstein.py} implements
\eqref{eq:r2-linear-forms}--\eqref{eq:r2-strong-numerator} with arbitrary-precision Python integers.
For a homogeneous polynomial of degree $d$, array position $j$ stores the coefficient of $U^{d-j}V^j$.
All $d+1$ positions are retained, including zeros.
Addition is entrywise and multiplication is coefficient convolution.
For linear forms $b_1,\ldots,b_n$, the elementary symmetric coefficients are constructed from
\[
e_0^{(0)}=1,\qquad e_k^{(0)}=0\ (k>0),\qquad
e_k^{(i)}=e_k^{(i-1)}+b_i e_{k-1}^{(i-1)}.
\]
The implementation updates $k$ in decreasing order, so every right-side value belongs to the previous stage.

For each order it uses exactly the profile list
\[
\mathcal P_n=\{(a,m):0\le a<n,\ 1\le m\le n-a\}\setminus\{(0,n)\}.
\]
It checks every ordered pair in $\mathcal P_n^2$ with $L<R$.
For each pair it constructs both degree-$4n$ numerator arrays and checks every coefficient.
It records negative-coefficient failures, zero counts, and a SHA-256 digest of the complete ordered coefficient stream.
It stops with an error if any negative coefficient occurs.
The exhaustive mode uses no sampling flag.

The exhaustive computation gives the following counts.
The coefficient column includes both numerator arrays.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{rrr}
\toprule
Order & Ordered profile intervals & Coefficient positions\\
\midrule
2&2&36\\
3&15&390\\
4&51&1\,734\\
5&126&5\,292\\
6&260&13\,000\\
7&477&27\,666\\
8&805&53\,130\\
9&1\,276&94\,424\\
10&1\,926&157\,932\\
11&2\,795&251\,550\\
12&3\,927&384\,846\\
13&5\,370&569\,220\\
14&7\,176&818\,064\\
15&9\,401&1\,146\,922\\
\midrule
Total&33\,607&3\,524\,206\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Every coefficient is nonnegative.
The certificate therefore proves $S-Q\ge0$ on each enumerated interval.
The representation, convex reduction, and coverage argument extend this result to every rank-two doubly stochastic matrix in the stated orders.
The independently checked $\mathcal S$ arrays also verify the strong inequality on each profile interval.
The main theorem needs only the contraction certificate.

A separate rational implementation starts from polynomials in the mean $t$ and then converts to Bernstein coefficients.
It agrees coefficient by coefficient with the integer construction on all $454$ profile intervals in orders two through six.
This compares $20\,452$ positions exactly.
A direct subset-permanent computation checks $378$ profile pairs in orders two through seven.
It gives $1\,390$ exact identity comparisons, $21$ deterministic cases, and $251$ uniform-profile instances.
A second direct computation checks another $209$ rational instances, including valid endpoints.
These finite checks test the identities and implementation.
The universal parameter coverage comes from the preceding analytic proof and the exhaustive coefficient signs.

The four entry points use the Python standard library:
\begin{verbatim}
python3 rank2_bernstein.py --first 2 --last 15
python3 rank2_bernstein_fraction_check.py --first 2 --last 6
python3 rank2_identity_audit.py
python3 rank2_boundary_audit.py
\end{verbatim}
They are included with the accompanying source and verification records.
The first two JSON reports contain timing fields, which can change on a repeated run.
The coefficient digests, counts, and mathematical results are the reproducibility criteria.

\subsection{Conclusion and equality}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-two-finite}]
The certificate and analytic reduction give
$\per(AA^\T)\le\per(A)$ for every matrix in the stated range.
Suppose now that $A\ne J_n$. Both centered vectors in~\eqref{eq:r2-centered} are then nonzero. Consequently $\kappa_y>0$, and
\[
e_2(u)=-\frac12\sum_i u_i^2<0.
\]
The $k=2$ term of~\eqref{eq:r2-gram-expansion} is strictly positive.
All its other terms are nonnegative, and its zeroth term is one.
Hence
\[
d\ge\per(AA^\T)>c_n.
\]
Transposition preserves the rank condition, so the same contraction applies to $A^\T$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:permanent-cs} then gives the third contraction in~\eqref{eq:r2-contractions}, in the original indexing.
Their weighted sum gives
\[
\Delta_n(A)\ge4d(d-c_n)>0.
\]
The strong inequality is also strict, since
\[
d^2\ge d\per(AA^\T)>c_n\per(AA^\T).
\]
At $A=J_n$, every product matrix in the statement is $J_n$ and every corresponding permanent is $c_n$.
Thus equality holds in both inequalities precisely in this case.
\end{proof}

All row and column types are included in these orders.
The result does not remove the order cutoff for arbitrary rank-two matrices.
A proof of the coefficient signs uniformly in $n$, or a different all-order argument, remains necessary.


\section{Closure, sparse perturbations, and boundary reduction}\label{sec:structural}

Direct sums and row and column permutations extend the matrix families above.
The sparse-perturbation theorems give additional noncirculant families and explicit margins.
The final boundary theorem is a reduction of the unrestricted strong inequality.
It does not prove the required boundary inequality.

\subsection{Direct sums and independent permutations}

\begin{lemma}[Closure of the strong bounds]\label{lem:struct-direct-sum}
Let $B_i\in\Omega_{m_i}$ for $1\le i\le r$, with $m_i\ge1$ and $\sum_i m_i=n$.
Suppose each $B_i$ satisfies both strong bounds~\eqref{eq:strong-pair} in its own dimension.
Then their direct sum $B=\bigoplus_i B_i$ satisfies both strong bounds in dimension $n$.
The same holds for $A=UBV$, where $U,V$ are arbitrary permutation matrices.
Each such $A$ satisfies Gyires's inequality.
If $r\ge2$, both strong bounds and Gyires's inequality are strict.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Permanents multiply over direct sums. Their two Gram matrices also decompose into the corresponding direct sums.
Thus
\[
 \per(B)^2\ge\left(\prod_i c_{m_i}\right)\per(BB^\T),
 \qquad
 \per(B)^2\ge\left(\prod_i c_{m_i}\right)\per(B^\T B).
\]
The multinomial theorem gives
\[
 1=\left(\sum_i\frac{m_i}{n}\right)^n
 \ge\frac{n!}{\prod_i m_i!}\prod_i\left(\frac{m_i}{n}\right)^{m_i}
 =\frac{c_n}{\prod_i c_{m_i}}.
\]
For $r\ge2$, there are other positive terms in that multinomial expansion.
The displayed inequality is then strict.
Both Gram permanents are positive because their positive diagonal entries supply a positive permanent term.
This proves the strong bounds and their strictness.

For $A=UBV$, one has
\[
 AA^\T=U(BB^\T)U^\T,
 \qquad A^\T A=V^\T(B^\T B)V,
 \qquad \per(A)=\per(B).
\]
Permutation invariance therefore preserves both strong bounds.
Lemma~\ref{lem:gram} now gives Gyires's inequality, with strictness when both bounds are strict.
This last step applies to the original indexing of $A^2$.
\end{proof}

This lemma permits different admissible matrices in different blocks.
For example, different permutation cycles can use different nonnegative triples of weights after stochastic normalization.
It also applies to blocks from the other proved matrix families.

\subsection{Positive-semidefinite matrices}

A real symmetric matrix is positive semidefinite if $x^\T Ax\ge0$ for every real vector $x$.
The next proposition follows from monotonicity in positive-semidefinite order and the van der Waerden bound.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:struct-psd}
If $A\in\Omega_n$ is symmetric and positive semidefinite, then it satisfies both strong bounds and Gyires's inequality.
Equality in any of these inequalities holds only at $J_n$.
The conclusion is preserved by independent row and column permutations.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every eigenvalue of $A$ lies in $[0,1]$.
Nonnegativity and row sums one bound the spectral radius by one, while positive semidefiniteness gives the lower bound.
Hence $0\preceq A^2\preceq A$.
The permanent is increasing in positive-semidefinite order.
To see this, if $0\preceq X\preceq Y$, a telescoping expansion gives
$X^{\otimes n}\preceq Y^{\otimes n}$.
Each term in the difference is a tensor product of positive-semidefinite matrices.
For
\[
 v=\frac1{\sqrt{n!}}\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}
 e_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{\sigma(n)},
\]
direct expansion gives $v^\T X^{\otimes n}v=\per(X)$.
Thus $\per(A^2)\le\per(A)$.
The van der Waerden bound and its equality case give
\[
 c_n\per(A^2)\le c_n\per(A)\le\per(A)^2,
\]
with the second inequality strict when $A\ne J_n$.
Since both Gram matrices equal $A^2$, this proves all assertions for $A$.
The permutation formulas in Lemma~\ref{lem:struct-direct-sum} preserve the Gram bounds.
Lemma~\ref{lem:gram} gives the final conclusion after relabeling.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Three exceptional rows and an exact cubic expansion}

A row is called exceptional if it differs from the corresponding row of $J_n$.
For $n\ge5$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:struct-K}
 K_n=\frac{6(n-5)^4+77(n-5)^3+333(n-5)^2+515(n-5)+149}
 {6(n-1)^2(n-2)^2}>0.
\end{equation}
The squared Frobenius norm is $\|B\|_F^2=\sum_{i,j}b_{ij}^2$.

\begin{theorem}[Three exceptional rows]\label{thm:struct-three-row}
Let $A\in\Omega_n$, with $n\ge5$, and suppose at most three rows are exceptional.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:struct-three-row}
 \per(A)^2-c_n\per(AA^\T)
 \ge c_n^2K_n\|A-J_n\|_F^2.
\end{equation}
Equality in the corresponding strong inequality occurs only at $J_n$.
If $A-J_n$ is supported on at most three rows and at most three columns, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:struct-three-block}
 \Delta_n(A)\ge4c_n^2K_n\|A-J_n\|_F^2.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Permute rows so that the first three contain all exceptions.
Set $B=A-J_n$, $s=\sum b_{ij}^2$, and $t=\sum b_{ij}^3$.
Both row sums and column sums of $B$ vanish.
Every entry satisfies $b_{ij}\ge-1/n$.
For a zero-sum matrix $B$, let $M_k(B)$ be the sum of the permanents of all its $k\times k$ submatrices, with $M_0=1$.
Expansion by the entries taken from $B$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:struct-minor-expansion}
 \per(J_n+B)=\sum_{k=0}^n(n-k)!n^{-(n-k)}M_k(B).
\end{equation}
The zero sums imply
\[
 M_1(B)=0,\qquad M_2(B)=\frac12\sum b_{ij}^2,
 \qquad M_3(B)=\frac23\sum b_{ij}^3.
\]
Here $k!M_k$ is the sum over ordered distinct row indices and ordered distinct column indices.
Apply inclusion-exclusion to these distinctness conditions.
A row or column index that occurs only once makes the unrestricted sum zero.
At $k=2$, only the identification of both row indices and both column indices survives.
At $k=3$, only the identification of all three row indices and all three column indices survives.
The two inclusion-exclusion factors in that case are both $2$.
Division by $2!$ or $3!$ gives the stated identities.
Since only three rows of $B$ can be nonzero, every $M_k(B)$ with $k>3$ vanishes.
Put
\[
 \alpha=\frac{n}{2(n-1)},\qquad
 \beta=\frac{2n^2}{3(n-1)(n-2)}.
\]
Equation~\eqref{eq:struct-minor-expansion} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:struct-cubic-permanent}
 \frac{\per(A)}{c_n}=1+\alpha s+\beta t.
\end{equation}

In each column, the three potentially nonzero deviations $x,y,z$ have sum zero and are at least $-1/n$.
They satisfy
\[
 x^3+y^3+z^3\ge-\frac{x^2+y^2+z^2}{2n}.
\]
If the cubic sum is negative, exactly one entry is negative.
Write it as $-r$, with $0<r\le1/n$, and the others as $u,v\ge0$, where $u+v=r$.
The cubic sum is $-3ruv$.
Also $r^2+u^2+v^2=2r^2-2uv\ge6uv$, since $r^2\ge4uv$.
This proves the inequality; a nonnegative cubic sum needs no estimate.
Summing over columns gives $t\ge-s/(2n)$.
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:struct-lower}
 \frac{\per(A)}{c_n}\ge1+as,
 \qquad a=\alpha-\frac\beta{2n}
 =\frac{n(3n-8)}{6(n-1)(n-2)}>0.
\end{equation}
The three exceptional entries of each column of $A$ sum to $3/n$.
Their squared deviations therefore sum to at most
$(3/n)^2-3/n^2=6/n^2$.
Thus $0\le s\le6/n$.

Let $D=BB^\T$.
It is positive semidefinite, has zero row sums, and is supported on three rows and three columns.
Its trace is $s$, and $AA^\T=J_n+D$.
Write $E=\sum d_{ij}^2$ and $U=\sum d_{ij}^3$.
The nonnegative eigenvalues give $E\le s^2$.
Also,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:struct-cubic-psd}
 0\le U\le s^3/6.
\end{equation}
For this estimate, use the plane
$H=\{x\in\R^3:x_1+x_2+x_3=0\}$.
For $x\in H$, the restriction of $\operatorname{diag}(x)$ to the orthonormal basis
$(1,-1,0)/\sqrt2$, $(1,1,-2)/\sqrt6$ is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
 -x_3/2&(x_1-x_2)/(2\sqrt3)\\
 (x_1-x_2)/(2\sqrt3)&x_3/2
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Its eigenvalues are $\pm\|x\|/\sqrt6$.
Hence for $x,y,z\in H$,
\[
 \left|\sum_i x_i y_i z_i\right|
 \le\frac1{\sqrt6}\|x\|\|y\|\|z\|.
\]
Take a spectral decomposition
$D=\sum_r\lambda_r u_ru_r^\T$ with $\lambda_r\ge0$ and unit $u_r\in H$.
Then
\[
 U=\sum_{r,s,t}\lambda_r\lambda_s\lambda_t
 \left(\sum_i u_{r,i}u_{s,i}u_{t,i}\right)^2
 \le\frac16\left(\sum_r\lambda_r\right)^3.
\]
This proves~\eqref{eq:struct-cubic-psd}.
Apply the same cubic permanent expansion to $D$ to obtain
\[
 \frac{\per(AA^\T)}{c_n}
 =1+\alpha E+\beta U
 \le1+\alpha s^2+\frac\beta6s^3.
\]
Together with~\eqref{eq:struct-lower}, this yields
\[
 \frac{\per(A)^2-c_n\per(AA^\T)}{c_n^2}
 \ge sF_n(s),
 \qquad
 F_n(s)=2a+(a^2-\alpha)s-\frac\beta6s^2.
\]
One has $a\le2/3$ and $\alpha\ge1/2$, so $a^2-\alpha<0$.
Thus $F_n$ decreases for $s\ge0$.
Direct algebra gives
\[
 F_n(6/n)=
 \frac{6n^4-43n^3+78n^2-40n+24}{6(n-1)^2(n-2)^2}=K_n.
\]
The expansion in~\eqref{eq:struct-K} proves $K_n>0$ for $n\ge5$.
This proves~\eqref{eq:struct-three-row} and its strictness away from $J_n$.

Under the additional column-support condition, apply the same theorem to $A^\T$.
Both Gram permanents are then at most
$\per(A)^2/c_n-c_nK_ns$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:gram} gives
$2\per(A^2)\le\per(AA^\T)+\per(A^\T A)$.
Adding the two estimates proves~\eqref{eq:struct-three-block}.
\end{proof}

For example, replace any chosen $3\times3$ block of $J_n$ by $(3/n)S$, where $S\in\Omega_3$.
The resulting matrix satisfies the support condition in~\eqref{eq:struct-three-block}.
The chosen row set and column set need not agree.
The family includes zero entries.
The one-sided statement~\eqref{eq:struct-three-row} alone does not imply Gyires's inequality without a bound for the other Gram matrix.

\subsection{A trace bound for centered positive-semidefinite perturbations}

The next estimate controls that other Gram matrix when the order is sufficiently large.
It imposes no restriction on the number of nonzero rows of its input.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:struct-psd-trace}
Let $D$ be a real positive-semidefinite $n\times n$ matrix, with $n\ge3$, zero row sums, and trace $s<1/e$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:struct-psd-trace}
 \frac{\per(J_n+D)}{c_n}
 \le1+\frac{n}{2(n-1)}s^2
 +\frac{2n^2}{3(n-1)(n-2)}s^3
 +\frac{(es)^4}{1-es}.
\end{equation}
Neither $D$ nor $J_n+D$ is required to have nonnegative entries.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $Y$ be a centered circular complex Gaussian vector with covariance $D$.
For example, take independent standard circular complex Gaussian coordinates and apply $D^{1/2}$.
The expectation of the squared absolute value of $\sum_iY_i$ is $\mathbf1^\T D\mathbf1=0$.
Thus $\sum_iY_i=0$ almost surely.
Let $Z$ be an independent standard circular complex Gaussian variable.
Gaussian moment pairing gives
\[
 \mathbb E\prod_i|W_i|^2=\per(\mathbb E[WW^*]).
\]
Indeed, after expansion in independent standard coordinates, a mixed moment is zero unless the two coordinate multiplicities agree.
When they agree with multiplicities $\alpha$, the moment is $\alpha!$.
This is the same counting identity as Lemma~\ref{lem:coefficient-inner-product}.
Apply it to $W_i=Z/\sqrt n+Y_i$ and expand the product.
Rotational symmetry eliminates terms with different powers of $Z$.
Since $\mathbb E|Z|^{2j}=j!$, one obtains
\begin{equation}\label{eq:struct-gaussian-expansion}
 \frac{\per(J_n+D)}{c_n}
 =\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{n^k}{(n)_k}\mathbb E|e_k(Y)|^2,
 \qquad (n)_k=n(n-1)\cdots(n-k+1).
\end{equation}
Here $e_k$ is the elementary symmetric polynomial of degree $k$.
The zeroth term is one. The first term is zero.

If $\sum_i y_i=0$, the elementary inequality
$|1+w|\le\exp(\operatorname{Re}w+|w|^2/2)$ gives
\[
 \left|\prod_i(1+zy_i)\right|
 \le\exp\bigl(|z|^2\|y\|^2/2\bigr).
\]
Cauchy's coefficient estimate on a circle of radius $r$, optimized at
$r^2=k/\|y\|^2$, gives
$|e_k(y)|^2\le(e/k)^k\|y\|^{2k}$ for $k\ge1$.
The zero vector satisfies this directly.
If $\lambda_i\ge0$ are the eigenvalues of $D$, Gaussian moments give
\[
 \mathbb E\|Y\|^{2k}
 =k!\sum_{|\alpha|=k}\prod_i\lambda_i^{\alpha_i}
 \le k!\left(\sum_i\lambda_i\right)^k=k!s^k.
\]
Thus the $k$th term of~\eqref{eq:struct-gaussian-expansion} is at most
\[
 \frac{n^k}{(n)_k}\left(\frac ek\right)^k k!s^k
 =\frac{(ens/k)^k}{\binom nk}\le(es)^k.
\]
The last inequality uses
$\binom nk=\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(n-i)/(k-i)\ge(n/k)^k$.

The exact low-degree identities from~\eqref{eq:struct-minor-expansion} give the second term
$\alpha\sum d_{ij}^2$ and third term $\beta\sum d_{ij}^3$, with the same $\alpha,\beta$ as above.
Positive semidefiniteness gives
$\sum d_{ij}^2\le s^2$ and
$|d_{ij}|\le\sqrt{d_{ii}d_{jj}}\le s$.
Consequently $\sum d_{ij}^3\le\sum|d_{ij}|^3\le s^3$.
Use these two sharper bounds and sum the geometric tail from degree four.
This proves~\eqref{eq:struct-psd-trace}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Three exceptional rows in orders at least 31]\label{thm:struct-three-row-31}
Let $A\in\Omega_n$ with $n\ge31$.
If at most three rows are exceptional, then, for
$\gamma=9301/445904>0$,
\[
 \per(A)^2-c_n\per(AA^\T)\ge c_n^2\gamma\|A-J_n\|_F^2,
 \qquad
 \per(A)^2-c_n\per(A^\T A)\ge c_n^2\gamma\|A-J_n\|_F^2.
\]
Hence
$\Delta_n(A)\ge4c_n^2\gamma\|A-J_n\|_F^2$.
Equality in either strong inequality or in Gyires's inequality occurs only at $J_n$.
The same conclusions hold if at most three columns are exceptional.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use $B,s,a,\alpha,\beta$ from Theorem~\ref{thm:struct-three-row}.
Then $\per(A)/c_n\ge1+as$, $s\le6/n$, and $a\ge1/2$ for $n\ge6$.
Both $BB^\T$ and $B^\T B$ are positive semidefinite, with zero row sums and trace $s$.
For $n\ge31$, one has $s\le6/31<1/e$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:struct-psd-trace} therefore applies to both Gram matrices.
Also $a^2-\alpha\ge-1/3$ and $\beta\le3/4$.
The first follows from $a^2\ge1/4$ and $\alpha\le31/60<7/12$.
The second is equivalent to $n^2-27n+18\ge0$.
For either Gram matrix $G$, these bounds give
\[
 \frac{\per(A)^2-c_n\per(G)}{c_n^2}
 \ge s\left(1-\frac s3-\frac{3s^2}4-\frac{e^4s^3}{1-es}\right).
\]
Every subtracted term increases with $s$ on $[0,1/e)$.
Use $s\le6/31$ and $e<11/4$ to obtain
\[
 1-\frac2{31}-\frac{27}{961}
 -\frac{(11/4)^4(6/31)^3}{1-(11/4)(6/31)}
 =\frac{9301}{445904}.
\]
For the elementary exponential estimate, the series for $e$ gives
$e<8/3+(1/24)\sum_{j\ge0}5^{-j}=87/32<11/4$.
The two Gram bounds follow.
Lemma~\ref{lem:gram} gives the Gyires margin.
Transposition proves the column version.
\end{proof}

The threshold $31$ is sufficient for these estimates.
It is not claimed to be optimal.
For orders $5$ through $30$, Theorem~\ref{thm:struct-three-row} gives the full Gyires conclusion only with its additional three-column support condition.

\subsection{The symmetrized denominator along a radial segment}

For a matrix with all row sums and column sums one, define
\[
 \mathcal Q(A)=\frac{\per(AA^\T)+\per(A^\T A)+2\per(A^2)}4,
 \qquad r_A=\frac{p_A+p_{A^\T}}2.
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:coefficient-inner-product} gives
\[
 \mathcal Q(A)=\|r_A\|_F^2,
 \qquad [z_1\cdots z_n]r_A=\per(A).
\]
One quarter of Gyires's denominator is therefore the squared Fischer norm of the average of the row-product and column-product polynomials.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:struct-radial}
Let $n\ge2$, $B\mathbf1=B^\T\mathbf1=0$, and $A_t=J_n+tB$.
There are numbers $q_k\ge0$ such that
\[
 \mathcal Q(A_t)=c_n+\sum_{k=2}^nq_kt^{2k}.
\]
Moreover,
\[
 q_2=\frac{(n-2)!}{n^{n-2}}
 \frac{\operatorname{tr}[(B^\T B+BB^\T)^2]}8.
\]
If $B\ne0$, then $q_2>0$ and $\mathcal Q(A_t)$ is strictly increasing for $t>0$.
The identity does not require nonnegative entries in $A_t$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Use orthonormal coordinates $z=z_0\mathbf1/\sqrt n+y$ with $y\perp\mathbf1$.
Each row form of $A_t$ is $z_0/\sqrt n+t(By)_i$.
Set
\[
 r_k(y)=\frac{e_k(By)+e_k(B^\T y)}2.
\]
Then $r_0=1$, $r_1=0$, and
\[
 r_{A_t}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^n
 t^k\frac{z_0^{n-k}}{n^{(n-k)/2}}r_k(y).
\]
The factorial inner product is invariant under orthogonal coordinate changes.
One direct justification is its Gaussian representation
$\|p\|_F^2=\mathbb E|p(Z)|^2$ for independent standard circular complex Gaussian coordinates $Z$.
The moment calculation in Lemma~\ref{lem:struct-psd-trace} proves this representation, and orthogonal changes preserve the Gaussian law.
Different powers of $z_0$ are orthogonal.
Therefore
\[
 q_k=\frac{(n-k)!}{n^{n-k}}\|r_k\|_F^2\ge0.
\]
With $M=B^\T B+BB^\T$, the zero-sum identities give
$r_2(y)=-y^\T M y/4$.
For a real symmetric matrix, direct coefficient expansion gives
$\|y^\T M y\|_F^2=2\operatorname{tr}(M^2)$.
The matrix $M$ annihilates $\mathbf1$, so its trace on the contrast space is its full trace.
This proves the formula for $q_2$.
Finally, $M$ is positive semidefinite and is zero exactly when $B=0$.
\end{proof}

The corresponding ratio need not increase.
This limitation already occurs for a doubly stochastic circulant of order three.
Let $P$ be a three-cycle, let $A=(I+P)/2$, and set $A_t=J_3+t(A-J_3)$ for $0\le t\le1$.
Its circulant weights are $(u,u,v)$, where $u=1/3+t/6$ and $v=1/3-t/3$.
Transposition permutes the row forms, so $\mathcal Q(A_t)=\per(A_tA_t^\T)$.
Direct permanent expansion gives
\[
 p(t)=\per(A_t)=\frac29+\frac{t^2}{12}-\frac{t^3}{18},
 \qquad
 q(t)=\mathcal Q(A_t)=\frac29+\frac{t^4}{48}+\frac{t^6}{144}.
\]
Thus $p(1)=q(1)=1/4$, $p'(1)=0$, and $q'(1)=1/8$.
It follows that
\[
 \left.\frac{d}{dt}\frac{p(t)^2}{q(t)}\right|_{t=1}=-\frac18<0.
\]
The endpoint ratio is $1/4>c_3=2/9$.
The example therefore refutes radial monotonicity but does not refute Gyires's inequality.

\subsection{A direct reduction to the matrix boundary}

The boundary of $\Omega_n$ consists of its matrices with at least one zero entry.
For a row, its support is the set of columns in which its entries are positive.

\begin{theorem}[Boundary reduction]\label{thm:struct-boundary}
Fix $n\ge2$.
The first strong bound~\eqref{eq:strong-pair} holds on all of $\Omega_n$ if and only if it holds on the boundary.
If it holds on the boundary, it is strict at every strictly positive nonuniform matrix.
More generally, every global maximizer on $\Omega_n$ of
\[
 F(A)=\sqrt{c_n}\,\|p_A\|_F-\per(A)
\]
has identical rows within each row-support class.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The function $F$ is continuous on the compact set $\Omega_n$, so it has a global maximizer.
Suppose a matrix has two distinct rows $r,s$ with the same support.
Put $\mu=(r+s)/2$, $d=(r-s)/2$, and
\[
 T=\min_{j:d_j\ne0}\frac{\mu_j}{|d_j|}.
\]
The minimum is over a nonempty finite set.
On the common support, both entries are positive, so $\mu_j>|d_j|$ when $d_j\ne0$.
Hence $T>1$.
Replace the two rows by $\mu+td$ and $\mu-td$ for $0\le t\le T$.
The resulting matrix $A(t)$ remains doubly stochastic.
Its row sums stay one, its column sums do not change, and the definition of $T$ gives nonnegative entries.
The original matrix is $A(1)$, and $A(T)$ has a zero entry.
If $R(z)$ is the product of the unchanged row forms, then
\[
 p_{A(t)}=\bigl[(\mu\cdot z)^2-t^2(d\cdot z)^2\bigr]R(z).
\]
This polynomial is affine in $t^2$.
The two endpoint polynomials are distinct because $d\ne0$ and $R$ is nonzero.
Both have value one at $\mathbf1$, so they cannot be positive scalar multiples.
Strict convexity of a Hilbert norm on the segment between such vectors gives
\[
 F(A)<(1-T^{-2})F(A(0))+T^{-2}F(A(T)).
\]
The subtracted permanent is a linear coefficient functional, so it does not affect this strict inequality.
A global maximizer cannot have such a row pair.
This proves the support-class assertion.

If a global maximizer is strictly positive, every row has the same support.
All rows must then agree, and the column sums force that matrix to be $J_n$.
Since $F(J_n)=0$, it follows that
\[
 \max_{A\in\Omega_n}F(A)
 =\max\left\{0,\max_{A\in\partial\Omega_n}F(A)\right\}.
\]
The first strong inequality is equivalent to $F(A)\le0$ because both terms before squaring are nonnegative.
This proves the boundary equivalence.
If boundary validity holds, the entire set has $F\le0$.
Every strictly positive nonuniform matrix has two distinct rows with common support.
The strict interpolation estimate therefore gives $F(A)<0$ there.
\end{proof}

The boundary has the same order as the original matrix.
The theorem is not a reduction to permutation matrices and is not an induction on the dimension.
No boundary-validity assertion for arbitrary order is assumed in the preceding matrix-family proofs.


\section{Scalar and two-group polynomial
inequalities}\label{sec:scalar}

Products of nonnegative linear forms connect the matrix inequalities to coefficient norm bounds.
The scalar inequalities in this section also apply to a larger class of polynomials.
The scalar bounds precede their polynomial interpretations.

For a homogeneous polynomial \(p\) of degree \(n\) in \(n\) variables,
write \(N(p)=\langle p,p\rangle_F\) for the squared Fischer norm
from Section \ref{sec:general}. Call \(p\) normalized when
\(p(\mathbf1)=1\). It is balanced when \(\partial_i p(\mathbf1)=1\) for
every variable. Its nonnegative coefficients then define a probability
law on exponent vectors, with mean one in each coordinate.

A nonnegative sequence has interval support if its positive terms occupy
consecutive indices. It is log-concave if \(u_k^2\ge u_{k-1}u_{k+1}\) at
every interior index. A likelihood ratio is the sequence \(P_k/B_k\) for
probability laws \(P,B\) with every \(B_k>0\). The scalar assumptions
below concern this ratio, not the reference law itself.

The term Lorentzian is used only with its stated coefficient criterion
in this section. For a homogeneous binary polynomial
\(\sum_{k=0}^n a_kX^kY^{n-k}\) with nonnegative coefficients, that
criterion is interval support and log-concavity of \(a_k/\binom nk\).
Replacement of \(X,Y\) by sums of disjoint groups of variables gives the
corresponding two-group class. These are standard Lorentzian closure and
coefficient properties \cite{brandenhuh2020}. Each theorem states its coefficient assumptions explicitly.

\subsection{Localization of likelihood
ratios}\label{scalar-localization-of-likelihood-ratios}

Let \(B=(B_0,\ldots,B_n)\) be a strictly positive probability law, and
let \(m\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}\). Let \(\mathcal U\) contain the nonnegative
log-concave sequences with interval support that satisfy \[
 u_m=1,\qquad \sum_{k=0}^n(k-m)B_ku_k=0.
\] The following localization statement will be used three times. Every
continuous strictly convex function of the coordinates \(u\) attains its
maximum on \(\mathcal U\). Every maximizer is either the singleton
\(u_m=1\), with all other entries zero, or has the form \[
 u_k=t^{k-m}\mathbf1_{\{a\le k\le b\}},\qquad a<m<b,
\] where \(t>0\) is the unique value that satisfies the mean equation.
Translation of the indices gives the same statement for any finite
integer interval and an interior integer mean.

To prove compactness, put \(Z=\sum B_ku_k\) and \(P_k=B_ku_k/Z\). This
law has mean \(m\). Concavity of the piecewise-linear interpolation of
\(\log u\) on its positive support gives \[
 \sum_k P_k\log u_k\le\log u_m=0.
\] The left side is \(D(P\Vert B)+\log Z\), where
\(D(P\Vert B)=\sum_{P_k>0}P_k\log(P_k/B_k)\) is relative entropy.
Jensen's inequality gives \(D(P\Vert B)\ge0\). Hence \(Z\le1\) and
\(0\le u_k\le B_k^{-1}\). The feasible set is closed. Indeed, its
multi-step log-concavity inequalities \[
 u_j^{k-i}\ge u_i^{k-j}u_k^{j-i}\qquad(i<j<k)
\] are continuous polynomial inequalities. They exclude an internal zero
between positive limiting endpoints. Thus \(\mathcal U\) is compact.

If a support endpoint equals \(m\), balance forces the singleton case.
Otherwise write the support as \([a,b]\), with \(a<m<b\). At each active
log-concavity equality, impose \[
 2h_k=h_{k-1}+h_{k+1}.
\] If any interior inequality is strict, these independent
second-difference equations leave a solution space of dimension at least
three. The two further equations \[
 h_m=0,\qquad\sum_{k=a}^b(k-m)B_ku_kh_k=0
\] therefore leave a nonzero solution. For small \(\varepsilon>0\), put
\(u_k^\pm=u_k(1\pm\varepsilon h_k)\) on the same support. Both
constraints hold exactly. At an active equality, the new normalized gap
is \[
 (1\pm\varepsilon h_k)^2-(1\pm\varepsilon h_{k-1})(1\pm\varepsilon h_{k+1})
 =\varepsilon^2(h_k-h_{k-1})^2\ge0.
\] Strict inequalities persist for small \(\varepsilon\). Thus \(u^+\)
and \(u^-\) are feasible and have average \(u\). Strict convexity makes
the average of their objective values larger than its value at \(u\).
This contradicts maximality. Every interior log-concavity inequality is
therefore equality, which gives the geometric form. The mean of a
positive exponential tilt is strictly increasing in \(\log t\), because
its derivative is the positive variance. Its limits are \(a\) and \(b\).
This proves the asserted existence and uniqueness of \(t\).

\subsection{The mean-one binomial
theorem}\label{scalar-the-mean-one-binomial-theorem}

Let \(n\ge2\), let \(B\) be the probability mass function of
\(\operatorname{Bin}(n,1/n)\), and let \(P\) be a probability mass
function on \(\{0,\ldots,n\}\) with mean one. Suppose

\[
r_k=\frac{P_k}{B_k}
\]

is log-concave, with interval support. Then

\begin{equation}\label{eq:m-1}
\boxed{\ \sum_{k=0}^nB_kr_k^2\le r_1^2.\ }
\end{equation}

Equality holds exactly when \(P=B\).

The interval-support and mean assumptions imply \(P_1>0\). Set
\(u_k=(P_k/B_k)/(P_1/B_1)\). Then \(u_1=1\), and the mean equation is \begin{equation}\label{eq:m-4}
\sum_{k=0}^n(k-1)B_ku_k=0.
\end{equation} The assertion is \(\Phi(u)=\sum B_ku_k^2\le1\). The preceding
localization applies to this strictly convex objective. A singleton has
objective \(B_1<1\). Every other candidate has support
\(\{0,\ldots,L\}\) with \(L\ge2\) and \begin{equation}\label{eq:m-6}
u_k=t^{k-1}\ (0\le k\le L),\qquad u_k=0\ (k>L).
\end{equation} Put \(F_L(z)=\sum_{k=0}^LB_kz^k\). The balancing root satisfies \begin{equation}\label{eq:m-7}
tF_L'(t)=F_L(t),\quad\text{equivalently}\quad
 \sum_{k=2}^L(k-1)B_kt^k=B_0.
\end{equation} The left side is strictly increasing for \(t>0\). Its root is
\(t_n=1\) for \(L=n\) and \(t_L>1\) for a proper truncation. It remains
to prove \begin{equation}\label{eq:m-8}
\Phi(u)=F_L(t_L^2)/t_L^2\le1.
\end{equation} \subsubsection{The balancing tilt}

Put \(c_k=B_k/B_0\). For \(k\ge1\),

\begin{equation}\label{eq:m-9}
c_k=\frac{\binom nk}{(n-1)^k}
\le\frac{n}{n-1}\frac1{k!}.
\end{equation}

Indeed the first factor in \((n)_k/(n-1)^k\) is \(n/(n-1)\), the second
is one, and all subsequent factors are at most one.

Let

\[
G_L(t)=\sum_{k=2}^L(k-1)c_kt^k.
\]

Then \(G_L(t_L)=1\), and

\[
1-G_L(1)=\sum_{k>L}(k-1)c_k
\le\frac{n}{n-1}\sum_{k>L}\frac{k-1}{k!}
=\frac{n}{n-1}\frac1{L!}.
\]

For \(t\ge1\),

\[
G_L'(t)\ge2c_2=\frac{n}{n-1}.
\]

Consequently,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:m-10}
\boxed{\ 0\le t_L-1\le\frac1{L!}.\ }
\end{equation}

This bound controls the change caused by truncating the binomial law.

\subsubsection{Comparison with the omitted binomial
tail}\label{scalar-comparison-with-the-omitted-binomial-tail}

Fix \(L<n\) and abbreviate \(t=t_L>1\). Let

\[
M=\sum_{k>L}B_k,\qquad
T=\sum_{k>L}(k-1)B_k.
\]

Subtracting the mean equation at \(t=1\) from \eqref{eq:m-7} gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:m-11}
T=\sum_{k=2}^L(k-1)B_k(t^k-1).
\end{equation}

Put \(f(z)=F_L(z)/z\). Eliminating \(B_0\) by \eqref{eq:m-7} gives the exact
identity

\begin{equation}\label{eq:m-12}
f(t^2)-f(1)=\sum_{k=3}^LB_k\psi_k(t),
\end{equation}

where

\[
\psi_k(t)=t^{2k-2}-1-(k-1)t^{k-2}(t^2-1).
\]

The \(k=2\) contribution vanishes exactly.

If \(L=2\), \eqref{eq:m-12} is zero, and \(f(t^2)=f(1)=1-M<1\), which proves
\eqref{eq:m-8} in that case.

Now assume \(L\ge3\). Write \(s=\log t\) and \(r=k-1\). Then

\[
\psi_k(t)=2e^{rs}\bigl(\sinh(rs)-r\sinh s\bigr).
\]

Taylor's integral remainder and \(\sinh s\ge s\) give

\[
\sinh(rs)-r\sinh s
\le \sinh(rs)-rs
\le\frac{r^3s^3}{6}\cosh(rs).
\]

Also \(e^{(r+1)s}-1\ge(r+1)s\). Hence

\[
\frac{\psi_k(t)}{(k-1)(t^k-1)}
\le\frac{r^2}{3(r+1)}e^{2rs}s^2
\le\frac r3e^{2rs}s^2.
\]

By \eqref{eq:m-10}, \(s\le1/L!\) and \(r\le L-1\), so \(2rs\le2/3\) and
\(e^{2rs}<2\). This gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:m-13}
\frac{\psi_k(t)}{(k-1)(t^k-1)}
<
C_L:=\frac{2(L-1)}{3(L!)^2}
<
\frac1{L+1}.
\end{equation}

The final inequality follows already from \(L!\ge L\).

The binomial tail satisfies a complementary estimate. For \(k\ge L+1\),

\[
\frac{B_{k+1}}{B_k}
=\frac{n-k}{(n-1)(k+1)}
\le\frac1{L+2}.
\]

Conditioned on \(K\ge L+1\), the excess \(K-(L+1)\) is stochastically
bounded by a geometric distribution with successive probability ratio
\(\rho=1/(L+2)\). More explicitly, write \(b_i=B_{L+1+i}\), with zero
values past the end of the support. The coefficient-ratio bound gives
\(b_{i+r}\le\rho^r b_i\) term by term, and hence

\[
\mathbb P(K-(L+1)\ge r\mid K>L)
=\frac{\sum_{i\ge0}b_{i+r}}{\sum_{i\ge0}b_i}
\le\rho^r.
\]

Summing these tail probabilities gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:m-14}
\frac TM
=\mathbb E[K-1\mid K>L]
\le L+\frac1{L+1}
<L+1.
\end{equation}

Combining \eqref{eq:m-11}--\eqref{eq:m-14},

\[
f(t^2)-f(1)
<\frac{T}{L+1}
<M.
\]

Since \(f(1)=F_L(1)=1-M\), this proves \(f(t^2)<1\). Together with the
\(L=2\) case, every proper truncation has strictly smaller objective
than one.

For \(L=n\), the unique root is \(t=1\) and the objective is exactly
one. The degenerate support \(\{1\}\) also has objective \(B_1<1\). Thus
the maximum is one.

\subsubsection{Equality}\label{scalar-equality}

If a feasible nongeometric sequence had objective one, the
strict-convexity perturbation in the localization argument would produce
a feasible sequence with objective greater than one, which is
impossible. Every proper geometric truncation has objective strictly
less than one by the tail estimate. The only remaining equality case is
\(L=n\), \(t=1\), and \(u_k=1\) for every \(k\).

Normalization then forces \(r_k=1\) and \(P=B\). This proves the
equality characterization in the theorem.

The proof applies to Lorentzian inputs, not only to real-stable inputs.
Properly truncated geometric likelihood ratios need not give real-rooted binary polynomials.
Restriction to Poisson-binomial distributions would therefore exclude some extremal candidates.

\subsubsection{Polynomial consequence}\label{scalar-polynomial-consequence}

Consider a homogeneous polynomial

\begin{equation}\label{eq:m-2}
p(x)=\sum_{k=0}^na_kx_1^k
       (x_2+\cdots+x_n)^{n-k}.
\end{equation}

Write \(m=n-1\). Normalization \(p(\mathbf1)=1\) gives the marginal
probabilities

\[
P_k=a_km^{n-k}.
\]

Balance is equivalent to \(\mathbb E_PK=1\): the other \(m\) coordinates
are symmetric, and homogeneity then makes each of their mean exponents
one as well.

The explicit coefficient condition for \eqref{eq:m-2} is log-concavity, with
interval support, of \(a_k/\binom nk\). Equivalently, \(r_k=P_k/B_k\) is
log-concave. This is the binary Lorentzian criterion applied to \(x_1\)
and the sum of the other variables, as defined above.

The central coefficient and squared Fischer norm satisfy

\begin{equation}\label{eq:m-3}
\frac{[x_1\cdots x_n]p}{c_n}=r_1,
\qquad
\frac{N(p)}{c_n}=\sum_kB_kr_k^2.
\end{equation}

For the second identity, the contribution with exponent \(k\) on \(x_1\)
is

\[
k!(n-k)!\,a_k^2m^{n-k}.
\]

Thus \eqref{eq:m-1} proves

\[
\bigl([x_1\cdots x_n]p\bigr)^2\ge c_nN(p)
\]

for this entire all-order Lorentzian family. Equality is the uniform
power \(p=((x_1+\cdots+x_n)/n)^n\).


\subsection{Symmetric reference laws}\label{scalar-symmetric-reference-laws}

\subsubsection{Statement}\label{scalar-statement}

Let \(N\ge1\) and let \(B\) be a strictly positive probability mass
function on \(\{-N,\ldots,N\}\) such that \(B_{-j}=B_j\). Let \(P\) be a
mean-zero probability mass function on the same set such that
\(r_j=P_j/B_j\) is log-concave with interval support. Then

\begin{equation}\label{eq:s-7}
\boxed{\quad\sum_{j=-N}^N B_jr_j^2\le r_0^2.\quad}
\end{equation}

Equality holds exactly when \(P=B\).

Notice that \(B\) itself need not be log-concave. The only shape
assumption is log-concavity of the likelihood ratio \(P/B\).

\subsubsection{Proof}\label{scalar-proof}

Set \(u=r/r_0\), which is legitimate because interval support and mean
zero imply \(P_0>0\). By the localization lemma, a maximizer is either
supported at zero or has the form

\[
u_j=e^{sj}\mathbf1_{\{-a\le j\le b\}}
\]

for integers \(a,b\ge1\). A singleton has objective \(B_0<1\).

If \(s=0\), balance and strict positivity of the symmetric reference
force \(a=b\). The objective is the reference probability of this
interval, at most one, with equality only for the full interval.

Suppose \(s>0\). If \(a\le b\), every symmetric pair in the interval
contributes a strictly positive amount to the mean, as do any unpaired
positive terms. Therefore balance forces \(a>b\). The mean equation
becomes

\begin{equation}\label{eq:s-8}
\sum_{j=1}^b 2jB_j\sinh(sj)
=\sum_{j=b+1}^a jB_j e^{-sj}.
\end{equation}

Subtract the reference mass of the interval from the squared-norm
objective. Pairing \(j\) and \(-j\) gives the exact identity

\begin{equation}\label{eq:s-9}
\Phi_B(u)-B([-a,b])
=\sum_{j=1}^b4B_j\sinh^2(sj)
-\sum_{j=b+1}^aB_j(1-e^{-2sj}).
\end{equation}

The function \(x\mapsto\sinh(sx)/x\) is strictly increasing on \(x>0\):
its derivative has numerator \(sx\cosh(sx)-\sinh(sx)\), whose derivative
in \(sx\) is positive. Thus

\begin{equation}\label{eq:s-10}
\begin{aligned}
\sum_{j=1}^b4B_j\sinh^2(sj)
&\le\frac{2\sinh(sb)}b
\sum_{j=1}^b2jB_j\sinh(sj)\\
&=\frac{2\sinh(sb)}b
\sum_{j=b+1}^a jB_j e^{-sj}\\
&<\sum_{j=b+1}^a2B_j\sinh(sj)e^{-sj}\\
&=\sum_{j=b+1}^aB_j(1-e^{-2sj}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

The strict inequality follows from \(j>b\) and the presence of at least
one unpaired term. Equations \eqref{eq:s-9} and \eqref{eq:s-10} imply

\[
\Phi_B(u)<B([-a,b])\le1.
\]

For \(s<0\), reflect the indices and apply the same argument.
Consequently, the only geometric candidate with objective one is
\(u\equiv1\) on the full interval. A nongeometric equality case would
admit the strictly improving two-sided perturbation from the
localization lemma, contradicting the bound. Therefore equality forces
\(u\equiv1\), and probability normalization gives \(r\equiv1\), or
\(P=B\). This proves \eqref{eq:s-7}.

\subsubsection{Central binomial
consequence}\label{scalar-central-binomial-consequence}

Take \(n=2m\) and \(B=\operatorname{Bin}(2m,1/2)\). It is symmetric
about its integer mean \(m\). Thus every mean-\(m\) probability mass
function \(P\) with log-concave likelihood ratio \(r=P/B\) satisfies

\[
\sum_{k=0}^{2m}B_kr_k^2\le r_m^2,
\]

with equality only for \(P=B\). This proves the arbitrary-order
central-mean case, in addition to the mean-one case above and its
reflection at mean \(n-1\).

\subsubsection{Exact examples}\label{scalar-exact-examples}

In these examples, the scalar ratio means \(r_m^2/\sum_k B_kr_k^2\), where \(m\) is the mean.

For \(B=\operatorname{Bin}(4,1/2)\), consider support \(\{1,2,3\}\).
Symmetry gives \(t=1\) and

\[
\Phi_B(u)=B_1+B_2+B_3=\frac78.
\]

After probability normalization, the likelihood ratio is constant
\(8/7\) on this interval; the scalar ratio is \(8/7>1\).

For a reference law which is not log-concave, take

\[
(B_{-2},B_{-1},B_0,B_1,B_2)
=\frac1{20}(6,1,6,1,6).
\]

It is symmetric and strictly positive, but \(B_{-1}^2<B_{-2}B_0\). The
theorem still applies to every mean-zero \(P\) with log-concave ratio
\(P/B\). For example, restriction to \(\{-1,0,1\}\) has \(t=1\),
objective \(2/5\), and scalar ratio \(5/2\).

\subsubsection{Two-group polynomial
consequence}\label{scalar-two-group-polynomial-consequence}

Let \(m+\ell=n\), and set

\[
X=x_1+\cdots+x_m,\qquad
Y=x_{m+1}+\cdots+x_n,\qquad
p(x)=\sum_{k=0}^n a_kX^kY^{n-k}.
\]

For a normalized polynomial, define

\[
P_k=a_km^k\ell^{n-k},\qquad
B_k=\binom nk\left(\frac mn\right)^k
\left(\frac\ell n\right)^{n-k}.
\]

Balance of all \(n\) variables is equivalent to \(\mathbb E_PK=m\). The
Lorentzian condition is log-concavity with interval support of
\(a_k/\binom nk\), equivalently of \(r=P/B\). The identities are

\begin{equation}\label{eq:s-11}
\frac{[x_1\cdots x_n]p}{c_n}=r_m,
\qquad
\frac{N(p)}{c_n}=\sum_{k=0}^nB_kr_k^2.
\end{equation}

Indeed the central coefficient is \(a_m m!\ell!\), and the contribution
to the squared Fischer norm from total degree \(k\) in the first group is

\[
k!(n-k)!a_k^2m^k\ell^{n-k}.
\]

Consequently the symmetric-reference theorem proves the target
Fischer-norm inequality for every balanced Lorentzian polynomial with
two groups of the same size. It does not require a product of linear
forms.

For unequal group sizes, \eqref{eq:s-11} is still exact, but the corresponding
integer-mean scalar inequality remains unproved.
The proof for symmetric reference laws cannot be applied directly to an
asymmetric binomial law by an exponential tilt, because that changes the
mean about which the balance condition is imposed.

\subsection{Duplication and the Gram
permanent}\label{scalar-duplication-and-the-gram-permanent}

There is a second consequence of the mean-one localization argument,
beyond the norm inequality itself.

\subsubsection{Scalar bridge theorem}\label{scalar-scalar-bridge-theorem}

Let \(n\ge2\), let \(B=\operatorname{Bin}(n,1/n)\), and let \(P\) be a
mean-one probability law whose ratio \(P/B\) is log-concave with
interval support. Write

\[
q=\frac{n-1}{n},\qquad g=B_1=q^{n-1}.
\]

Then

\begin{equation}\label{eq:d-17}
\boxed{\quad
P_1^2+2P_0P_2
\ge (1+q)g^2\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{P_k^2}{B_k}.
\quad}
\end{equation}

Equality holds exactly for \(P=B\).

\subsubsection{Proof}\label{scalar-proof-1}

Set \(u_k=(P_k/B_k)/(P_1/B_1)\) as in the mean-one proof. Since
\(B_0=qg\) and \(B_2=g/2\), \eqref{eq:d-17} is equivalent to

\begin{equation}\label{eq:d-18}
\Psi(u):=(1+q)\sum_kB_ku_k^2-q u_0u_2\le1.
\end{equation}

The same compact feasible set and two-sided multiplicative perturbations
from the localization argument apply. It remains to check strict convexity
of the new quadratic objective. All coordinates except 0 and 2 are
diagonal positive terms. The remaining \(2\times2\) block is positive
definite exactly when

\begin{equation}\label{eq:d-19}
(1+q)^2B_0B_2>\frac{q^2}{4},
\quad\text{or equivalently}\quad
2(1+q)^2g^2>q.
\end{equation}

The elementary bound \((1-1/n)^{n-1}>e^{-1}\) follows from
\(\log(1-x)>-x/(1-x)\) for \(0<x<1\). Also \((1+q)^2/q\ge4\). Therefore

\[
\frac{2(1+q)^2g^2}{q}>\frac8{e^2}>1,
\]

which proves \eqref{eq:d-19}. Thus \(\Psi\) is strictly convex.

The localization proof now forces a maximizer to be a geometric
likelihood ratio on an initial interval \(\{0,\ldots,L\}\), \(L\ge2\),
or the singleton at 1. In the geometric case, \(u_0u_2=1\), so

\[
\Psi(u)=(1+q)\Phi_B(u)-q\le1
\]

by the already proved mean-one theorem. Its strictness on every proper
geometric truncation gives strictness here as well.

For the singleton, \(\Psi(u)=(1+q)g<1\). Indeed Bernoulli's inequality
gives

\[
g^{-1}=\left(1+\frac1{n-1}\right)^{n-1}\ge2,
\]

while \(q<1\). Strict convexity and the same perturbation argument rule
out any further equality case. Hence equality forces \(P=B\).

\subsubsection{Polynomial form}\label{scalar-polynomial-form}

For a balanced normalized Lorentzian polynomial of the form

\[
p(x)=\sum_{k=0}^n a_k x_1^k(x_2+\cdots+x_n)^{n-k},
\]

\eqref{eq:d-17} is exactly the coefficient inequality

\begin{equation}\label{eq:d-20}
\boxed{\quad
2^n[x_1^2\cdots x_n^2]p(x)^2
\ge\frac{(2n)!}{n!\,n^n}\,N(p).
\quad}
\end{equation}

To verify the constants directly, put \(m=n-1\) and \(P_k=a_km^{n-k}\).
The left side of \eqref{eq:d-20} is

\[
2(2n-2)!(a_1^2+2a_0a_2),
\]

while \(N(p)=c_n\sum_kP_k^2/B_k\). Substitution gives exactly \eqref{eq:d-17}.
Equality in \eqref{eq:d-20} is the uniform power \(((x_1+\cdots+x_n)/n)^n\).

For a matrix product polynomial, the left side of \eqref{eq:d-20} is
\(\operatorname{per}(A\otimes E_2)\), where \(E_2\) is the all-ones
\(2\times2\) matrix. Thus \eqref{eq:d-20} compares the permanent of the duplicated matrix with its Gram permanent for this polynomial family. No extension to arbitrary
two-group or multivariate Lorentzian polynomials is asserted here.

\subsection{The remaining general integer-mean reduction}

The localization statement also gives an exact reduction for any interior integer mean $m$ and $B=\operatorname{Bin}(n,m/n)$.
For a law $P$ of mean $m$ with log-concave ratio $r=P/B$ and interval support, the proposed inequality is
\[
 \sum_k B_kr_k^2\le r_m^2.
\]
Set $u=r/r_m$. The inequality holds for every such law if and only if
\[
 \sum_{k=a}^bB_kt^{2(k-m)}\le1
\]
for every interval $a<m<b$, at its unique balancing root
\[
 \sum_{k=a}^b(k-m)B_kt^{k-m}=0.
\]
The singleton candidate has value $B_m<1$. The equivalence follows because localization identifies every maximizer, and each geometric candidate is feasible. The mean-one theorem, its reflection, and the symmetric-reference theorem prove the cases $m=1$, $m=n-1$, and $n=2m$. The other cases for arbitrary log-concave likelihood ratios remain unproved here. The Poisson-binomial theorem in Section~\ref{sec:four-block} has stronger conclusions on its more restricted class.


\section{A two-law scalar reduction in arbitrary order}\label{sec:r2-scalar-reduction}

The rank-two representation also gives an exact scalar form of the strong permanent inequality.
A further reduction permits a larger class than Poisson-binomial laws.
It reduces that scalar assertion to pairs of geometric likelihood ratios on intervals.
The resulting scalar inequality remains unproved.
It does not extend the finite-order theorem to every rank-two matrix.

\subsection{The permanent and a binomial-noise operator}

Fix $n\ge2$ and $0<t<n$.
Use the representation~\eqref{eq:r2-representation}, with $\theta=t/n$.
Let $P_x,P_y$ be the corresponding Poisson-binomial laws, and put
\[
 B(k)=\binom nk\theta^k(1-\theta)^{n-k},\qquad
 r(k)=\frac{P_x(k)}{B(k)},\qquad s(k)=\frac{P_y(k)}{B(k)}.
\]
The reference law $B$ is positive at every index.
For functions on $\{0,\ldots,n\}$, write
$\langle f,g\rangle_B=\sum_k B(k)f(k)g(k)$.

Expand each entry of~\eqref{eq:r2-representation} into its two summands.
If $k$ rows use the first summand, the column assignment has $k!(n-k)!$ possible matchings for each selected column set.
Summing over row and column sets gives
\[
 \per(A)=\sum_{k=0}^n
 \frac{k!(n-k)!}{t^k(n-t)^{n-k}}P_x(k)P_y(k).
\]
The scalar coefficient is $c_n/B(k)$.
Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-law-permanent}
 \frac{\per(A)}{c_n}=\langle r,s\rangle_B.
\end{equation}

For $0\le\kappa\le1$, define a Markov chain on $n$ Bernoulli bits as follows.
Each bit is retained with probability $\kappa$.
Otherwise, replace it by an independent Bernoulli variable with parameter $\theta$.
Apply these operations independently across coordinates.
The induced transition on the number of successes depends only on the initial number of successes.
Write $T_\kappa(k,\ell)$ for this transition probability, and use $T_\kappa$ also for its operator on functions.
The bit transition is reversible for $\operatorname{Bern}(\theta)$.
Thus the count transition is reversible for $B$:
\[
 B(k)T_\kappa(k,\ell)=B(\ell)T_\kappa(\ell,k).
\]
If the initial bit parameters are $x_i$, their new parameters are
$x'_i=\theta+\kappa(x_i-\theta)$.
Reversibility shows that their new count likelihood ratio is $T_\kappa r$.

For the law $P_y$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-law-kappa}
 \kappa(s)=1-\frac{\operatorname{Var}(P_y)}{t(1-t/n)}
 =\frac{n\sum_j y_j^2-t^2}{t(n-t)}.
\end{equation}
This is the same $\kappa_y\in[0,1]$ as in~\eqref{eq:r2-gram-expansion}.
The Gram matrix is the matrix in~\eqref{eq:r2-representation} made from $x$ and
$x'=\theta\mathbf1+\kappa(s)(x-\theta\mathbf1)$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:r2-law-permanent} therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-law-gram}
 \frac{\per(AA^\T)}{c_n}=\langle r,T_{\kappa(s)}r\rangle_B.
\end{equation}
Consequently the strong rank-two inequality is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-two-law-target}
 \langle r,s\rangle_B^2\ge\langle r,T_{\kappa(s)}r\rangle_B
\end{equation}
for Poisson-binomial laws of equal mean.
Both identities hold in every order; they do not assert the inequality in every order.

\subsection{The larger class and its variance bound}

A law $P$ on $\{0,\ldots,n\}$ is \emph{ultra-log-concave of order $n$} if its support is an interval and $P(k)/\binom nk$ is log-concave there.
At fixed $\theta$, this is equivalent to log-concavity of $P/B$.
The two normalizations differ by a positive geometric sequence.
Newton's coefficient inequalities for a product of nonnegative binary linear forms show that Poisson-binomial laws belong to this class.

Let $\mathcal U_{n,t}$ be the ultra-log-concave laws with mean $t$.
This is a compact set of probability vectors.
For example, put $u_k=P(k)/B(k)$.
The inequalities
\[
 u_j^{k-i}\ge u_i^{k-j}u_k^{j-i}\qquad(i<j<k)
\]
are closed conditions.
They express log-concavity and exclude internal zeros between two positive coefficients.
The fixed-mass and fixed-mean conditions are also closed.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:r2-ulc-variance}
For $P\in\mathcal U_{n,t}$ and every convex function $\varphi$ on the index interval,
\[
 \mathbb E_P\varphi(K)\le\mathbb E_B\varphi(K).
\]
In particular, $0\le\operatorname{Var}(P)\le t(1-t/n)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The positive indices of $P-B$ are the indices where $P/B>1$.
Log-concavity makes them an interval.
Thus $P-B$ has the sign pattern negative, positive, negative, with zeros permitted.
If there are no positive entries, the equal total masses imply $P=B$.
Otherwise let $a,b$ be the first and last positive indices.
For $a<b$, take the secant line $L$ through $(a,\varphi(a))$ and $(b,\varphi(b))$.
Convexity gives $\varphi-L\le0$ between those indices and $\varphi-L\ge0$ outside them.
Hence $(P-B)(\varphi-L)\le0$ at every index.
For $a=b$, use a supporting line to the convex function at $a$ instead.
The same product inequality holds.
The sum of $(P-B)L$ is zero because $P$ and $B$ have equal mass and mean.
Summing proves the expectation comparison.
Take $\varphi(k)=(k-t)^2$ for the variance bound.
\end{proof}

For arbitrary $P,Q\in\mathcal U_{n,t}$, set $r=P/B$, $s=Q/B$, and
\[
 \kappa(s)=1-\frac{\operatorname{Var}(Q)}{t(1-t/n)}.
\]
The lemma makes this a parameter in $[0,1]$.
It is affine in $Q$ on the fixed-mean set.
The proposed extension is~\eqref{eq:r2-two-law-target} for all such $P,Q$.
This extension is unproved.

\subsection{Separate convexity of the square-root deficit}

Define
\[
 \mathcal D(r,s)=\sqrt{\langle r,T_{\kappa(s)}r\rangle_B}
                 -\langle r,s\rangle_B.
\]
Since the inner product of the nonnegative functions $r,s$ is nonnegative,
the target~\eqref{eq:r2-two-law-target} is equivalent to $\mathcal D(r,s)\le0$.

To obtain the spectrum of $T_\kappa$, take independent bits $X_i$ with parameter $\theta$ and put
$\xi_i=(X_i-\theta)/\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)}$.
The functions
\[
 K_k=\binom nk^{-1/2}\sum_{|S|=k}\prod_{i\in S}\xi_i,
 \qquad 0\le k\le n,
\]
depend only on the number of successes.
They are the normalized Krawtchouk polynomials.
Independence makes products from distinct subsets orthogonal, with squared norm one.
Thus $K_0,\ldots,K_n$ form an orthonormal basis for the count space with weight $B$.
The conditional mean of an updated centered bit is $\kappa\xi_i$.
Independence of the updates therefore gives
\[
 T_\kappa K_k=\kappa^kK_k.
\]
All eigenvalues are nonnegative, so $T_\kappa$ is positive semidefinite.
For fixed $s$, the first term of $\mathcal D$ is a seminorm of $r$.
Subtracting a linear functional preserves convexity.

Mass one and mean $t$ imply
$r=1+\sum_{k=2}^n a_kK_k$.
The coefficient of $K_1=(K-t)/\sqrt{t(1-t/n)}$ vanishes.
Therefore
\begin{align*}
 \sqrt{\langle r,T_\kappa r\rangle_B}
 &=\sqrt{1+\sum_{k=2}^n a_k^2\kappa^k}\\
 &=\bigl\|(1,|a_2|\kappa,|a_3|\kappa^{3/2},\ldots,|a_n|\kappa^{n/2})\bigr\|_2.
\end{align*}
Every coordinate is nonnegative and convex in $\kappa\ge0$.
The Euclidean norm is convex and increases in each coordinate on the nonnegative orthant.
The displayed function is therefore convex in $\kappa$.
Since $\kappa(s)$ is affine, $\mathcal D$ is convex in $s$ for fixed $r$.
These arguments apply on the convex hull of $\mathcal U_{n,t}$, where the variance bound remains valid by affinity.
They prove separate convexity, without a claim of joint convexity.

\subsection{Localization to two support intervals}

\begin{lemma}[Fixed-mean ultra-log-concave localization]\label{lem:r2-ulc-localization}
Let $F$ be a continuous functional on $\mathcal U_{n,t}$ that extends convexly to its convex hull.
Then $F$ has a maximizer whose likelihood ratio $P/B$ is geometric on its support interval.
A singleton is permitted when $t$ is an integer.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Compactness gives a maximizer.
Among the maximizers, choose one that maximizes the strictly convex quantity $\sum_kP(k)^2$.
Write its positive support as $[a,b]$ and $u=P/B$.
At each equality index $u_k^2=u_{k-1}u_{k+1}$ impose
$2h_k=h_{k-1}+h_{k+1}$.
If some interior log-concavity inequality is strict, these independent second-difference equations leave a solution space of dimension at least three.
The further equations
\[
 \sum_kP(k)h_k=0,\qquad \sum_k kP(k)h_k=0
\]
therefore leave a nonzero solution $h$.
For sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, put $P^\pm(k)=P(k)(1\pm\varepsilon h_k)$ on the same support.
They preserve positivity, mass, and mean.
At an equality index, their log-concavity gap is
\[
 u_k^2\varepsilon^2(h_k^2-h_{k-1}h_{k+1})
 =u_k^2\varepsilon^2(h_k-h_{k-1})^2\ge0.
\]
The strict inequalities remain true for small $\varepsilon$.
Thus both perturbed laws belong to $\mathcal U_{n,t}$ and their average is $P$.
Convexity and maximality force both to maximize $F$.
Strict convexity of $\sum_kP(k)^2$ makes one have a larger auxiliary value, a contradiction.
Every interior inequality must therefore be equality.
This gives a geometric likelihood ratio.
Supports of one or two points already have the required form.
\end{proof}

Apply this lemma first to one argument of a maximizing pair for $\mathcal D$, and then to the other.
Each replacement preserves the global maximum.
The proposed extension is therefore equivalent to the same inequality for pairs
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-tilted-interval}
 P_I(k)=\frac{B(k)e^{\lambda_I k}\mathbf1_{\{k\in I\}}}{Z_I},
 \qquad Z_I=\sum_{k\in I}B(k)e^{\lambda_I k},
\end{equation}
where both laws have mean $t$.
For a nontrivial interval $I=[a,b]$, this requires $a<t<b$.
There is then exactly one finite $\lambda_I$.
Indeed, the derivative of the mean with respect to $\lambda_I$ is the positive variance, and its limits are $a,b$.
When $t$ is an integer, the singleton at $t$ is also included, with $\lambda_I=0$.
It covers every limiting case in which the mean reaches a support endpoint.

For precision, put $M_\kappa(k,\ell)=B(k)T_\kappa(k,\ell)$.
The joint generating polynomial of the initial and updated counts is
\begin{align}\label{eq:r2-joint-kernel}
 \sum_{k,\ell}M_\kappa(k,\ell)z^kw^\ell
 =\bigl[&(1-\theta)^2+\kappa\theta(1-\theta)
 +\theta(1-\theta)(1-\kappa)(z+w)\notag\\
 &+(\theta^2+\kappa\theta(1-\theta))zw\bigr]^n.
\end{align}
The four terms are the four joint probabilities for one bit and its update.
For two interval laws $P_I,P_L$, set
$\kappa_L=1-\operatorname{Var}(P_L)/(t(1-t/n))$.
After canceling the factor $Z_I^{-2}$, the remaining inequality is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-open-interval-inequality}
 \left[\sum_{k\in I\cap L}B(k)e^{(\lambda_I+\lambda_L)k}\right]^2
 \ge Z_L^2\sum_{k,\ell\in I}M_{\kappa_L}(k,\ell)e^{\lambda_I(k+\ell)}.
\end{equation}
\textbf{Inequality~\eqref{eq:r2-open-interval-inequality} is unproved in general.}
There are finitely many interval pairs for each fixed $n,t$, but $t$ is still continuous.
If either law is the full binomial law, the target is equality.
This follows from its likelihood ratio one and from $T_0r=\langle r,1\rangle_B=1$.
These cases check the normalization; they do not establish the other pairs.

A failure of~\eqref{eq:r2-open-interval-inequality} would refute this larger scalar extension.
It would not by itself give a matrix counterexample, since the interval laws need not be Poisson-binomial.
The unsquared contraction from Section~\ref{sec:rank-two-finite} is not asserted for this larger class.
The accompanying exact rational check verifies the permanent identity, variance identity, Gram identity, and count-law update in orders three, four, and five.
One of its means is $5/3$.
Those checks support the implementation of the identities; the proofs above establish the identities in general.


\section{Coordinate deletion and exact finite
certificates}\label{sec:deletion}

Coordinate deletion extracts the coefficient of one variable.
A deletion inequality holds for matrix-product polynomials of degree three and for two-shift cycles of every order, as proved below.
The unrestricted deletion inequality remains unproved.

For a homogeneous nonnegative polynomial \(q\) of degree \(d\) in \(d\)
variables, define \[
 \operatorname{cap}(q)=\inf_{x_i>0}\frac{q(x)}{x_1\cdots x_d}.
\] A positive diagonal matrix \(D\) is capacity-balancing when
\(q(Dx)/\det D\) is balanced. Here balance means that each derivative at
\(\mathbf1\) equals the value at \(\mathbf1\). The polynomial need not
be normalized to value one. At a finite capacity minimum, these are the
logarithmic critical-point equations. For a normalized polynomial they
agree with the definition in Section \ref{sec:scalar}.

The squared Fischer norm \(N\) uses the same definition at each degree and
number of variables. If \(q_j=[x_j]p\), write \[
 H_j=\frac{N(q_j(D_jx))}{(\det D_j)^2}
\] when a capacity-balancing diagonal exists. The degree-three case
below has a specified limiting expression when a finite diagonal does
not exist. The rank-two closure section treats its own limiting cases.
No general minimum-norm interpretation of \(H_j\) is assumed.

The cubic deletion inequality is \[
 \frac13\sum_{j=1}^3H_j\ge\frac49 N(p_A)
\] for every \(A\in\Omega_3\), with equality exactly at \(J_3\). Its proof follows.

\subsection{The cubic deletion
theorem}\label{deletion-the-cubic-deletion-theorem}

Write

\[
p_A(x)=\prod_{i=1}^3(Ax)_i,\qquad
b=[x_1x_2x_3]p_A=\operatorname{per}A.
\]

For each \(j\), the deletion \(q_j=[x_j]p_A\) is a binary quadratic

\[
q_j(x_k,x_\ell)=a_jx_k^2+b x_kx_\ell+c_jx_\ell^2.
\]

When \(a_j,c_j>0\), choose diagonal scales with product one and with
\(a_jd_k^2=c_jd_\ell^2=\sqrt{a_jc_j}\). The scaled quadratic is balanced
and realizes the capacity infimum, so its squared Fischer norm is

\[
H_j=2a_jc_j+b^2+2a_jc_j=b^2+4a_jc_j.
\]

If one of \(a_j,c_j\) vanishes, this expression is the limit of the
balanced norms. A finite balancing diagonal need not exist in that
degenerate case. The final polynomial inequality extends to it by
continuity.

The permanent Cauchy--Binet identity gives

\[
N(p_A)=\operatorname{per}(AA^T).
\]

Since \(g(n)=((n-1)/n)^{n-1}\), and in particular \(g(3)=4/9\), the average-deletion statement
\(\frac13\sum_jH_j\ge g(3)N(p_A)\) is exactly

\begin{equation}\label{eq:cd-a}
3\sum_jH_j-4N(p_A)
=9b^2+12\sum_ja_jc_j-4\operatorname{per}(AA^T)\ge0.
\end{equation}


\subsection{Birkhoff parametrization and chamber
coverage}\label{deletion-birkhoff-parametrization-and-chamber-coverage}

List the six permutation matrices of order three as \(P_0,\ldots,P_5\),
with \(P_0=I\), and write

\[
A=\sum_{r=0}^5t_rP_r,\qquad t_r\ge0.
\]

For a doubly stochastic matrix the weights can be chosen with sum one.
The polynomial in \eqref{eq:cd-a} is homogeneous of degree six in the weights, so
the coefficient calculation need not impose this normalization. It
proves the inequality for every common row and column sum.

Given an ordering \(\pi\) of the six weights, set

\[
t_{\pi(r)}=z_0+\cdots+z_r,\qquad z_r\ge0.
\]

The 720 chambers cover the nonnegative orthant, including ties. A row
permutation of \(A\) leaves \(p_A\) unchanged because it only reorders
its linear factors. Its action on the six permutation matrices is
transitive. Therefore whichever Birkhoff vertex has the smallest weight
can be sent to \(P_0\). This justifies the primary reduction to the 120
orderings with \(\pi(0)=0\).

Base-seven monomial encoding is exact because every exponent is at most six.
Every intermediate product also has total degree at most six, so addition of exponents cannot cause a carry between positions.

\subsection{Independent exact
verification}\label{deletion-independent-exact-verification}

The independent checker uses a different construction:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  It builds the matrix directly in gap coordinates, without expanding in
  raw Birkhoff weights and without applying the primary triangular
  substitution.
\item
  It computes \(N(p_A)\) directly as \(\operatorname{per}(AA^T)\),
  rather than summing factorial-weighted squares of product-polynomial
  coefficients.
\item
  It obtains \(a_j,c_j\) from the three possible choices of the unique
  row assigned to column \(j\).
\item
  It uses base-eight exponent encoding and checks all 720 chambers
  without relying on the symmetry reduction.
\end{itemize}

The program is \nolinkurl{deletion_n3_independent_audit.py}. Its
recorded output has the corresponding \texttt{.json} name. The primary
program is \nolinkurl{deletion_n3_certificate.py}.
