The program \nolinkurl{deletion_n3_crosscheck.py} gives a second independent
construction with exponent tuples. The three implementations agree exactly.

The exact integer computations give the following results:

{\def\LTcaptype{}\stepcounter{LT@tables} % unnumbered, unique table instance
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Quantity & Value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Chambers checked & 720 \\
Positive coefficient positions checked & 324,360 \\
Smallest positive coefficient & 5 \\
Primary chamber counts and minima matched & 120 of 120 \\
Independent scalar integer point checks & 5,760 \\
\end{longtable}
}

The positive coefficient counts per chamber have the following
histogram:

{\def\LTcaptype{}\stepcounter{LT@tables} % unnumbered, unique table instance
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}rr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Number of positive coefficients & Number of chambers \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
411 & 72 \\
452 & 72 \\
454 & 144 \\
455 & 144 \\
456 & 288 \\
\end{longtable}
}

The scalar checks evaluate the generated polynomial at nonnegative
integer gap vectors and compare it with direct scalar permanents and the
explicit binary-quadratic formula for \(H_j\). They do not reuse the
primary coefficient-norm expansion.

The coefficient signs establish the inequality on each whole nonnegative parameter region.

\subsection{Equality in the order-three
certificate}\label{deletion-equality-in-the-order-three-certificate}

The independent computation also checks the coefficients of the pure powers
\(z_r^6\). In every chamber:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The coefficient of \(z_0^6\) is zero.
\item
  The coefficients of \(z_1^6,z_2^6,z_4^6,z_5^6\) are positive.
\item
  The coefficient of \(z_3^6\) is positive in 648 chambers and zero in
  72 chambers.
\item
  In each of those 72 chambers, the three permutation matrices in the
  tail sum to the all-ones matrix \(E\).
\end{itemize}

Since all coefficients are nonnegative, equality in \eqref{eq:cd-a} forces
\(z_1=z_2=z_4=z_5=0\). If \(z_3\) is nonzero, its coefficient must
vanish, and its tail is therefore \(E\). The uniform baseline is
\(\sum_rP_r=2E\). In either case, \(A\) is a scalar multiple of \(E\).

Conversely, a scalar multiple of \(E\) gives equality directly. Thus
equality in the average-deletion certificate holds exactly at \(J_3\)
among doubly stochastic matrices.

\subsection{Conditional induction: normalization and
closure}\label{deletion-conditional-induction-normalization-and-closure}

For a homogeneous degree-\(n\) polynomial \(p\) with nonnegative
coefficients, call it balanced when

\[
\partial_ip(\mathbf1)=p(\mathbf1)\quad\text{for every }i.
\]

The coefficient distribution then has mean exponent one in each
coordinate. Weighted AM--GM gives \(\operatorname{cap}(p)=p(\mathbf1)\).

Suppose the following lemma were proved for every \(n\ge2\) and every
balanced real-stable polynomial with strictly positive degree-\(n\)
coefficients:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:cd-b}
\max_j H_j\ge g(n)N(p),\qquad
g(n)=\left(\frac{n-1}{n}\right)^{n-1}.
\end{equation}

For an index \(j\) supplied by \eqref{eq:cd-b}, put

\[
r(x)=\frac{q_j(D_jx)}{\det D_j},\qquad q_j=[x_j]p.
\]

Then:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Differentiation followed by specialization \(x_j=0\) preserves real
  stability, or gives zero. Here strict coefficient positivity prevents
  zero.
\item
  \(q_j\) has nonnegative coefficients, degree \(n-1\), and \(n-1\)
  variables.
\item
  Positive diagonal scaling and positive scalar multiplication preserve
  stability and coefficient positivity.
\item
  The capacity critical-point equations say that \(r\) is balanced.
\item
  The central coefficient of \(r\) equals the central coefficient of
  \(p\): diagonal scaling multiplies that coefficient by exactly
  \(\det D_j\).
\item
  \(N(r)=H_j\) by definition.
\end{enumerate}

Therefore the inductive inequality in degree \(n-1\) would give

\[
\bigl([x_1\cdots x_n]p\bigr)^2
\ \ge c_{n-1}H_j
\ \ge c_{n-1}g(n)N(p)
\ =c_nN(p).
\]

No normalization to \(p(\mathbf1)=1\) is necessary, since all these
inequalities are homogeneous of degree two in the polynomial
coefficients. If the lemma is instead stated with that normalization,
divide by \(p(\mathbf1)\) and restore the scalar afterward.

The degree-one base case is equality. In degree two, a balanced binary
quadratic has the form \(a x^2+bxy+a y^2\). Real stability gives
\(b^2\ge4a^2\), and hence

\[
b^2\ge\frac12(b^2+4a^2)=c_2N(p).
\]

This also shows that the degree-three matrix-product certificate,
together with the valid binary base case, independently implies the
strong permanent inequality for all doubly stochastic matrices of order
three.

An induction also requires closure of the polynomial class under deletion. The polynomial
\(q_j\) is generally a sum of products, not a single product of
nonnegative linear forms. A theorem about matrix products in degree
three does not establish \eqref{eq:cd-b} for all stable polynomials in degree
three, and therefore does not by itself enable the next induction step
in degree four.

\subsection{Boundary cases can be handled without assuming finite
balancing}\label{deletion-boundary-cases-can-be-handled-without-assuming-finite-balancing}

For a nonzero homogeneous polynomial of degree \(d\) in \(d\) variables
with strictly positive coefficients, capacity balancing exists. On the
logarithmic gauge \(\sum_i y_i=0\), the function

\[
\log q(e^y)-\sum_i y_i
\]

is coercive because \(q\) contains every pure power with a positive
coefficient. It therefore attains a minimum. The Hessian is a covariance
matrix; with full monomial support it is positive definite on the gauge
subspace. The balancing diagonal is unique up to common scalar
multiplication, which does not change \(H_j\).

For a general nonnegative balanced real-stable polynomial, use the approximation

\[
p_\varepsilon(x)=p(M_\varepsilon x),\qquad
M_\varepsilon=(1-\varepsilon)I+\varepsilon J_n,\quad 0<\varepsilon<1.
\]

This approximation has all the required properties:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(M_\varepsilon\) is strictly positive and doubly stochastic.
\item
  It maps vectors with all imaginary parts positive to vectors with all
  imaginary parts positive, so \(p_\varepsilon\) is real stable.
\item
  The chain rule and double stochasticity preserve balance.
\item
  It preserves degree and makes every degree-\(n\) coefficient strictly
  positive.
\item
  \(p_\varepsilon\to p\) coefficientwise as \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\).
\end{itemize}

Thus one can prove the conditional induction for strictly positive
coefficient polynomials and pass directly to the final inequality by
continuity of its central coefficient and squared Fischer norm. This avoids
asserting that a finite capacity-balancing diagonal exists for every
degenerate deletion polynomial. It also avoids the unjustified
perturbation \(p+\varepsilon(\sum x_i)^n\), since arbitrary sums of
stable polynomials need not be stable.

\subsection{The permanent-gradient
identity}\label{deletion-the-permanent-gradient-identity}

For a matrix product \(p_A=\prod_i(Ax)_i\), the following identity
computes \(H_j\) without storing the full coefficient array. It holds
for every specified positive diagonal \(D_j\), independently of whether
it balances capacity. Fix \(j\), write

\[
a=A[:,j],\qquad B=A[:,\{0,\ldots,n-1\}\setminus\{j\}]D_j,
\qquad G=BB^T.
\]

Differentiating the product gives

\[
q_j(D_jx)=\sum_i a_i\prod_{r\ne i}(Bx)_r.
\]

The Fischer inner product of the products indexed by \(i,k\) is the
permanent of \(G\) with row \(i\) and column \(k\) deleted.
Consequently,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:td-7}
H_j=\frac{a^T\bigl(\nabla\operatorname{per}(G)\bigr)a}{(\det D_j)^2}.
\end{equation}

Here the gradient is the entrywise matrix derivative. The deletion polynomial can also be written using
\(L_i(x)=\sum_{k\ne j}a_{ik}x_k\),

\begin{equation}\label{eq:td-8}
q_j(x)=\left(\prod_iL_i(x)\right)\left(\sum_i\frac{a_{ij}}{L_i(x)}\right).
\end{equation}

The rational expression \eqref{eq:td-8} is first read where all \(L_i(x)\) are
nonzero. Multiplication by their product gives the polynomial identity,
which extends to every \(x\). The permanent-gradient formula follows
from the same coefficient pairing as Lemma
\ref{lem:coefficient-inner-product}, now with \(n-1\) row forms. It is
an identity and does not supply a lower bound for \(H_j\).

\subsection{Deletion on a two-shift
cycle}\label{deletion-deletion-on-a-two-shift-cycle}

This section proves \(H_j\ge g(n)N(p_n)\), where
\(g(n)=((n-1)/n)^{n-1}\), for every two-shift cycle in every order
\(n\ge2\). The family already satisfies the strong permanent inequality.
This additional result gives its exact deletion behavior.

Take

\[
p_n(x)=\prod_{i\bmod n}(a x_i+b x_{i+1}),\qquad a,b\ge0.
\]

Deleting one variable joins its adjacent factors:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:td-9}
q_j=(a^2x_{j-1}+b^2x_{j+1})
\prod_{i\notin\{j-1,j\}}(a x_i+b x_{i+1}).
\end{equation}

For \(a,b>0\), diagonal balancing turns \eqref{eq:td-9}, after division by the
product of diagonal entries, into the length-\(n-1\) cycle product with
weights

\begin{equation}\label{eq:td-10}
a'=a^{n/(n-1)},\qquad b'=b^{n/(n-1)}.
\end{equation}

To verify this, view \eqref{eq:td-9} as a cycle of \(n-1\) two-term forms with
coefficient pairs \(\alpha_i,\beta_i\). Their products are \(a^n,b^n\).
Choose variable scales so that every transformed ratio of coefficients
is \(a'/b'\); this is possible because the product of the required
ratios is one. The product of row multipliers is exactly the product of
variable scales. The resulting circulant product is balanced, and
therefore realizes the capacity minimum.

Let \(N_n(a,b)=N(p_n)\). With \(d=a^2+b^2\), let \(\lambda,\mu\) be the
roots of \(z^2-dz-a^2b^2=0\). The cyclic tridiagonal permanent formula
gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:td-11}
N_n(a,b)=2(ab)^n+\lambda^n+\mu^n.
\end{equation}

At \(n=1\), this reads \(2ab+\lambda+\mu=(a+b)^2\), which also agrees
with the coincident-shift one-dimensional matrix.

Thus \eqref{eq:td-10} gives the exact identity

\begin{equation}\label{eq:td-12}
H_j=N_{n-1}\bigl(a^{n/(n-1)},b^{n/(n-1)}\bigr).
\end{equation}

Formula \eqref{eq:td-11} gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:td-13}
H_j\ge(\sqrt2-1)N_n(a,b)\quad(n\ge2).
\end{equation}

By homogeneity assume \(ab=1\) and put \(t=\log(a/b)\). Define

\[
\phi(t)=\operatorname{arsinh}(\cosh t),\qquad
h=\phi(0)=\log(1+\sqrt2).
\]

Then

\[
\phi''(t)=\frac{2\cosh t}{(1+\cosh^2t)^{3/2}}>0,
\]

so with \(u=n\phi(t)\) and \(v=(n-1)\phi(nt/(n-1))\), convexity yields
\(v\ge u-h\). Write \(r=e^{-h}=\sqrt2-1\). Formula \eqref{eq:td-11} becomes
\(N_n=2+e^u+(-1)^ne^{-u}\), and its analog for \eqref{eq:td-12} has exponent
\(v\) and the opposite parity. These expressions increase with their
nonnegative exponent. If \(n\) is odd,

\[
\bigl(2+e^{u-h}+e^{-u+h}\bigr)
-r\bigl(2+e^u-e^{-u}\bigr)
=2(1-r)+e^{-u}(r+r^{-1})>0.
\]

If \(n\) is even, the corresponding difference is

\[
2(1-r)-e^{-u}(r+r^{-1})
=(r+r^{-1})(r-e^{-u})\ge0,
\]

because \(u\ge h\). This proves \eqref{eq:td-13}, with zero weights handled by
continuity.

Since \(g(n)\) decreases with \(n\), and \(g(5)=256/625<\sqrt2-1\),
\eqref{eq:td-13} proves \(H_j\ge g(n)N(p_n)\) for this family in every order
\(n\ge5\). The small orders follow directly:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  For \(n=2\),
  \(H=(a^2+b^2)^2\ge\tfrac12((a^2+b^2)^2+4a^2b^2)=g(2)N_2\).
\item
  For \(n=3\), after division by \(a^3b^3\), \(9H-4N_3\) equals
  \(5z^3-39z+46\), where \(z=a/b+b/a\ge2\). Its derivative is positive
  for \(z\ge2\), and its value at two is eight.
\item
  For \(n=4\), with \(ab=1\), set \(x=\cosh(2t/3)=1+y\), \(y\ge0\). The
  difference \(64H-27N_4\) is \[
  52+1848y+11892y^2+31424y^3+31968y^4+14208y^5+2368y^6,
  \] which is positive.
\end{itemize}

Accordingly the capacity-balanced deletion inequality is proved on every
two-shift cycle. No analogous closure under deletion has been
established for three or more arbitrary shifts: the deletion becomes a
sum of products, and does not generally remain a single circulant
product.


\section{Rank-two deletion and balanced limits}\label{sec:rank-two-closure}

The finite theorem in Section~\ref{sec:rank-two-finite} does not give a proof in all orders.
Two closure properties are relevant to an induction on the number of variables.
First, extraction of one copy of a variable preserves the rank-two product class.
This remains true after capacity balancing, even when no finite balancing scale exists.
Second, a column with a zero entry gives a real-rooted collision polynomial.
Neither result proves the deletion norm inequality needed for that induction.

Write $N(p)=\langle p,p\rangle_F$ for the squared Fischer norm.
The same coefficient formula applies to any degree and any number of variables.
The proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:coefficient-inner-product} also permits rectangular factor matrices:
the number of linear factors determines the order of the Gram permanent.

\subsection{Extraction of one copy of a variable}

For a polynomial $p$, the notation $[x_j]p$ means the coefficient of exactly one copy of $x_j$.
The result is a polynomial in the remaining variables.
It is obtained by differentiating in $x_j$ and then setting $x_j=0$.

\begin{lemma}[Binary directional derivative]\label{lem:r2-binary-derivative}
Let $F(u,v)=\prod_{i=1}^n(c_i u+d_i v)$, where $c_i,d_i\ge0$, and let $a,b\ge0$.
Then $(a\partial_u+b\partial_v)F$ is either zero or a product of $n-1$ nonnegative binary linear forms, with a positive scalar factor.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The assertion is immediate if $n=1$.
First assume $n\ge2$, all $c_i,d_i$ are positive, their ratios are distinct, and $(a,b)\ne(0,0)$.
Order the roots of $F(t,1)$ as $r_1<\cdots<r_n<0$.
For
\[
 G(t,1)=\sum_{i=1}^n(ac_i+bd_i)\prod_{k\ne i}(c_k t+d_k),
\]
the sign at $r_i$ is $(-1)^{n-i}$.
Only the summand that omits the factor with root $r_i$ is nonzero there.
The signs alternate, so $G(t,1)$ has a root in each interval $(r_i,r_{i+1})$.
Its degree is $n-1$ and its leading coefficient is positive.
It therefore factors into $n-1$ linear factors with negative roots.
Homogenization gives the asserted nonnegative binary factors.

For zero coefficients or repeated ratios, use a sequence of positive coefficients with distinct ratios.
The derivative polynomials converge coefficientwise to $G$.
Normalize each of their binary factors so its two coefficients sum to one.
The normalized factors belong to the compact segment
$\{(s,1-s):0\le s\le1\}$.
The remaining scalar is the value of the derivative at $(1,1)$.
If $G$ is nonzero, this scalar tends to the positive number $G(1,1)$.
A convergent subsequence of the factor lists gives the required factorization of $G$.
This argument includes factors $u$ and $v$.
If the derivative vanishes, it gives the stated zero case.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Rank-two deletion closure]\label{prop:r2-deletion-closure}
Let $A\ge0$ be an $n\times n$ matrix of rank at most two.
Then $[x_j]p_A$ is either zero or a positive scalar multiple of a product of $n-1$ nonnegative linear forms whose coefficient matrix has rank at most two.
If $A\in\Omega_n$, this polynomial is nonzero, and its central coefficient is $\per(A)>0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A zero row makes $p_A=0$, so suppose all rows are nonzero.
Positive row normalization changes $p_A$ only by a positive scalar.
It also preserves rank.
Thus it suffices first to assume that $A$ is row stochastic.
Its rows belong to a linear space of dimension at most two.
The coordinate-sum functional equals one on every row.
Consequently their affine span has dimension at most one.
If they are not all equal, let $v_1,v_2$ be the endpoint rows of their convex hull.
There are $\theta_i\in[0,1]$ such that
\[
 A_{i\bullet}=\theta_i v_1+(1-\theta_i)v_2.
\]
Set $L_1(x)=v_1\cdot x$, $L_2(x)=v_2\cdot x$, and
$F(u,v)=\prod_i(\theta_i u+(1-\theta_i)v)$.
Then $p_A=F(L_1,L_2)$.
If all rows are equal, use $L_1=L_2$ instead.

Write $L_1=ax_j+L'_1$ and $L_2=bx_j+L'_2$, where $a,b\ge0$ and the primed forms omit $x_j$.
The chain rule gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-singleton-extraction}
 q_j:=[x_j]p_A=(a\partial_u+b\partial_v)F(L'_1,L'_2).
\end{equation}
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:r2-binary-derivative}.
Each resulting linear factor has a nonnegative coefficient vector in the span of the coefficient vectors of $L'_1,L'_2$.
The factor matrix therefore has rank at most two.
A factor can vanish after substitution; this gives precisely the allowed zero case.
For $n\ge2$, the positive scalar from row normalization can be put into one factor.
For $n=1$, the deletion is a constant, which is covered by the scalar multiple of the empty product.

Now suppose $A$ is doubly stochastic.
For any set $S$ of rows, let $N(S)$ be the columns with a positive entry in one of those rows.
The total row mass of $S$ is $|S|$.
The column sums give $|S|\le|N(S)|$.
Hall's matching theorem gives a permutation that uses only positive entries.
Thus $\per(A)>0$.
Finally,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-central-preservation}
 \left[\prod_{k\ne j}x_k\right]q_j
 =[x_1\cdots x_n]p_A=\per(A)>0.
\end{equation}
This excludes the zero polynomial.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Capacity and a unique balanced limit}

For a nonnegative homogeneous polynomial $q$ of degree $m$ in $m$ variables, define its capacity by
\[
 \operatorname{cap}(q)=\inf_{x_1,\ldots,x_m>0}
 \frac{q(x_1,\ldots,x_m)}{x_1\cdots x_m}.
\]
If its central coefficient is $d>0$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-capacity-bounds}
 0<d\le\operatorname{cap}(q)\le q(\mathbf1)<\infty.
\end{equation}
The first inequality uses the central monomial alone.

\begin{theorem}[Unique capacity-balanced limit]\label{thm:r2-balanced-limit}
Let $m\ge1$, and let $q$ be a nonnegative homogeneous polynomial of degree $m$ in $m$ variables, with capacity $\gamma>0$.
For $z\in\R^m$ with $\sum_i z_i=0$, put
\[
 q_z(x)=q(e^{z_1}x_1,\ldots,e^{z_m}x_m),\qquad C(z)=q_z(\mathbf1).
\]
Every sequence with $C(z)\to\gamma$ has the same coefficientwise limit $\bar q$.
This limit satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-balanced-limit}
 \bar q(\mathbf1)=\gamma,
 \qquad \partial_i\bar q(\mathbf1)=\gamma\quad(1\le i\le m),
 \qquad \bar q_{\mathbf1}=q_{\mathbf1}.
\end{equation}
In particular, $\bar q/\gamma$ is normalized and balanced.
The quantity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-H-definition}
 H(q):=N(\bar q)
\end{equation}
is well-defined and equals the limit of $N(q_z)$ along every capacity-minimizing sequence.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Homogeneity permits determinant-one variable scales, so $\inf_{\sum z_i=0}C(z)=\gamma$.
Write $q_\alpha(z)=q_\alpha e^{\alpha\cdot z}$ for the coefficient of $x^\alpha$ in $q_z$.
For two such scale vectors $z,w$,
\begin{align}
 \sum_\alpha\bigl(\sqrt{q_\alpha(z)}-\sqrt{q_\alpha(w)}\bigr)^2
 &=C(z)+C(w)-2C((z+w)/2)\notag\\
 &\le C(z)+C(w)-2\gamma.\label{eq:r2-square-root-distance}
\end{align}
Along any capacity-minimizing sequence, the vector of square roots of the coefficients is Cauchy.
There are finitely many coefficients, so this vector has a limit.
The same inequality, with one point from each of two minimizing sequences, shows that their limits agree.
Squaring the coordinates gives a unique coefficientwise limit $\bar q$.
Its coefficient sum is $\gamma$.
The central coefficient is unchanged because its scaling multiplier is $e^{\sum_i z_i}=1$.

For any fixed $h\in\R^m$, the capacity bound gives
\[
 q_z(e^{h_1},\ldots,e^{h_m})e^{-\sum_i h_i}\ge\gamma.
\]
Pass to the coefficientwise limit.
The smooth function $\bar q(e^h)e^{-\sum_i h_i}$ has minimum $\gamma$ at $h=0$.
Its derivative in coordinate $i$ at zero is
$\partial_i\bar q(\mathbf1)-\bar q(\mathbf1)$.
This proves the balance identities.
The squared Fischer norm is continuous in the finite coefficient vector.
This proves the assertion about $H(q)$.
\end{proof}

A finite minimizing scale need not exist.
For example, $q(x,y)=x(x+y)$ has capacity one, because $q(x,y)/(xy)=1+x/y$.
The determinant-one scales $(e^{-t},e^t)$ give
\[
 q_t(x,y)=e^{-2t}x^2+xy\longrightarrow xy.
\]
The theorem gives this limit without an assumption of finite attainment.
This example concerns the general capacity statement; it is not asserted to be a deletion of a doubly stochastic product.

\begin{corollary}[Rank-two closure through balancing]\label{cor:r2-balanced-product}
Suppose $q$ is a product of $m$ nonnegative linear forms, with coefficient matrix of rank at most two and $\operatorname{cap}(q)=\gamma>0$.
Then the limit in Theorem~\ref{thm:r2-balanced-limit} has the form
\[
 \bar q=\gamma p_M
\]
for some $M\in\Omega_m$ of rank at most two.
In particular, every singleton deletion of a doubly stochastic rank-two product has this property.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $B$ be a nonnegative coefficient matrix for the factors of $q$.
Put any positive overall scalar into one row.
No row is zero, because $q\ne0$.
For a positive diagonal variable scale $D_z$, normalize each row of $BD_z$ to sum one.
Call the resulting row-stochastic matrix $M_z$.
Its rank is at most two, and the product of the removed row sums is $C(z)$.
Hence
\[
 p_{M_z}=q_z/C(z).
\]
Along a capacity-minimizing sequence, take a convergent subsequence of $M_z$.
The row-stochastic matrices form a compact set.
Its limit $M$ is nonnegative and row stochastic.
It has rank at most two, since this condition is closed: all $3\times3$ minors vanish.
For $m<3$, the rank condition is automatic.
Continuity of the product polynomial gives $p_M=\bar q/\gamma$.

For a row-stochastic matrix,
$\partial_jp_M(\mathbf1)=\sum_i M_{ij}$.
The balance identities in Theorem~\ref{thm:r2-balanced-limit} therefore make every column sum one.
Thus $M\in\Omega_m$.
The last assertion follows from Proposition~\ref{prop:r2-deletion-closure} and~\eqref{eq:r2-capacity-bounds}.
The factor matrix need not be unique.
The polynomial limit and its squared Fischer norm are unique, which is all that this conclusion requires.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The exact missing step for induction}

The row-pair variation in Theorem~\ref{thm:struct-boundary} preserves the row space.
It therefore also gives a boundary reduction within the rank-at-most-two class.
That class is compact inside $\Omega_n$.
The closure results above would then permit induction if the following assertion were proved:
for every rank-at-most-two matrix $A\in\Omega_n$ on the boundary, some column $j$ with a zero entry satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-missing-deletion}
 H([x_j]p_A)\ge g_nN(p_A),
 \qquad g_n=\left(\frac{n-1}{n}\right)^{n-1}.
\end{equation}
\textbf{Assertion~\eqref{eq:r2-missing-deletion} is unproved.}
It is not a conclusion of the closure or capacity arguments.

To check the proposed implication, put $q_j=[x_j]p_A$ and $\bar q_j=\gamma p_M$ as above.
The strong inequality at order $n-1$, if available, gives
\[
 \per(A)^2
 =\gamma^2\per(M)^2
 \ge c_{n-1}N(\bar q_j)
 =c_{n-1}H(q_j).
\]
Here the central coefficient is preserved, and the factor $\gamma^2$ cancels in the norm.
Assertion~\eqref{eq:r2-missing-deletion} would give
\[
 \per(A)^2\ge c_{n-1}g_nN(p_A)=c_nN(p_A).
\]
The identity $c_{n-1}g_n=c_n$ is exact.
Thus this missing assertion would complete an induction, with the trivial order-one case as its base.

The version that requires the same bound for every column is false.
For example, let
\[
 A=\frac1{1500}
 \begin{pmatrix}482&725&293\\478&775&247\\540&0&960\end{pmatrix}.
\]
This matrix is doubly stochastic of rank two.
Its only zero is in column two.
For deletion of column one,
\[
 q=[x_1]p_A=a y^2+b yz+c z^2,
 \quad
 a=\frac{899}{10000},\quad b=\frac{303539}{1125000},\quad
 c=\frac{4797067}{56250000}.
\]
For a determinant-one scale $(e^t,e^{-t})$, the two outer coefficients become $ae^{2t}$ and $ce^{-2t}$.
The minimum coefficient sum occurs when both equal $\sqrt{ac}$.
Thus $H(q)=b^2+4ac$.
Direct rational arithmetic gives
\[
 g_3N(p_A)-H(q)=\frac{4550848849393}{1186523437500000}>0.
\]
The failing column has no zero, so this example does not disprove~\eqref{eq:r2-missing-deletion}.

\subsection{A real-rooted collision marginal}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:r2-collision-real-rooted}
Let $A\ge0$ have rank at most two and no zero rows.
Suppose a nonzero column $a=a_j$ contains a zero entry.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-collision-polynomial}
 h_j(z)=\per\bigl(AA^\T+(z-1)aa^\T\bigr)
\end{equation}
has nonnegative coefficients and only nonpositive real roots.
A positive constant polynomial is permitted.
If $q_k=[x_j^k]p_A$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-collision-coefficients}
 h_j(z)=\sum_{k=0}^n k!N(q_k)z^k.
\end{equation}
The same conclusion holds after arbitrary positive scaling of the other columns.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $z\ge0$, scale column $j$ of $A$ by $\sqrt z$.
The resulting Gram matrix is $AA^\T+(z-1)aa^\T$.
The coefficient identity in Lemma~\ref{lem:coefficient-inner-product}, grouped by the exponent of $x_j$, gives~\eqref{eq:r2-collision-coefficients}.
Both sides are polynomials in $z$, so the identity holds for all real $z$.
It proves coefficient nonnegativity.

A nonnegative rank-one matrix with no zero rows has every nonzero column strictly positive.
The stated hypotheses therefore imply rank two.
Write $G=AA^\T=UU^\T$, with $U$ of full column rank two.
The vector $a$ belongs to the column space of $U$, so $a=Uw$ for some $w\ne0$.
Since
\[
 G-aa^\T=A_{-j}A_{-j}^\T\succeq0,
\]
full column rank implies $I-ww^\T\succeq0$.
Indeed, $U^\T$ maps onto $\R^2$, so its quadratic form tests every vector in $\R^2$.
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-collision-s}
 0<s:=\|w\|^2\le1.
\end{equation}

Choose a row $i$ with $a_i=0$.
Its feature vector $u_i$ is nonzero, because $G_{ii}>0$.
It is orthogonal to $w$.
Thus $e_1=u_i/\|u_i\|$ and $e_2=w/\|w\|$ form an orthonormal basis of $\R^2$.
Let $(\alpha_k,\beta_k)$ be the coordinates of row $u_k$ in this basis.
Both coordinates are nonnegative:
\[
 \alpha_k=\frac{G_{ki}}{\|u_i\|}\ge0,
 \qquad \beta_k=\frac{a_k}{\|w\|}\ge0.
\]
The first inequality uses entrywise nonnegativity of $AA^\T$.
In these coordinates,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-collision-binary-gram}
 G+(z-1)aa^\T=\alpha\alpha^\T+(1-s+sz)\beta\beta^\T.
\end{equation}

Define $f(t)=\prod_{k=1}^n(\alpha_k+\beta_k t)=\sum_{k=0}^n f_kt^k$.
No factor is zero, because no row of $U$ is zero.
All roots of $f$ are nonpositive real numbers; zero roots and a missing top degree are allowed.
Apply the factorial coefficient identity to this product of binary forms.
It gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:r2-collision-binary-sum}
 h_j(z)=\sum_{k=0}^n k!(n-k)!f_k^2(1-s+sz)^k
 =n!R(1-s+sz),
 \qquad R(t)=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{f_k^2}{\binom nk}t^k.
\end{equation}
The Schur--Szeg\H{o} composition theorem states that negative-rooted degree-$n$ polynomials are closed under binomially normalized coefficientwise composition.
Its application to $f$ with itself shows that $R$ has only nonpositive real roots
\cite[Proposition~1.5]{kostov-shapiro2006}.
For zero coefficients or missing degree, first replace the coefficients of each linear factor by positive values and then take a limit.
This gives negative-rooted degree-$n$ approximants.
The limit is nonzero because $R(1)>0$.
Thus the same root conclusion holds for the limit, with a lower degree permitted.

By~\eqref{eq:r2-collision-s}, the affine substitution $1-s+sz$ has positive slope and a nonnegative constant term.
A root $r\le0$ of $R$ gives a root $(r-1+s)/s\le0$ of $h_j$.
This proves real-rootedness.
Also, $h_j(1)=\per(G)>0$ because the identity permutation contributes the positive product of the diagonal entries of $G$.
Positive scaling of other columns preserves all the hypotheses, so the proof applies after such a scale.
\end{proof}

The normalized coefficients of $h_j$ form a probability law for the occupancy of column $j$ under the factorial coefficient weights.
Real-rootedness makes this a sum of independent Bernoulli variables, with deterministic factors permitted.
Its mean need not equal one, even when $A$ is doubly stochastic.
For example,
\[
 A=\begin{pmatrix}
 1/2&1/4&1/4\\1/2&1/4&1/4\\0&1/2&1/2
 \end{pmatrix}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 h_1(z)=\frac{3+8z+4z^2}{64}.
\]
Its roots are $-1/2,-3/2$.
The corresponding Bernoulli parameters are $2/3$ and $2/5$, so
\[
 \frac{h'_1(1)}{h_1(1)}=\frac{16}{15}\ne1.
\]
The integer-mean likelihood theorem therefore cannot be applied with target mean one.
This real-rootedness result does not prove~\eqref{eq:r2-missing-deletion}.
A zero row would make $h_j$ identically zero, so no normalized law would exist.
A zero column gives a constant polynomial.
These cases explain the explicit nonvanishing hypotheses in the theorem.


\section{Local polynomial inequalities and boundary reductions}\label{sec:local-polynomial}

The matrix product polynomial places the strong Gram inequality inside a larger polynomial problem.
This section proves that problem near the uniform power.
It also gives exact reductions and counterexamples that identify the limits of the argument.
The neighborhood may depend on the degree.
No unrestricted polynomial inequality is asserted.

\subsection{The polynomial classes and the deletion functional}

A real polynomial is \emph{real stable} if it has no zero when every variable has positive imaginary part.
A nonzero homogeneous polynomial is \emph{hyperbolic in direction} $e$ if $p(e)\ne0$ and $t\mapsto p(x+te)$ has only real zeros for every real $x$.
For a homogeneous polynomial with nonnegative coefficients, hyperbolicity in a strictly positive direction implies real stability.
This follows from the hyperbolicity-cone theorem and the stability characterization in \citet[Proposition~4.4 and Theorem~4.5]{borcea-branden-liggett2009}.

For a set $S$ of nonnegative integer vectors with a common coordinate sum, \emph{M-convexity} means the following exchange property.
If $\alpha,\beta\in S$ and $\alpha_i>\beta_i$, there is a $j$ with $\alpha_j<\beta_j$ such that both $\alpha-e_i+e_j$ and $\beta+e_i-e_j$ belong to $S$.
The following conditions characterize a \emph{Lorentzian} polynomial.
A homogeneous polynomial with nonnegative coefficients is Lorentzian if its support is M-convex and every derivative of order two less than its degree has a Hessian with at most one positive eigenvalue.
Degrees zero and one have the usual nonnegative-coefficient convention.
This characterization agrees with the closed class defined by Br\"and\'en and Huh.
Real-stable homogeneous polynomials with nonnegative coefficients belong to this class.
The class is closed under limits, differentiation, specialization to zero, and nonnegative linear substitutions \cite[Theorems~2.10 and 2.25]{brandenhuh2020}.

Fix $n\ge2$ and put
\[
 m=n-1,\qquad R(x)=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^nx_i,\qquad
 p_0=R^n,\qquad g_n=\left(\frac{n-1}{n}\right)^{n-1}.
\]
Thus $c_n=g_nc_m$.
Unless stated otherwise, $p$ has degree $n$ in $n$ variables and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-balance}
 p(\mathbf1)=1,\qquad \partial_i p(\mathbf1)=1\quad(1\le i\le n).
\end{equation}
These conditions are called normalization and balance.
For an unnormalized polynomial, balance means $\partial_i p(\mathbf1)=p(\mathbf1)$.
Write
\[
 a(p)=[x_1\cdots x_n]p,\qquad N(p)=\langle p,p\rangle_F.
\]
The polynomial form of the strong inequality is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-target}
 a(p)^2\ge c_nN(p).
\end{equation}
For $p=p_A$, this is $\per(A)^2\ge c_n\per(AA^\T)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:coefficient-inner-product}.

For a degree-$m$ polynomial $q$ in $m$ variables, define its \emph{capacity} by
\[
 \operatorname{Cap}(q)=\inf_{x_i>0}\frac{q(x)}{\prod_i x_i}.
\]
A positive diagonal matrix $D$ \emph{balances} $q$ if $q(Dx)$ is balanced.
For the deletion $q_j=[x_j]p$, coefficient extraction leaves $m$ variables.
Whenever a balancing matrix $D_j$ exists, set
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 H_j(p)&=\frac{N(q_j(D_jx))}{(\det D_j)^2},&
 \mathcal D_{\rm av}(p)&=\frac{\sum_jH_j(p)}{ng_nN(p)},\\
 \mathcal D_{\max}(p)&=\frac{\max_jH_j(p)}{g_nN(p)},&
 \mathcal S(p)&=\frac{a(p)^2}{c_nN(p)}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
A common scalar factor in $D_j$ cancels in $H_j$.
All three ratios equal one at $p_0$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:lp-analytic}
Near $p_0$, the balancing matrices exist and are unique up to a common scalar factor.
In the gauge $\det D_j=1$, their logarithms and the functions $H_j$ are real analytic in the coefficients.
Also, every normalized $p$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-orthogonal}
 N(p)=c_n+N(p-p_0).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The uniform deletion is
\[
 q_{0,j}=g_n\left(\frac{\sum_{i\ne j}x_i}{m}\right)^m.
\]
Write $D=\operatorname{diag}(e^{y_i})$ with $\sum_i y_i=0$.
The logarithmic capacity objective is $\Psi_q(y)=\log q(e^y)-\sum_i y_i$.
Its gradient is the mean exponent minus $\mathbf1$.
Its Hessian is the covariance matrix of the coefficient distribution tilted by $e^{\alpha\cdot y}$.
For $q_{0,j}$ this distribution is multinomial with $m$ trials and equal cell probabilities.
The Hessian at zero is $I-\mathbf1\mathbf1^\T/m$.
It is the identity on the gauge subspace $\mathbf1^\perp$.
The implicit-function theorem gives an analytic critical point for nearby coefficients.
These coefficients are strictly positive.
The pure monomials then make $\Psi_q$ coercive on the gauge subspace, and its Hessian is positive definite there.
Thus this critical point is the unique global minimum.
For $m=1$, the gauge subspace is zero and the assertion is immediate.
Finally, the coefficients of $p_0$ give
$\langle p_0,h\rangle_F=c_nh(\mathbf1)$.
Apply this identity to $h=p-p_0$ to obtain \eqref{eq:lp-orthogonal}.
\end{proof}

Define the dimensionless distance
\[
 d_F(p,p_0)=\sqrt{N(p-p_0)/c_n}.
\]
The next calculation does not require stability.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:lp-variation}
Suppose $p_t=p_0+th+t^2k+o(t^2)$ is a normalized balanced coefficient path.
For each $j$, put
\[
 s_j=[x_j]h,\quad a_j=s_j(\mathbf1),\quad
 v_j=\nabla s_j(\mathbf1)-a_j\mathbf1,\quad
 b_j=([x_j]k)(\mathbf1).
\]
Then $\mathbf1^\T v_j=0$, and in the zero-sum logarithmic gauge,
\begin{align}
 \log D_j(t)&=-t v_j/g_n+O(t^2),\label{eq:lp-scaling}\\
 H_j(p_t)&=g_nc_n+2c_na_jt+
 \bigl(N(s_j)-2c_m\|v_j\|^2+2c_nb_j\bigr)t^2+o(t^2),\label{eq:lp-hessian-expansion}\\
 N(p_t)&=c_n+N(h)t^2+o(t^2).\label{eq:lp-norm-expansion}
\end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Euler's identity gives $\sum_i\partial_i s_j(\mathbf1)=ma_j$.
Differentiating the capacity equation in Lemma~\ref{lem:lp-analytic} gives \eqref{eq:lp-scaling}.
To compute the second coefficient, write $q_0=q_{0,j}$, $s=s_j$, $v=v_j$, and $d=-v/g_n$.
Let $L_d=\sum_i d_i x_i\partial_i$.
The first coefficient of the scaled deletion is $s+L_dq_0$.
The Fischer identities
\[
 \langle q_0,f\rangle_F=c_nf(\mathbf1),\quad
 \langle s,L_dq_0\rangle_F=g_nc_m\,d\cdot v,\quad
 N(L_dq_0)=g_n^2c_m\|d\|^2
\]
follow by expanding the uniform power.
They imply $N(s+L_dq_0)=N(s)-c_m\|v\|^2$.
The second coefficient of the scaled polynomial contains
$[x_j]k+L_ds+\tfrac12L_d^2q_0+L_eq_0$, where $\sum_i e_i=0$ is the second logarithmic displacement.
Its inner product with $2q_0$ is
\[
 2c_nb_j+2c_n d\cdot v+c_ng_n\|d\|^2
 =2c_nb_j-c_m\|v\|^2.
\]
The $L_e$ term vanishes because $\sum_i e_i=0$.
This proves \eqref{eq:lp-hessian-expansion}.
Equation~\eqref{eq:lp-norm-expansion} follows from \eqref{eq:lp-orthogonal}.
\end{proof}

For a straight path, the quadratic coefficient of $n^{-1}\sum_jH_j-g_nN(p)$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-general-quadratic}
 \mathcal Q(h)=\sum_{|\alpha|=n}
 \left(\frac{k_1(\alpha)}n-g_n\right)\alpha!h_\alpha^2
 -\frac{2c_m}{n}\sum_j\|v_j\|^2,
\end{equation}
where $k_1(\alpha)=\#\{j:\alpha_j=1\}$.
Indeed, $\sum_jN([x_j]h)=\sum_\alpha k_1(\alpha)\alpha!h_\alpha^2$.
The first directional derivative of $\mathcal D_{\max}$ is $2\max_j a_j/g_n$.
At second order, take the largest individual second coefficient among the indices with maximal $a_j$.
This accounts for the possible nondifferentiability of the maximum.

\subsection{The stable tangent cone and the sharp local theorem}

A \emph{contrast vector} is a real vector orthogonal to $\mathbf1$.
Let $P=I-\mathbf1\mathbf1^\T/n$ denote the projection onto the contrast subspace.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:lp-stable-tangent}
The possible first variations of normalized balanced stable polynomials at $p_0$ are exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-tangent}
 h(x)=-R(x)^{n-2}x^\T Bx,\qquad B=B^\T\succeq0,\qquad B\mathbf1=0.
\end{equation}
The matrix $B$ is unique.
Every such variation is realized by the straight path $p_t=p_0+th$ for all sufficiently small $t\ge0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix a contrast vector $x$.
For a one-sided differentiable stable path $p_t=p_0+th+o(t)$, the polynomial
$f_t(z)=p_t(z\mathbf1+x)$ is monic and real-rooted.
Its coefficient of $z^{n-1}$ is $\nabla p_t(\mathbf1)\cdot x=0$.
If its roots are $\rho_1(t),\ldots,\rho_n(t)$, then
\[
 \sum_i\rho_i(t)=0,\qquad
 [z^{n-2}]f_t=-\frac12\sum_i\rho_i(t)^2=O(t).
\]
Thus each root is $O(\sqrt t)$.
Every elementary symmetric function of order $r\ge3$ is $O(t^{r/2})=o(t)$.
The first variation therefore has only contrast degree two.
Its quadratic form is nonpositive on the contrast subspace.
Normalization removes contrast degree zero, and balance removes contrast degree one.
This gives the unique matrix in \eqref{eq:lp-tangent}.
The same argument applies to limits of normalized coefficient differences along sequences.

Conversely, consider
\[
 p_t=R^{n-2}(R^2-tx^\T Bx).
\]
For small $t\ge0$, the quadratic factor has strictly positive coefficients.
Its matrix is $\mathbf1\mathbf1^\T/n^2-tB$.
It has one positive eigenvalue and all other eigenvalues are nonpositive.
It is positive on every strictly positive real vector.
If it vanished at $u+iv$ with $v>0$, its real and imaginary parts would give
$Q(u)=Q(v)>0$ and $Q(u,v)=0$.
The orthogonal complement of a positive vector for a quadratic form with one positive direction is negative semidefinite.
This contradicts $Q(u)>0$.
The quadratic factor is stable, and so is its product with $R^{n-2}$.
The identity $B\mathbf1=0$ gives normalization and balance.
\end{proof}

When $\operatorname{rank}B\ge2$, the quadratic factor has rank at least three for $t>0$.
It cannot factor into two real linear forms.
Thus the local theorem includes polynomials that are not products of linear forms.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:lp-stable-variations}
For the tangent \eqref{eq:lp-tangent}, put $\kappa_n=((n-1)/n)^{n-3}$.
Then
\begin{align}
 a_j&=\kappa_nB_{jj},\label{eq:lp-aj}\\
 N(h)&=c_n\frac{2n}{n-1}\operatorname{tr}(B^2),\label{eq:lp-tangent-norm}\\
 a(h)&=c_n\frac{n}{n-1}\operatorname{tr}B.\label{eq:lp-tangent-central}
\end{align}
Consequently,
\begin{align}
 \mathcal D_{\rm av}(p_t)&=1+\frac{2n}{(n-1)^2}\operatorname{tr}B\,t+o(t),\label{eq:lp-first-deletion}\\
 \mathcal D_{\max}(p_t)&=1+2\left(\frac n{n-1}\right)^2\max_jB_{jj}\,t+o(t),\label{eq:lp-first-maximum}\\
 \mathcal S(p_t)&=1+\frac{2n}{n-1}\operatorname{tr}B\,t+o(t).\label{eq:lp-first-strong}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Set every variable except $x_j=z$ equal to one.
Then
\[
 h(1,\ldots,z,\ldots,1)
 =-B_{jj}\left(\frac{n-1+z}{n}\right)^{n-2}(z-1)^2.
\]
Its linear coefficient is \eqref{eq:lp-aj}.
For \eqref{eq:lp-tangent-norm}, use an orthonormal coordinate with first coordinate $n^{-1/2}\sum_i x_i$.
The remaining coordinates are contrast coordinates.
The identity $\langle f,g\rangle_F=f(\partial)g|_{x=0}$ shows that the Fischer inner product is invariant under orthogonal changes of variables.
The squared Fischer norm of a quadratic form with matrix $B$ is $2\operatorname{tr}(B^2)$.
The factor $R^{n-2}$ therefore gives
$N(h)=2(n-2)!n^{-(n-2)}\operatorname{tr}(B^2)$.
This is \eqref{eq:lp-tangent-norm}.
In the central coefficient, the diagonal terms of $x^\T Bx$ make no contribution.
The off-diagonal entries sum to $-\operatorname{tr}B$ because $B\mathbf1=0$.
Thus $a(h)=(n-2)!n^{-(n-2)}\operatorname{tr}B$.
The three expansions now follow from Lemma~\ref{lem:lp-variation}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:lp-second}
Along the straight stable path in Theorem~\ref{thm:lp-stable-tangent}, the quadratic coefficient of $\mathcal D_{\rm av}$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-second}
 Q_n(B)=\frac{n^2(3n^2-5n+4)}{(n-1)^5}\sum_jB_{jj}^2
 -\frac{2n(2n-1)}{(n-1)^3}\operatorname{tr}(B^2).
\end{equation}
It is strictly negative for $n\ge3$ and $B\ne0$.
The quadratic coefficient of $\mathcal S$ is
\[
 \frac{n^2}{(n-1)^2}(\operatorname{tr}B)^2
 -\frac{2n}{n-1}\operatorname{tr}(B^2).
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Assume first $n\ge3$.
Fix $j$, put $b=B_{jj}$, and let $u=B_{-j,j}$ and $C=B_{-j,-j}$.
Write the remaining variables as $y=r\mathbf1+z$, with $\sum z_i=0$.
Let $r_j=u+(b/m)\mathbf1$ and $B_j=P_mCP_m$, where $P_m=I-\mathbf1\mathbf1^\T/m$.
Coefficient extraction gives
\[
 s_j=\kappa_n\left(br^m-\frac2n r^{m-1}r_j\cdot z
 -\frac{n-2}{n}r^{m-2}z^\T B_jz\right),\qquad
 v_j=-\frac{2\kappa_n}{n}r_j.
\]
The three contrast degrees are orthogonal for the Fischer inner product.
Their squared norms, before multiplication by their displayed coefficients, are
$c_m$, $c_m\|r_j\|^2$, and $2mc_m\operatorname{tr}(B_j^2)/(m-1)$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:lp-variation} gives the individual quadratic coefficient
\[
 \left(\frac nm\right)^4
 \left(b^2-\frac4{n^2}\|r_j\|^2
 +\frac{2m(n-2)}{n^2}\operatorname{tr}(B_j^2)\right)
 -\frac{2n}{m}\operatorname{tr}(B^2).
\]
Expanding the projections and using $B\mathbf1=0$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \sum_j\|r_j\|^2
 &=\operatorname{tr}(B^2)-\frac n{n-1}\sum_jB_{jj}^2,\\
 \sum_j\operatorname{tr}(B_j^2)
 &=\frac{n(n-3)}{n-1}\operatorname{tr}(B^2)
 +\frac{n^2}{(n-1)^2}\sum_jB_{jj}^2.
\end{align*}
Averaging proves \eqref{eq:lp-second}.
For $n=2$, write $B=b\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1\\-1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ and extract the two linear deletions directly.
The quadratic coefficient is zero, as \eqref{eq:lp-second} states.
For every $n$, the zero row sums and Cauchy--Schwarz give
$\sum_jB_{jj}^2\le(n-1)\operatorname{tr}(B^2)/n$.
For $n\ge3$, substitution yields
\[
 Q_n(B)\le-\frac{n(n-2)(n+1)}{(n-1)^4}\operatorname{tr}(B^2)<0.
\]
Finally, insert \eqref{eq:lp-tangent-norm} and \eqref{eq:lp-tangent-central} into the ratio defining $\mathcal S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:lp-stable-neighborhood}
For each $n\ge2$, there is a $\delta_n>0$ such that every normalized balanced stable $p$ with
$0<d_F(p,p_0)<\delta_n$ satisfies
\[
 \mathcal D_{\rm av}(p)>1,\qquad \mathcal D_{\max}(p)>1,\qquad
 a(p)^2>c_nN(p).
\]
Moreover, the sharp asymptotic bounds are
\begin{align}
 \liminf_{p\to p_0}\frac{\mathcal D_{\rm av}(p)-1}{d_F(p,p_0)}
 &\ge\frac{\sqrt{2n}}{(n-1)^{3/2}},\label{eq:lp-sharp-deletion}\\
 \liminf_{p\to p_0}\frac{\mathcal S(p)-1}{d_F(p,p_0)}
 &\ge\sqrt{\frac{2n}{n-1}}.\label{eq:lp-sharp-strong}
\end{align}
The limits run through distinct normalized balanced stable polynomials.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For a fixed nonzero tangent, divide \eqref{eq:lp-first-deletion} and \eqref{eq:lp-first-strong} by the distance from \eqref{eq:lp-tangent-norm}.
The resulting factors are the constants in \eqref{eq:lp-sharp-deletion} and \eqref{eq:lp-sharp-strong}, multiplied by
$\operatorname{tr}B/\sqrt{\operatorname{tr}(B^2)}\ge1$.

To include every approach to $p_0$, take an arbitrary sequence $p_\ell\to p_0$ and set
$t_\ell=\sqrt{N(p_\ell-p_0)}$ and $h_\ell=(p_\ell-p_0)/t_\ell$.
The coefficient unit sphere is compact.
Each convergent subsequence of $h_\ell$ has a limit of norm one.
The root-variance proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:lp-stable-tangent}, with $O(t_\ell)$ in place of $O(t)$, puts this limit in the same tangent cone.
Analyticity gives a uniform first-order remainder on the compact sphere.
Thus both lower bounds hold for the arbitrary sequence.
Their positive constants give the stated neighborhood.
Also $\mathcal D_{\max}\ge\mathcal D_{\rm av}$.
Rank-one matrices $B=vv^\T$ with $v\perp\mathbf1$ realize equality in both asymptotic constants.
\end{proof}

The negative second coefficient in Proposition~\ref{prop:lp-second} is consistent with this theorem.
The positive first coefficient dominates it near $p_0$.
It also shows that convexity of the deletion ratio along these stable straight paths cannot prove a global inequality.

\subsection{A variance-deficit quantity and exact local control}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:lp-energy}
For every normalized balanced stable $p$, let
\[
 B(p)=-\tfrac12P\nabla^2p(\mathbf1)P,\qquad E(p)=2\operatorname{tr}B(p).
\]
Then $B(p)\succeq0$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-energy}
 E(p)=n-1-\operatorname{tr}\operatorname{Cov}(\alpha)\ge0,
\end{equation}
where $\Pr(\alpha=\gamma)=p_\gamma$.
Equality holds exactly at $p=p_0$.
For a doubly stochastic matrix,
\[
 B(p_A)=\tfrac12(A-J_n)^\T(A-J_n),\qquad E(p_A)=\|A-J_n\|_F^2.
\]
Uniform variable mixing satisfies
\[
 p_s(x)=p((J_n+s(I-J_n))x),\qquad E(p_s)=s^2E(p).
\]
As $E(p)\to0$ within this stable class,
\begin{align}
 \mathcal D_{\rm av}(p)&=1+\frac{n}{(n-1)^2}E(p)+O_n(E(p)^{3/2}),\label{eq:lp-energy-deletion}\\
 \mathcal S(p)&=1+\frac{n}{n-1}E(p)+O_n(E(p)^{3/2}).\label{eq:lp-energy-strong}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a contrast vector $x$, the monic real-rooted polynomial $p(z\mathbf1+x)$ has root sum zero.
Its quadratic contrast coefficient is therefore $-\tfrac12\sum_i\rho_i^2\le0$.
This proves $B(p)\succeq0$.
Balance gives $\mathbb E\alpha=\mathbf1$ and
\[
 \operatorname{Cov}(\alpha)=\nabla^2p(\mathbf1)+I-\mathbf1\mathbf1^\T.
\]
This covariance annihilates $\mathbf1$ because $\sum_i\alpha_i=n$.
Take the trace after projection to obtain \eqref{eq:lp-energy}.
If $E=0$, the positive semidefinite matrix $B(p)$ vanishes.
Every contrast restriction has zero root variance and hence equals $z^n$.
These restrictions determine $p$, so $p=p_0$.
For $p_A$, direct differentiation gives $\nabla^2p_A(\mathbf1)=\mathbf1\mathbf1^\T-A^\T A$.
Projection proves the matrix formulas.
The mixing map acts as multiplication by $s$ on contrast vectors, which proves its exact scaling relation.

Expand by contrast degree,
\[
 p=R^n-R^{n-2}x^\T B(p)x+\sum_{r=3}^nR^{n-r}f_r(x),
\]
where each $f_r$ depends only on $Px$.
For a unit contrast vector $x$, its restriction coefficients are elementary symmetric functions of the real roots.
The elementary symmetric mean inequality, followed by Cauchy--Schwarz, gives
\[
 |f_r(x)|\le\binom nr
 \left(\frac{\sum_i\rho_i^2}{n}\right)^{r/2}
 =\binom nr\left(\frac{2x^TB(p)x}{n}\right)^{r/2}.
\]
Finite-dimensional norm equivalence gives $f_r=O_n(E^{r/2})$ in coefficient norm.
The quadratic contrast part is $O_n(E)$.
Apply the analytic first-variation formulas to this expansion.
The higher contrast degrees give $O_n(E^{3/2})$, and the analytic quadratic remainder is $O_n(E^2)$.
This proves \eqref{eq:lp-energy-deletion} and \eqref{eq:lp-energy-strong}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:lp-matrix-local}
For each $n\ge2$, there is an $\varepsilon_n>0$ such that every doubly stochastic $A$ with
$0<\|A-J_n\|_F<\varepsilon_n$ satisfies both strict strong Gram inequalities and $\Delta_n(A)>0$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The normalized balanced stable polynomials $p_A$ and $p_{A^\T}$ depend continuously on $A$ and converge to $p_0$ as $A\to J_n$.
Proposition~\ref{prop:lp-energy} shows that neither equals $p_0$ when $A\ne J_n$.
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:lp-stable-neighborhood} to both polynomials and then Lemma~\ref{lem:gram}.
\end{proof}

The quantity $E$ does not make the deletion ratio monotone.
Let $A=(I+P_3)/2$, where $P_3$ is a cyclic permutation, and let $A_s=J_3+s(A-J_3)$.
The central coefficient of $p_{A_s}$ is
$b(s)=2/9+s^2/12-s^3/18$.
Each of its six coefficients of type $(2,1,0)$ is $a(s)=1/9+s^3/72$.
Every deletion is balanced, with $H(s)=b(s)^2+4a(s)^2$.
Direct differentiation at $s=1$ gives
\[
 H=\tfrac18,\quad H'=\tfrac1{24},\quad N=\tfrac14,\quad N'=\tfrac18,
 \qquad \left.\frac{d}{ds}\mathcal D_{\rm av}(p_{A_s})\right|_{s=1}=-\frac3{16}.
\]
Thus the ratio decreases near this endpoint while $E$ increases.

\subsection{The Lorentzian local theorem}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:lp-lorentzian-local}
For each $n\ge2$, a neighborhood of $p_0$ exists in which every distinct normalized balanced Lorentzian polynomial satisfies
\[
 \mathcal D_{\rm av}(p)>1\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad a(p)^2>c_nN(p).
\]
No sharp distance constant for this larger class is asserted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At $p_0$, the Hessian of every order-$(n-2)$ derivative is $c_n\mathbf1\mathbf1^\T$.
The normalized balanced Lorentzian tangent cone therefore consists exactly of the polynomials $h$ with $h(\mathbf1)=0$, $\nabla h(\mathbf1)=0$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-lorentzian-tangent}
 P\operatorname{Hess}(\partial^\beta h)P\preceq0\qquad(|\beta|=n-2).
\end{equation}
Necessity follows by testing quadratic forms on the nullspace of the uniform Hessian.
For sufficiency, decompose each Hessian along $\mathbf1$ and $\mathbf1^\perp$.
Its Schur complement for $p_0+th$ has the form
$tC-t^2bb^\T/(a_0+ta)$, with $C\preceq0$ and $a_0>0$.
This is negative semidefinite for small $t\ge0$.
The coefficients remain positive, so full support and the Lorentzian characterization prove sufficiency.

Fix $j$ and write
\[
 h(1,\ldots,z,\ldots,1)=\sum_{r=0}^nh_{j,r}z^r,\qquad
 b_r=\binom nr\frac{(n-1)^{n-r}}{n^n},\qquad z_r=h_{j,r}/b_r.
\]
The binary marginal, with the other variables equal, turns \eqref{eq:lp-lorentzian-tangent} into
$2z_r-z_{r-1}-z_{r+1}\ge0$ for $1\le r<n$.
To see the sign directly, apply the mixed derivatives of that marginal to the binary contrast direction $(n-1,-1)$.
Each resulting quadratic form is a positive sum of the forms in \eqref{eq:lp-lorentzian-tangent} evaluated at the contrast vector with entry $n-1$ in position $j$ and $-1$ elsewhere.
Its coefficients, divided by the uniform binomial coefficients, give the stated second difference.
Thus $(z_r)$ is concave.
If $K\sim\operatorname{Bin}(n,1/n)$, normalization and balance imply
\[
 \mathbb Ez_K=0,\qquad \mathbb E(Kz_K)=0,\qquad \mathbb EK=1.
\]
Jensen's inequality gives $z_1\ge0$ and hence $a_j=g_nz_1\ge0$.
If $a_j=0$, equality in Jensen, together with the full support of $K$, forces $(z_r)$ to be affine.
The two moment constraints then force every $z_r$ to vanish.

Suppose all $a_j$ vanish.
All one-coordinate marginals of $h$ vanish, so the diagonal entries of $\nabla^2h(\mathbf1)$ are zero.
Also $\nabla^2h(\mathbf1)\mathbf1=(n-1)\nabla h(\mathbf1)=0$.
The coefficient identity
\[
 \nabla^2h(\mathbf1)=\sum_{|\beta|=n-2}\frac{\operatorname{Hess}(\partial^\beta h)}{\beta!}
\]
makes its projection a positive weighted sum of the negative semidefinite matrices in \eqref{eq:lp-lorentzian-tangent}.
Its trace is zero, so it vanishes, and every summand vanishes.
All second contrast derivatives of $h$ are therefore zero polynomials.
Hence $h=aR^n+R^{n-1}L$, where $L$ is a contrast linear form.
Normalization and balance give $a=L=0$.
The preceding argument proves $\sum_j a_j>0$ for every nonzero tangent.
Compactness on the coefficient unit sphere, followed by Lemma~\ref{lem:lp-variation}, gives the strict average-deletion neighborhood.

The norm statement follows by induction on $n$.
For $n=2$, a normalized balanced binary quadratic is
$p=a(x^2+y^2)+bxy$ with $2a+b=1$.
The Lorentzian condition gives $b\ge2a$, and
$b^2-\tfrac12N(p)=\tfrac12(b^2-4a^2)\ge0$, with equality only at $p_0$.
For the induction step, each normalized balanced deletion
$\widetilde q_j=q_j(D_jx)/q_j(D_j\mathbf1)$ is Lorentzian and converges to the uniform power of degree $m$.
Shrink the neighborhood until the induction hypothesis applies to every deletion.
Since $a(q_j)=a(p)$, it gives $a(p)^2\ge c_mH_j(p)$.
Average these inequalities and use the strict deletion inequality to obtain
$a(p)^2\ge c_m n^{-1}\sum_jH_j(p)>c_mg_nN(p)=c_nN(p)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Why balance alone does not suffice}

Multiplication by $x_j$ embeds $[x_j]p$ as the orthogonal projection onto the monomials with exponent exactly one in coordinate $j$.
Thus
\[
 \sum_jN([x_j]p)=\sum_\alpha k_1(\alpha)\alpha!p_\alpha^2,
 \qquad \sum_jN(\partial_jp)=nN(p).
\]
For the second identity, a monomial with exponent $\alpha$ contributes
$\sum_j\alpha_j\alpha!p_\alpha^2=n\alpha!p_\alpha^2$.
The first operator has zero eigenvalues because some monomials have no exponent equal to one.
It has no positive lower bound on the full coefficient space.

An exact perturbation also rules out the desired inequalities under positivity and balance alone.
Let
\[
 V=(x_1-x_2)(x_2-x_3)(x_3-x_1),\qquad
 p_\varepsilon=R^3+\varepsilon V,\qquad 0<|\varepsilon|<1/9.
\]
Every coefficient is positive, and normalization and balance hold.
Direct coefficient calculation and binary-quadratic balancing give
\[
 a(p_\varepsilon)=\frac29,\quad N(p_\varepsilon)=\frac29+12\varepsilon^2,
 \quad H_j(p_\varepsilon)=\frac8{81}-4\varepsilon^2.
\]
Therefore
\[
 \frac13\sum_jH_j(p_\varepsilon)-g_3N(p_\varepsilon)
 =-\frac{28}{3}\varepsilon^2<0,
\]
and \eqref{eq:lp-target} fails as well.
For a contrast $x$ with $V(x)\ne0$, the restriction is $z^3+\varepsilon V(x)$.
It has nonreal zeros, so this perturbation is not stable.
Theorem~\ref{thm:lp-lorentzian-local} also excludes it from the Lorentzian class for sufficiently small nonzero $\varepsilon$.

\subsection{Subtraction to a pure-coefficient boundary}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:lp-boundary-subtraction}
Let $p$ be a balanced Lorentzian polynomial of degree $n$ in $n$ variables with nonnegative coefficients.
Put $S=\sum_i x_i$ and $\mu=\min_i[x_i^n]p$.
Then $q=p-\mu S^n$ is nonnegative, balanced, and Lorentzian.
It has a zero pure coefficient and may be zero.
Consequently, \eqref{eq:lp-target} holds for the full balanced Lorentzian class if and only if it holds on the subclass with a zero pure coefficient.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $d_\alpha=p_\alpha/\binom n\alpha$.
The two-by-two principal minors of the derivative Hessians give
\[
 d_\alpha^2\ge d_{\alpha-e_i+e_j}d_{\alpha+e_i-e_j}
 \qquad(\alpha_i,\alpha_j>0).
\]
First assume all coefficients are positive.
Among the indices minimizing $d_\alpha$, choose one maximizing $\sum_i\alpha_i^2$.
If two coordinates are positive, the displayed inequality and minimality force both adjacent indices to attain the same minimum.
At least one has a larger squared coordinate sum, a contradiction.
Thus the minimum occurs at a pure index, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-coefficient-minimum}
 p_\alpha\ge\mu\binom n\alpha.
\end{equation}
For a nonzero polynomial with zero coefficients, substitute $((1-\varepsilon)I+\varepsilon J_n)x$ for $x$.
This preserves balance and the Lorentzian property and makes every coefficient positive.
Let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ in \eqref{eq:lp-coefficient-minimum}.
The zero polynomial is immediate.

For $0\le t<\mu$, every coefficient of $p-tS^n$ is positive and
\[
 \operatorname{Hess}(\partial^\beta(p-tS^n))
 =\operatorname{Hess}(\partial^\beta p)-tn!\mathbf1\mathbf1^\T.
\]
Subtracting a positive semidefinite matrix cannot increase the number of positive eigenvalues.
Full support therefore gives the Lorentzian property.
Closedness gives the endpoint $t=\mu$.
Balance is linear, and the coefficient attaining $\mu$ becomes zero.

The set $\{f:a(f)\ge\sqrt{c_n}\sqrt{N(f)}\}$ is a convex cone.
Also $a(S^n)=n!$ and $N(S^n)=n!n^n$, so $S^n$ belongs to its boundary.
If $q$ satisfies the target inequality, the triangle inequality gives
\[
 \sqrt{c_nN(p)}\le\sqrt{c_nN(q)}+\mu\sqrt{c_nN(S^n)}
 \le a(q)+\mu n!=a(p).
\]
The reverse implication is inclusion of the boundary subclass.
\end{proof}

There is also an exact ratio statement.
For normalized nonnegative $q$, put $p_\lambda=(1-\lambda)p_0+\lambda q$,
$u=a(q)/c_n-1$, and $v=N(q)/c_n-1\ge0$.
Orthogonality gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-segment-ratio}
 \mathcal S(p_\lambda)=\frac{(1+\lambda u)^2}{1+\lambda^2v}
 \ge\min\{1,\mathcal S(q)\},\qquad 0\le\lambda\le1.
\end{equation}
Indeed, its derivative has the sign of $u-\lambda v$ whenever $1+\lambda u>0$.
An interior critical point can only be a maximum.
The endpoints with zero numerator follow by continuity.
If a normalized nonuniform $p$ has $\mu>0$, then
\[
 q=\frac{p-\mu S^n}{1-\mu n^n},\qquad
 p=(1-\lambda)p_0+\lambda q,\qquad \lambda=1-\mu n^n.
\]
The denominator is positive by \eqref{eq:lp-coefficient-minimum} and nonuniformity.
Thus a counterexample can be moved to the pure-coefficient boundary with no increase in its ratio.
Along any admissible subtraction direction, a ratio below one strictly decreases as $\lambda$ increases.
There is no interior local minimum below one.

This reduction does not connect the boundary to the local theorem through admissible line segments.
For example, $q=\tfrac14x_1(x_2+x_3)^2$ is normalized, balanced, and stable.
For every $0<\lambda<1$, the Hessian of
$\partial_1((1-\lambda)R^3+\lambda q)$ is a positive sum of two nonparallel positive rank-one matrices.
It has two positive eigenvalues.
Thus the intervening polynomial is not Lorentzian.

\subsection{Cubic stability, deletion, and boundary rigidity}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:lp-cubic-subtraction}
For a balanced nonnegative stable ternary cubic, the subtraction in Theorem~\ref{thm:lp-boundary-subtraction} preserves stability, with zero permitted at the endpoint.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For a contrast vector $y$, write
$p(s\mathbf1+y)=as^3+u(y)s+v(y)$, where $a=p(\mathbf1)>0$.
Balance removes the quadratic coefficient.
Real-rootedness gives $u(y)\le0$ and $-4u(y)^3\ge27a v(y)^2$.
Subtracting $tS^3$ changes $a$ to $a_t=a-27t$ and leaves $u,v$ fixed.
If $a_t>0$, the same discriminant inequality holds with $a_t$.
Thus the residual is hyperbolic in direction $\mathbf1$.
Its coefficients remain nonnegative by Theorem~\ref{thm:lp-boundary-subtraction}, so it is stable.
Closure handles the endpoint.
If $a_t=0$, nonnegative coefficients and zero value at $\mathbf1$ force the residual to be zero.
\end{proof}

For a ternary cubic, write its pure coefficients as $U_i$, its central coefficient as $b$, and its deletions as
$[x_j]p=a_jx_k^2+b x_kx_\ell+c_jx_\ell^2$.
Binary balancing gives $H_j=b^2+4a_jc_j$.
If one of $a_j,c_j$ is zero, this formula denotes the limit along positive diagonals and equals the corresponding infimum.
A coefficient calculation gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-cubic-cone}
 G(p):=3\sum_jH_j-4N(p)
 =5b^2-\sum_j\bigl((a_j+c_j)^2+7(a_j-c_j)^2\bigr)-24\sum_iU_i^2.
\end{equation}
The inequality $G\ge0$ is precisely the average-deletion inequality in degree three.
For $b\ge0$, it defines a convex cone because its right side is five times a linear coordinate squared minus a sum of squares.
The polynomial $S^3$ lies on its boundary.
Theorem~\ref{thm:lp-boundary-subtraction} therefore reduces the Lorentzian cubic deletion problem to zero pure coefficients.
Proposition~\ref{prop:lp-cubic-subtraction} makes the same reduction inside the stable cubic class.
The deletion inequality on that full boundary subclass remains unproved here.

A proposed estimate using only the mixed coefficients would require
$\sum_j((a_j+c_j)^2+7(a_j-c_j)^2)\le3b^2$.
It fails in the Lorentzian class.
Consider
\[
 p(x,y,z)=(y+z)\left(\frac{(y+z)^2}{4\sqrt3}
 +\frac{x(y+z)}2+\frac{x^2}{\sqrt3}\right).
\]
It is balanced, with $p(\mathbf1)=2+4\sqrt3/3$, and $b=1$, $U_x=0$.
Its $y$ and $z$ derivatives are squares of linear forms.
Its $x$ derivative is $(y+z)((y+z)/2+2x/\sqrt3)$.
These quadratics have at most one positive Hessian eigenvalue.
Its support is the degree-three simplex without $(3,0,0)$, which satisfies the exchange property.
Thus it is Lorentzian.
Direct expansion gives
\[
 N(p)=13/3,\quad H_x=H_y=H_z=2,\quad G(p)=2/3,
 \quad \sum_j((a_j+c_j)^2+7(a_j-c_j)^2)=10/3.
\]
The quadratic factor, viewed in $x$ and $y+z$, has discriminant $-1/12$ and is not stable.
This example does not disprove the proposed estimate restricted to stable cubics.
That restricted estimate is also unproved here.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:lp-cubic-rigidity}
Let $p(x,y,z)$ be a nonzero balanced nonnegative stable cubic with zero $x^3$ coefficient.
If $\operatorname{Hess}(\partial_xp)$ is singular, then $p$ factors over the reals.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
If the degree in $x$ is at most one, balance says that the mean $x$-exponent is one.
Every nonzero monomial must therefore have $x$-exponent one, and $p=xq(y,z)$.
Otherwise the degree in $x$ is two.
The derivative $\partial_xp$ is a stable quadratic with singular Hessian and zero $x^2$ coefficient.
Its rank cannot be one because a rank-one quadratic with that coefficient zero is independent of $x$.
Its rank is therefore two, with one positive and one negative eigenvalue, so it factors into real linear forms.
The stable linear factors can be chosen with nonnegative coefficients.
Exactly one depends on $x$.
Thus $\partial_xp=L(y,z)(cx+M(y,z))$, with $c>0$ and nonnegative linear forms $L,M$.
Integration gives
\[
 p=L\left(\frac c2x^2+Mx\right)+r(y,z).
\]
For every real $y,z$ with $L\ne0$, specialization gives a nonzero stable quadratic in $x$.
Its discriminant is $L(LM^2-2cr)\ge0$.
If $r$ did not vanish on the line $L=0$, crossing that line near a point where $r\ne0$ would change the sign of this discriminant.
Therefore $L$ divides $r$, and $p=LQ$.
\end{proof}

Subtraction of a general balanced stable polynomial does not preserve the Lorentzian property.
For an exact example, take
\[
 p=(2y+4z)(2x+3y+z)(4x+y+z),\qquad
 h=\tfrac18(y+z)(2x+y+z)^2.
\]
Both are balanced stable products with zero $x^3$ coefficient.
Their values at $\mathbf1$ are $216$ and $4$.
In the order $(x^2y,x^2z,xy^2,xyz,xz^2,y^3,y^2z,yz^2,z^3)$ their coefficients are
\[
 (16,32,28,68,24,6,20,18,4),\qquad
 (\tfrac12,\tfrac12,\tfrac12,1,\tfrac12,\tfrac18,\tfrac38,\tfrac38,\tfrac18).
\]
All displayed coefficients of $p-h$ are positive, but
\[
 \operatorname{Hess}(\partial_x(p-h))=
 \begin{pmatrix}0&31&63\\31&55&67\\63&67&47\end{pmatrix}
\]
has determinant $-1760$ and trace $102$.
It has two positive eigenvalues, so $p-h$ is not Lorentzian.
The failure occurs for arbitrarily small positive subtraction as well.
For $v=(-11,16,-8)^\T$,
$\operatorname{Hess}(\partial_xp)v=0$ and
$v^\T\operatorname{Hess}(\partial_xh)v=-112$.
The zero eigenvalue therefore becomes positive under small subtraction, while the existing positive eigenvalue remains positive.
This prevents an unrestricted iteration of the uniform-power subtraction argument.

\subsection{An admissible attenuation and a finite support identity}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:lp-attenuation}
Let $p$ be Lorentzian, and let $r$ be its maximum degree in variable $x_i$.
Multiply each coefficient with exponent $\alpha_i=r$ by $\lambda\in[0,1]$.
The resulting polynomial $p_\lambda$ is Lorentzian.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $\lambda>0$, the support is unchanged and is M-convex.
Consider a quadratic derivative $\partial^\beta p$.
If $\beta_i=r-2$, attenuation decreases only its $ii$ Hessian entry.
This subtracts a positive semidefinite diagonal matrix.
If $\beta_i=r-1$, the $ii$ entry is zero and the changed row and column are obtained by diagonal congruence with factor $\lambda$ in position $i$.
If $\beta_i=r$, the Hessian is multiplied by $\lambda$ and its $i$th row and column are zero.
All other Hessians are unchanged or zero.
Each retains at most one positive eigenvalue.
The characterization proves the assertion for $\lambda>0$, and closedness gives $\lambda=0$.
\end{proof}

This operation permits positive diagonal balancing whenever the mean exponent is in the relative interior of the support polytope.
It does not imply monotonicity or convexity of the balanced squared Fischer norm.

For completeness, one support class has an exact entropy reformulation.
Let $S$ be a nonempty M-convex set of degree-$n$ exponent vectors.
Assume $\mathbf1\in S$ and that $\mathbf1$ belongs to the relative interior of $\operatorname{conv}S$.
Define
\[
 p_S(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in S}\frac{x^\alpha}{\alpha!},\qquad
 Q_\alpha=\frac{c_n}{\alpha!},\qquad
 F_S(z)=\sum_{\alpha\in S}Q_\alpha e^{(\alpha-\mathbf1)\cdot z}.
\]
Here $Q$ is the multinomial probability distribution on all degree-$n$ exponent vectors.
The polynomial $p_S$ is Lorentzian by \citet[Theorem~3.10]{brandenhuh2020}.
On the span of the vectors $\alpha-\mathbf1$, the relative-interior assumption makes $F_S$ coercive and strictly convex.
Indeed, every nonzero direction has a positive inner product with some $\alpha-\mathbf1$, and its Hessian is a positive weighted sum of their outer products.
It has a unique minimizer modulo orthogonal redundancies.
Choose a representative $z_*$ with $\sum_i z_{*,i}=0$.
Then $\nabla F_S(z_*)=0$.

The tilted polynomial $q(x)=p_S(e^{z_{*,1}}x_1,\ldots,e^{z_{*,n}}x_n)$ is balanced and has central coefficient one.
Direct substitution gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lp-mconvex-norm}
 c_nN(q)=F_S(2z_*).
\end{equation}
Thus its target inequality is exactly $F_S(2z_*)\le1$.
This last inequality is not proved for arbitrary $S$ here.

To state the entropy identity, put
\[
 P_*(\alpha)=\frac{Q_\alpha\mathbf1_{\{\alpha\in S\}}
 e^{(\alpha-\mathbf1)\cdot z_*}}{F_S(z_*)}.
\]
This distribution has mean $\mathbf1$.
For probability distributions $P,Q$ with $Q>0$, define
$D(P\Vert Q)=\sum_\alpha P_\alpha\log(P_\alpha/Q_\alpha)$ and
$D_2(P\Vert Q)=\log\sum_\alpha P_\alpha^2/Q_\alpha$.
These are the relative entropy and the order-two R\'enyi divergence.
The zero mean of $\alpha-\mathbf1$ gives
\[
 D(P_*\Vert Q)=-\log F_S(z_*)=\log\frac{P_*(\mathbf1)}{Q_{\mathbf1}},
 \qquad
 D_2(P_*\Vert Q)=\log F_S(2z_*)-2\log F_S(z_*).
\]
Therefore \eqref{eq:lp-mconvex-norm} makes the remaining inequality equivalent to
$D_2(P_*\Vert Q)\le2D(P_*\Vert Q)$.
These are exact identities, not a proof of that entropy inequality.
If the mean lies on a proper face, the same identities apply after restriction to the minimal face containing it, provided the central exponent remains in that face.
The interior argument then takes place in its affine span.


\subsection{A multivariate obstruction to two-sided scalar localization}
\label{sec:multivariate-localization-obstruction}

The scalar localization proof uses two-sided linear coefficient perturbations.
At an active binary log-concavity equality, its linear constraint makes the quadratic error nonnegative.
This implication fails for multivariate derivative Hessians.
The following example also shows that binary coefficient inequalities and coordinate marginals do not replace Lorentzianity.

First consider a real symmetric matrix with one positive eigenvalue, $r$ negative eigenvalues, and a nontrivial kernel.
In orthonormal range and kernel coordinates, write it and a perturbation as
\[
 H=\begin{pmatrix}D&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 K=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\B^\T&C\end{pmatrix},
\]
where $D$ is invertible and has inertia $(1,r)$.
For sufficiently small $\varepsilon$, the range block $D+\varepsilon A$ retains that inertia.
Its kernel Schur complement in $H+\varepsilon K$ is
\[
 \varepsilon C-\varepsilon^2B^\T(D+\varepsilon A)^{-1}B.
\]
If $C$ has a nonzero eigenvalue, one sign of $\varepsilon$ makes this complement positive in a direction.
That direction supplies a second positive eigenvalue of the full matrix.
Thus preservation of at most one positive eigenvalue for both signs requires $C=0$.
Under this necessary condition, the exact remaining condition is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mv-schur-condition}
 B^\T(D+\varepsilon A)^{-1}B\succeq0.
\end{equation}
In particular, $B^\T D^{-1}B\succeq0$ is necessary.
It is sufficient for both small signs when
$\ker(B^\T D^{-1}B)=\ker B$.
Indeed, the form is positive definite on the orthogonal complement of $\ker B$, remains positive there by continuity, and vanishes on $\ker B$ for every $\varepsilon$.
The simpler condition $K(\ker H)=0$ is also sufficient.
When $D$ has only a positive eigenvalue, these conditions explain the favorable binary behavior.
When $D$ also has a negative direction, the linear condition $C=0$ does not control the sign in~\eqref{eq:mv-schur-condition}.

Now set
\[
 p_0=(x+y)(y+z)(z+x)
 =x^2y+x^2z+xy^2+xz^2+y^2z+yz^2+2xyz.
\]
This is a stable, and hence Lorentzian, product polynomial.
It is balanced because $p_0(\mathbf1)=8$ and each first derivative at $\mathbf1$ is $8$.
Moreover,
\[
 \frac{p_0}{8}=p_A,\qquad
 A=\frac12\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\0&1&1\\1&0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\Omega_3.
\]
The polynomial cannot be expressed in one variable and the sum of the other two variables.
For example, at $x=0$ it equals $yz(y+z)$, which is not determined by $y+z$ alone.
Symmetry gives the same conclusion for the other choices of special variable.

Suppose $p_0\pm\varepsilon h$ is nonnegative, balanced, Lorentzian, and has central coefficient $2$ for both signs of every sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$.
The zero pure coefficients of $p_0$ force the pure coefficients of $h$ to vanish.
Its central coefficient also vanishes. Thus
\[
 h=A_1x^2y+B_1x^2z+C_1xy^2+D_1xz^2+E_1y^2z+F_1yz^2.
\]
The kernels of the three derivative Hessians of $p_0$ are spanned, respectively, by
$(0,1,-1)$, $(1,0,-1)$, and $(1,-1,0)$.
The necessary zero kernel-compression conditions give
\[
 C_1+D_1=0,\qquad A_1+F_1=0,\qquad B_1+E_1=0.
\]
They imply $h(\mathbf1)=0$.
The balance equations for $x$ and $y$ then give
$A_1+B_1=0$ and $C_1-B_1=0$.
Consequently every possible direction is a scalar multiple of
\[
 V=(x-y)(x-z)(y-z),\qquad
 (A_1,B_1,C_1,D_1,E_1,F_1)=a(1,-1,-1,1,1,-1).
\]
For $p_t=p_0+tV$, direct differentiation gives
\[
 H_x(t)=\operatorname{Hess}(\partial_xp_t)
 =\begin{pmatrix}
 0&2(1+t)&2(1-t)\\
 2(1+t)&2(1-t)&2\\
 2(1-t)&2&2(1+t)
 \end{pmatrix},
\]
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mv-determinant}
 \det H_x(t)=-64t^2,\qquad \operatorname{tr}H_x(t)=4.
\end{equation}
For $t\ne0$, the negative determinant and positive trace force exactly two positive eigenvalues and one negative eigenvalue.
Thus no nonzero supported two-sided direction is feasible.
The other two derivative Hessians have the same determinant and trace.
In the basis $e_1,e_2+e_3,e_2-e_3$, the displayed Hessian is congruent to
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}0&4&4t\\4&8&-4t\\4t&-4t&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Its range block is $D=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&4\\4&8\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ and its coupling vector is $b=(4,-4)^\T$.
Since $b^\T D^{-1}b=-16$, the kernel Schur complement is $16t^2$.
This gives the exact quadratic failure that the scalar dimension count misses.

For $|t|<1$, all seven supported coefficients of $p_t$ are positive.
The polynomial remains balanced and has central coefficient $2$.
Its three coordinate marginals are unchanged:
\[
 p_t(x,1,1)=2+4x+2x^2,
\]
with the analogous identities in $y$ and $z$.
Each $2\times2$ principal minor of each derivative Hessian is one of
\[
 -4(1+t)^2,\qquad -4(1-t)^2,\qquad -4t^2.
\]
Thus every binary coefficient-line log-concavity condition holds.
The coordinate probability marginals are also ultra-log-concave.
Nevertheless, the full Hessian condition fails by~\eqref{eq:mv-determinant}.
The squared Fischer norm is
\[
 N(p_t)=16+12t^2.
\]
At $t=1/2$, the normalized polynomial $P=p_t/8$ satisfies
\[
 [xyz]P=\frac14,\qquad N(P)=\frac{19}{64},\qquad
 c_3N(P)-([xyz]P)^2=\frac1{288}>0.
\]
This refutes an extension of the norm bound based only on balance, marginal ultra-log-concavity, and binary coefficient inequalities.
It is neither a Lorentzian counterexample nor a Gyires counterexample.

The rigidity assertion concerns two-sided linear perturbations on the original support.
It does not make $p_0$ a global norm maximizer at fixed central coefficient.
Indeed, consider the one-sided path
\[
 q_s=p_0+\frac{s}{3}(x^3+y^3+z^3),\qquad 0\le s\le1.
\]
It remains balanced and has central coefficient $2$.
The derivative Hessians have a zero contrast direction.
On the complementary two-dimensional subspace, their congruent block is
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2s&4\\4&8\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, with determinant $16(s-1)\le0$ and positive trace.
Their full signatures therefore have at most one positive eigenvalue.
For $s>0$ the coefficient support is the full degree-three simplex; at $s=0$ the polynomial is the stable product already considered.
The Lorentzian criterion thus applies along the whole path.
Also,
\[
 N(q_s)=16+2s^2,\qquad
 q_1=\frac{(x+y+z)^3}{3},\qquad
 \frac{([xyz]p_0)^2}{c_3N(p_0)}=\frac98.
\]
The example excludes the direct claim that every non-binary input can be removed by a supported two-sided perturbation.
A broader localization proof must also treat changes of support or compare the norms at other rigid points.


\section{Exact expectations in a regular bipartite configuration
model}\label{sec:configuration}

Let \(d\ge2\) and \(n\ge1\). Give each of \(n\) row vertices and \(n\)
column vertices exactly \(d\) labeled stubs, and choose a uniformly
random bijection between the \(dn\) row stubs and the \(dn\) column
stubs. Let \(G\) be the resulting \(n\times n\) nonnegative integer
adjacency matrix, with parallel edges counted. Then \(G/d\) is doubly
stochastic in every realization.

Define

\[
F_d(x,y)=
\sum_{\substack{k,r\ge0\\k+2r\le d}}
\frac{d!(k+r)!}{k!(r!)^2(d-k-2r)!}\,x^ky^r.
\]

The expected Gram permanent is

\begin{equation}\label{eq:rg-2}
\boxed{
E\operatorname{per}(GG^T)
=\frac{n!d^n}{(dn)!}
\sum_{t=0}^{n}
(d-1)^{n-t}\bigl((d-2)n+t\bigr)!(n-t)!
[x^ty^{n-t}]F_d(x,y)^n.
}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Counting proof}\label{configuration-counting-proof}

Expand the Gram permanent as a pair of edge choices, colored red and
blue, with one red and one blue edge at every row. At a column, the red
edges and blue edges are paired; if there are \(\ell\) of each color,
this contributes \(\ell!\).

Let \(t\) be the number of row vertices that use the same edge for both
colors. These contribute one stub of type RB each; every other row
contributes distinct R-only and B-only stubs. The number of ways to
color the row stubs is

\[
\binom nt d^t\bigl(d(d-1)\bigr)^{n-t}.
\]

At a column, suppose \(k\) stubs are RB, \(r\) are R-only, and \(r\) are
B-only. The remaining \(d-k-2r\) stubs are unused. There are

\[
\frac{d!}{k!(r!)^2(d-k-2r)!}
\]

such stub colorings and \((k+r)!\) pairings of the colored edges,
producing the corresponding term of \(F_d\). Across all columns, the
prescribed color totals are \(t,n-t,n-t,(d-2)n+t\).

For any fixed row and column colorings with these totals, the
probability that a uniform stub bijection respects all four colors is

\[
\frac{t!\,(n-t)!^2\,((d-2)n+t)!}{(dn)!}.
\]

Multiplying and cancelling factorials gives \eqref{eq:rg-2}.

For illustration,

\[
F_2(x,y)=1+2x+2x^2+2y,
\]

\[
F_3(x,y)=1+3x+6x^2+6x^3+6y+12xy.
\]

\subsection{The second moment}\label{configuration-the-second-moment}

For two perfect matchings, each column must receive exactly one red and
one blue edge. Thus its local possibilities are RB or R-only plus
B-only. Replace \(F_d\) in \eqref{eq:rg-2} by

\[
L_d(x,y)=dx+d(d-1)y.
\]

The result is exactly \(E\operatorname{per}(G)^2\). In particular, if

\[
w_t=(d-1)^{n-t}((d-2)n+t)!(n-t)!,
\]

then

\begin{equation}\label{eq:rg-3}
\frac{c_nE\operatorname{per}(GG^T)}{E\operatorname{per}(G)^2}
=\frac{n!}{n^n}
\frac{\sum_t w_t[x^ty^{n-t}]F_d(x,y)^n}
     {\sum_t w_t\binom nt d^n(d-1)^{n-t}}.
\end{equation}

Every quantity in \eqref{eq:rg-3} is an explicitly computable integer before the
final division.

These are exact finite identities. A violation of the ratio bound for
one pair of parameters would imply a realization that violates the
strong inequality. No such violation is established here. The identities
do not assert the unrestricted Gyires conjecture.


\section{Exact limits of stronger proof steps}\label{sec:obstructions}

The preceding proofs use specific matrix or polynomial structure.
This section gives exact counterexamples to several stronger statements.
Each counterexample concerns an auxiliary statement.
None is a counterexample to Gyires's inequality.
The squared Fischer norm remains
$N(p)=\sum_\alpha\alpha!p_\alpha^2$. Put
$g_n=((n-1)/n)^{n-1}=c_n/c_{n-1}$.
For a singleton deletion $q_j=[x_j]p$, let $H(q_j)$ be its squared Fischer norm after
capacity balancing with determinant one.
A balanced limit is used when a finite positive scaling does not exist.
Thus, at a positive capacity-balancing diagonal matrix $D$,
\[
 H(q_j)=\frac{N(q_j(Dx))}{(\det D)^2}.
\]

\subsection{Negative coefficients in a general Birkhoff sorting chamber}

The sorted-gap proof for circulants does not extend to arbitrary lists of
permutation matrices by the same coefficient-sign assertion.
Let $E$ be the order-four all-ones matrix, and define
\[
 A(t,u,v)=6tE+u(3E-2I)+vI,\qquad t,u,v\ge0.
\]
The sum of all $24$ order-four permutation matrices is $6E$.
The identity and the nine derangements sum to $3E-2I$.
Indeed, each off-diagonal position occurs in three derangements.
These subsets are nested, and the last subset can be the identity alone.
Thus $t,u,v$ are three gap variables in an actual sorting chamber for
Birkhoff coefficients, with all other gaps set to zero.
Nevertheless,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-birkhoff}
 [t^6uv]\bigl(4^4\per(A)^2-4!\per(AA^\T)\bigr)=-6879707136.
\end{equation}

The coefficient can be computed without expanding the full permanent.
Put $H=I-E/4$, so $\|H\|_F^2=3$.
For an order-four matrix $X$ with zero row and column sums,
\[
 \per(aE+X)=24a^4+a^2\|X\|_F^2+O(X^3).
\]
The linear term vanishes.
For the quadratic term, summing products from two distinct rows and columns
and using the zero sums gives $\|X\|_F^2/2$ before the factor $2!a^2$.
Also
\[
 (aE+X)(aE+X)^\T=4a^2E+XX^\T.
\]
The term linear in $XX^\T$ in its permanent vanishes, since the sum of the
entries of $XX^\T$ is zero.
The quadratic part in $X$ of the defect is therefore
$256\cdot24\cdot2\,a^6\|X\|_F^2$.
In the displayed family, $X=(-2u+v)H$.
For the term of total degree two in $u,v$, the scalar part can be replaced
by $a=6t$.
Its contribution is
\[
 256\cdot24\cdot2\cdot6^6\cdot(-12)=-6879707136,
\]
which proves~\eqref{eq:obs-birkhoff}.
The exact full expansion has twelve negative coefficients.
A negative coefficient does not imply a negative value on the nonnegative
orthant. It excludes this coefficientwise proof step only.

\subsection{A zero-containing column need not be a useful deletion}

The condition $[x_j^n]p=0$ does not imply $H([x_j]p)\ge g_nN(p)$.
It also does not suffice to choose the best column among those with this
vanishing coefficient.
Both failures occur for products of doubly stochastic row forms.

First let $A=M/8$, where
\[
 M=\begin{pmatrix}
 0&0&5&3\\3&0&0&5\\2&3&3&0\\3&5&0&0
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad p_M(x)=\prod_{i=1}^4(Mx)_i.
\]
Every row and column sum of $M$ is eight.
Working with $M$ instead of $A$ multiplies both squared norms by $8^8$.
It does not change their ratio.
Direct coefficient expansion gives
\[
 N(p_M)=2276500,\qquad
 q_1(y,z,w)=[x_1]p_M=(5z+3w)(45y^2+45yz+95yw+45zw).
\]
Let $t$ be the unique positive root of
\[
 f(t)=19t^4+9t^3-15t-15=0.
\]
Descartes' rule gives at most one positive root, and exact endpoint
substitution gives
\[
 t\in I:=\left[\frac{1021945}{10^6},\frac{1021946}{10^6}\right].
\]
The signs at the two endpoints are opposite, so this root exists.
The scale $D=(t,1,t^2)$ capacity-balances $q_1$.
To check this, write its quadratic factor as $Q$.
The equation $yQ_y=Q$ at $D$ reduces to $45t^2=45t^2$.
The expression $w(q_1)_w-q_1$ at $D$ equals $15t f(t)$.
Homogeneity gives the remaining balance equation.
The function
\[
 (z_1,z_2,z_3)\longmapsto
 \log q_1(e^{z_1},e^{z_2},e^{z_3})-z_1-z_2-z_3
\]
is convex. Its stationary point is therefore a global capacity minimum.
The support spans the degree-three affine hyperplane, so the scale is
unique modulo common multiplication.

Expanding the norm at this scale gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-zero-laurent}
