 H(q_1)=101250t^{-4}+202500t^{-2}+372100+72900t^2+162450t^4.
\end{equation}
All coefficients are positive. Use the lower endpoint of $I$ for negative
powers and the upper endpoint for positive powers to obtain an upper bound.
The resulting rational comparison gives
\[
 \frac{H(q_1)}{(27/64)N(p_M)}<\frac{95}{100}<1.
\]
This proves the prescribed-column failure.
The corresponding lower interval bound also gives $H(q_1)>911200=N(q_1)$.
Thus capacity balancing can increase this individual gauge-normalized norm.

For a failure with only one eligible column, use
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-one-zero-matrix}
 A'=\frac1{7200}\begin{pmatrix}
 0&24&4479&2697\\
 2697&16&16&4471\\
 1806&2689&2689&16\\
 2697&4471&16&16
 \end{pmatrix}=\frac{M'}{7200}.
\end{equation}
This matrix is doubly stochastic, and its only zero entry is the first
entry of the first row.
Its product polynomial has only one zero degree-four coefficient,
namely $[x_1^4]p_{M'}$.
For any other monomial, assign one occurrence of another variable to the
first row. All remaining rows have positive entries.

The following rational interval calculation establishes a capacity-balancing scale and bounds the resulting squared Fischer norm.
Put $q=[x_1]p_{M'}$, and use the gauge $D=(y,z,1)$.
For $q=\sum_{a+b+c=3}q_{abc}y^az^bw^c$, its ten coefficients, in the
order indicated, are
\[
\begin{array}{c|r@{\qquad}c|r}
(a,b,c)&q_{abc}&(a,b,c)&q_{abc}\\\hline
(0,0,3)&870637556880&(0,1,2)&89561760156999\\
(0,2,1)&146964165788097&(0,3,0)&1041518854368\\
(1,0,2)&185659808813829&(1,1,1)&397840876407234\\
(1,2,0)&147375866123649&(2,0,1)&89762967708687\\
(2,1,0)&147118426392969&(3,0,0)&784079123688
\end{array}
\]
and
\[
 N(p_{M'})=966483494962008824960673644628.
\]
Define
\[
 F_1(y,z)=yq_y(y,z,1)-q(y,z,1),\qquad
 F_2(y,z)=zq_z(y,z,1)-q(y,z,1).
\]
Take the rational box
\[
 X=\left[\frac{9789}{10000},\frac{9791}{10000}\right]
 \times\left[\frac{9584}{10000},\frac{9586}{10000}\right],\qquad
 c=\left(\frac{979}{1000},\frac{1917}{2000}\right),
 \qquad R=F'(c)^{-1}.
\]
The determinant defining $R$ is nonzero.
Evaluate the derivatives as interval polynomials on $X$ to obtain an
interval Jacobian $J(X)$.
Ordinary rational interval arithmetic gives
\[
 c-RF(c)+(I-RJ(X))(X-c)
 \subset
 \left[\frac{9789782}{10^7},\frac{9789784}{10^7}\right]
 \times
 \left[\frac{9584771}{10^7},\frac{9584773}{10^7}\right]
 \subset\operatorname{int}X.
\]
The mean-value formula therefore shows that $x\mapsto x-RF(x)$ maps $X$
into itself. Brouwer's fixed-point theorem gives a zero of $F$ in $X$.
Convexity of the logarithmic capacity objective makes it a capacity
minimum. Positivity of all ten coefficients gives uniqueness up to a
common scale.

For every $(y,z)\in X$, evaluate
\[
 \frac{N(q(yY,zZ,W))}{(yz)^2}
 =\sum_{a+b+c=3}a!b!c!q_{abc}^2y^{2a-2}z^{2b-2}
\]
with rational interval arithmetic.
The upper endpoint gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-one-zero-bound}
 \frac{H(q)}{(27/64)N(p_{M'})}<\frac{956}{1000}<1.
\end{equation}
The stated coefficients, box, and interval rules specify the certificate
completely. The accompanying checker uses integers and rational numbers
for every decisive comparison.
Since column one is the only zero-containing column,
\eqref{eq:obs-one-zero-bound} also excludes a maximum restricted to such
columns.
For comparison, direct integer permanent evaluation gives the row strong deficit
$\per(M')^2-c_4\per(M'M'^\T)$, the column strong deficit
$\per(M')^2-c_4\per(M'^\T M')$, and the Gyires deficit $\Delta_4(M')$:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \text{Row strong deficit}&=\frac{541356282302301691357955012577}{8},\\
 \text{Column strong deficit}&=\frac{540961461646503266054750633505}{8},\\
 \text{Gyires deficit}&=299098419452330888771758615932.
 \end{aligned}
\]
All three are positive. No maximum over all columns is refuted.

\subsection{The ultra-log-concave class does not satisfy the stronger contraction}

Use the binomial-noise operator from the rank-two reduction.
Set $n=4$, $t=2$, $B=\operatorname{Bin}(4,1/2)$, and
\[
 P=\delta_2,\qquad Q=(0,2/7,3/7,2/7,0),\qquad r=P/B,\quad s=Q/B.
\]
Both laws have mean two and log-concave likelihood ratios.
Indeed, $s$ is constant on $\{1,2,3\}$ and zero elsewhere; $r$ has singleton
support. Their cross term and variance parameter are
\[
 \langle r,s\rangle_B=\frac87,\qquad
 \kappa(s)=1-\operatorname{Var}(Q)=\frac37.
\]
For completeness, condition on two ones among the four initial bits.
At retention parameter $\kappa$, each initial one remains one with
probability $(1+\kappa)/2$, and each initial zero becomes one with
probability $(1-\kappa)/2$.
The probability of two final ones is
$(3+2\kappa^2+3\kappa^4)/8$.
Division by $B(2)=3/8$ therefore gives
\[
 \langle r,T_\kappa r\rangle_B=1+\frac23\kappa^2+\kappa^4.
\]
At $\kappa=3/7$ this is
\[
 \frac{2776}{2401}=\frac87+\frac{32}{2401}>\langle r,s\rangle_B.
\]
Thus the stronger contraction does not hold for all ultra-log-concave laws.
The strong scalar inequality~\eqref{eq:r2-two-law-target} still holds: $2776/2401<64/49$.
The generating polynomial of $Q$ is $z(2+3z+2z^2)/7$.
Its quadratic factor has discriminant $9-16<0$.
It is therefore not a Poisson-binomial law, whose generating polynomial
is a product of real linear factors.

\subsection{Stochastic complements require more than a pivot sign}

For a doubly stochastic matrix $A$ and a pivot $a_{ij}<1$, define
\[
 B^{ij}_{kl}=a_{kl}+\frac{a_{kj}a_{il}}{1-a_{ij}},
 \qquad k\ne i,\quad l\ne j.
\]
This stochastic complement is doubly stochastic.
For example, its row sum is
$1-a_{kj}+a_{kj}(1-a_{ij})/(1-a_{ij})=1$.
The column calculation is the same, and all entries are nonnegative.
Put
\[
 R(A)=\frac{\per(A)^2}{\per(AA^\T)},\qquad
 Q_{ij}(A)=\frac{g_nR(B^{ij})}{R(A)}.
\]
A universal bound $\min_{a_{ij}<1}Q_{ij}(A)\le1$ would prove the strong
inequality by induction, because $c_n=g_nc_{n-1}$.
This bound remains unproved here.
The following exact examples exclude selection by positive entries alone,
selection by zero entries alone, and two natural averages.

For $n=m+1\ge3$, use the symmetric star matrix
\[
 S_n=\frac1m\begin{pmatrix}
 0&1&1&\cdots&1\\
 1&m-1&0&\cdots&0\\
 1&0&m-1&\cdots&0\\
 \vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
 1&0&0&\cdots&m-1
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
A contributing permutation exchanges the center with one leaf and fixes
all other leaves. All $m$ contributions have the same weight. Hence
\[
 \per(S_n)=\frac{(m-1)^{m-1}}{m^m}.
\]
The perfect-matching marginal
$\pi_{ij}=a_{ij}\per(A_{\widehat i,\widehat j})/\per(A)$ equals $a_{ij}$.
A center edge occurs in one of the $m$ matchings, and a leaf diagonal
occurs in $m-1$ of them.
Every positive pivot gives, up to independent row and column permutations,
\[
 T_m=\frac1m\begin{pmatrix}
 1&1&1&\cdots&1\\
 1&m-1&0&\cdots&0\\
 1&0&m-1&\cdots&0\\
 \vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
 1&0&0&\cdots&m-1
 \end{pmatrix}
\]
of order $m$.
For a leaf diagonal pivot, the update puts $1/m$ in the center diagonal.
For a center-edge pivot, the affected leaf row becomes the uniform row.
This proves the assertion for both positive-pivot types.

At $n=6$, exact permanent sums give
\[
\begin{array}{c|cc}
 &S_6&T_5\\\hline
 \per&256/3125&512/3125\\
 \per(\,cdot\,\cdot^\T)&3222392/48828125&40856/390625\\
 R&40960/402799&32768/127675
\end{array}
\]
and every positive pivot has
\[
 Q_{ij}(S_6)=\frac{10069975}{9928008}>1.
\]
Thus no positive pivot works, including a pivot of maximum entry.
Since the positive entries sum to $n$ and $\pi_{ij}=a_{ij}$, both averages
\[
 \frac1n\sum_{ij}a_{ij}Q_{ij},\qquad
 \frac1n\sum_{ij}\pi_{ij}Q_{ij}
\]
have this same value, greater than one.
A zero pivot between distinct leaves does work:
$Q_{12}(S_6)=53959742125/81341812224<1$, with the center indexed by zero.
The center zero pivot instead has ratio
$4412398635249631/1254003292274688>1$.
These numbers are finite rational permanent evaluations; the checker
computes each sum by subset dynamic programming.
Also $R(S_6)>c_6=5/324$.

Conversely, for $S_3$, one has
$\per(S_3)=\per(S_3S_3^\T)=1/4$.
All positive pivots have ratio $8/9$, whereas all zero pivots have ratio
$200/153>1$.
For a zero pivot, the complement is a relabeling of
$\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}3/4&1/4\\1/4&3/4\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$.
Its permanent ratio is $25/34$; multiplication by $g_3/R(S_3)=16/9$
gives $200/153$.
Thus a useful pivot, if it always exists, must be allowed to come from
either support class.

\subsection{Averaging minimum norm scales does not replace capacity balancing}

For a nonnegative degree-$m$ polynomial in $m$ variables, define
\[
 \mathcal C_F(q)=\inf_{D>0}\frac{N(q(Dx))}{(\det D)^2}.
\]
Then $\mathcal C_F(q)\le H(q)$.
The proposed sufficient deletion bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-fock-false}
 \sum_j\mathcal C_F([x_j]p)\ge ng_nN(p)
\end{equation}
is false, even for a rank-two doubly stochastic product polynomial.
This does not exclude the analogous bound with $H$.

Let $n\ge3$, $0<a<1$, $b=1-a$, and set
\[
 u=(0,a,b/(n-2),\ldots,b/(n-2)),\qquad
 A_{i1}=u_i,\qquad A_{ij}=\frac{1-u_i}{n-1}\quad(j\ge2).
\]
This matrix is doubly stochastic and has rank two.
With $Y=x_2+\cdots+x_n$, its product polynomial is
\[
 p=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}P_k x_1^k\left(\frac{Y}{n-1}\right)^{n-k},\qquad
 P=\operatorname{Bern}(a)+\operatorname{Bin}(n-2,b/(n-2)).
\]
Write $B=\operatorname{Bin}(n,1/n)$ and
$C_n(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(P_k^2/B_k)z^k$.
Expansion of the powers of $Y$ gives $N(p)=c_nC_n(1)$.
The special deletion is
$q_1=P_1(Y/(n-1))^{n-1}$, so the identity scale gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-special-trial}
 \frac{\mathcal C_F(q_1)}{g_nN(p)}
 \le\frac{P_1^2}{g_n^2C_n(1)}.
\end{equation}
For an ordinary column $j\ge2$, put
$Z=\sum_{i\ge2,\,i\ne j}x_i$. Then
\[
 q_j=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{(n-k)P_k}{(n-1)^{n-k}}x_1^kZ^{n-1-k}.
\]
At the explicit trial scale $x_1\mapsto\sqrt z\,x_1$, the squared
scaling determinant is $z$.
The multinomial expansion of each power of $Z$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-ordinary-trial}
 \frac{\mathcal C_F(q_j)}{g_nN(p)}
 \le\frac1{g_nC_n(1)}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{P_k^2}{B_k}
 \frac{n-k}{n-1}\left(\frac{n-2}{n-1}\right)^{n-1-k}z^{k-1}.
\end{equation}
No optimizing scale is needed for these upper bounds.

Take $n=700$, $a=7/10$, and $z=9/10$.
The following integer formulas give a reproducible finite certificate.
Set $M=10(n-2)$, $L=M-3$, and
\[
 C_k=\binom{n-2}{k}3^kL^{n-2-k},\quad
 I_k=3C_k+7C_{k-1},\quad D=10M^{n-2},
\]
where out-of-range coefficients are zero.
Thus $P_k=I_k/D$ and $\sum I_k=\sum kI_k=D$.
Define
\[
 T_k=I_k^2k!(n-k)!,\qquad W=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}T_k(n-1)^k.
\]
Then $N(p)=W/[D^2(n-1)^n]$.
The special and ordinary trial norms divided by $N(p)$ are, respectively,
\[
 S=\frac{I_1^2(n-1)!(n-1)}W,
\]
\[
 O=\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}
 T_k(n-k)(n-2)^{n-1-k}(n-1)^{2k}9^k10^{n-1-k}}
 {(n-1)^n\,9\,10^{n-2}\,W}.
\]
Exact rational comparison gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-700}
 \frac{\sum_j\mathcal C_F(q_j)}{ng_nN(p)}
 \le\frac{S+(n-1)O}{ng_n}<\frac{99996}{100000}<1.
\end{equation}
This establishes the finite counterexample to~\eqref{eq:obs-fock-false}.
The script checks the mass, mean, and strict rational comparison directly.
The decimal value $0.9999594712323396\ldots$ is only a description of the
exact ratio.

The same failure occurs in all sufficiently large orders of this family.
For fixed $a,b$, the law $P$ tends to $\operatorname{Bern}(a)+\operatorname{Pois}(b)$.
The reference $B$ tends to $\operatorname{Pois}(1)$.
The limiting collision series is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:obs-collision-limit}
 C_\infty(z)=e^{1-2b+b^2z}\bigl[b^2(1+az)^2+a^2z\bigr].
\end{equation}
To verify it, the limiting mass at $k\ge1$ is
$e^{-b}b^{k-1}(b^2+ak)/k!$, with the same formula interpreted at $k=0$.
Squaring, dividing by $e^{-1}/k!$, and using the exponential series and
its first two derivatives gives~\eqref{eq:obs-collision-limit}.
The ordinary trial ratio tends to
$C_\infty(z)/(zC_\infty(1))$.
The special ratio is bounded by $e^2$, since $P_1\le1$,
$C_n(1)\ge1$, and $g_n>e^{-1}$.
Its contribution to the average therefore tends to zero.
At $a=7/10$ and $z=9/10$, the limiting average is
\[
 e^{-9/1000}\frac{75569}{75010}<1.
\]
Indeed, $75569/75010=1+559/75010<1+9/1000<e^{9/1000}$.

Here are bounds that justify passage to the collision sums.
For $k\ge1$,
\[
 P_{n,k}\le\frac{b^{k-1}(b^2+ak)}{k!},\qquad
 B_{n,k}^{-1}\le e^{k+2}k!.
\]
The first follows by deleting factors at most one from each binomial mass.
For the second, use
$\binom nk\ge(n/k)^k$, $k^k/k!\le e^k$, and
$(1-1/n)^{-n}\le e^2$ for $n\ge3$.
The $k=0$ terms are bounded separately.
The remaining factor in~\eqref{eq:obs-ordinary-trial}, after division by
$g_n$ and omission of $z^{k-1}$, is at most $3e/2$.
These bounds give a summable factorial majorant at the fixed positive
trial scale. Dominated convergence applies.
The failure concerns the average minimum norm only.
It leaves open both the maximum-column minimum-norm bound and the
capacity-balanced deletion bounds.

\subsection{Admissible attenuation need not improve the balanced norm}

Proposition~\ref{prop:lp-attenuation} proves that attenuation of the highest
coordinate layer preserves Lorentzianity.
The balanced norm is nevertheless neither monotone nor convex along all
such paths.

For monotonicity, take $B=\operatorname{Bin}(3,1/3)=(8,12,6,1)/27$ and
attenuate its last likelihood coefficient, giving $(1,1,1,\lambda)$.
The positive balancing tilt $t$ has mean one exactly when
\[
 6t^2+2\lambda t^3=8.
\]
Normalize the central likelihood to one.
Its squared likelihood norm is
\[
 \Phi(\lambda)=\frac8{27t^2}+\frac{12}{27}
 +\frac{6t^2}{27}+\frac{\lambda^2t^4}{27}.
\]
At zero, implicit differentiation gives
\[
 t=\frac2{\sqrt3},\qquad t'(0)=-\frac29,\qquad
 \Phi(0)=\frac{26}{27},\qquad
 \Phi'(0)=-\frac4{81\sqrt3}<0.
\]
At $\lambda=1$, one has $t=1$ and $\Phi(1)=1$.
Thus filling this coefficient first decreases the balanced norm.
This excludes continuous coefficient-filling monotonicity.
It does not exclude discrete inclusion monotonicity for supports.

For convexity, use the three-variable polynomial
\[
 p_\lambda(x,y,z)=\frac{y^3+3y^2z+3yz^2}{6}
 +\frac{x(y+z)^2}{2}+\lambda\frac{x^2(y+z)}2,
 \qquad0\le\lambda\le1.
\]
At one, this is the exponential-generating polynomial on the cubic
compositions other than $(3,0,0)$ and $(0,0,3)$.
The support is M-convex: it is the set of nonnegative integer vectors of
sum three subject to $\alpha_x,\alpha_z\le2$; a transfer from an excess
coordinate to a deficient coordinate preserves these upper bounds.
The polynomial is therefore Lorentzian, and attenuation preserves this
property for all $\lambda$.
Its $xyz$ coefficient is one.
Let $G(\lambda)=c_3N(\widehat p_\lambda)$, where the balanced scale preserves
this central coefficient.
At zero, use the balanced limit $x(y+z)^2/2$, which gives $G(0)=4/9$.

Set $y=1$, $z=t$, $x=u$, and
\[
 R(t)=\frac{1+3t+3t^2}{6},\qquad
 Q(t)=\frac{(1+t)^2}{2},\qquad L(t)=\frac{1+t}{2}.
\]
The capacity objective is
$R(t)/(ut)+Q(t)/t+\lambda uL(t)/t$.
For $\lambda>0$, minimization in $u$ gives $u^2=R/(\lambda L)$.
With $\varepsilon=\sqrt\lambda$, the remaining objective is
\[
 \frac{Q(t)}t+2\varepsilon\frac{\sqrt{R(t)L(t)}}t.
\]
At $\varepsilon=0$, its unique minimum is $t=1$,
with $(Q/t)''(1)=1$.
The implicit function theorem gives
\[
 t=1+k\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^2),\qquad
 k=\frac3{14}\sqrt{\frac76},\qquad k^2=\frac3{56},
\]
since $[2\sqrt{RL}/t]'(1)=-(3/14)\sqrt{7/6}$.
This stationary point is the capacity minimum by convexity in logarithmic
scale coordinates.
After dividing the scaled polynomial by $ut$, direct coefficient expansion
gives
\[
 N=1+\frac{t^2+t^{-2}}2+\lambda H(t),
\]
where
\[
 H(t)=\frac{L(t)}{R(t)}\left(\frac1{6t^2}+\frac12+\frac{t^2}2\right)
 +\frac{R(t)}{2L(t)}(t^{-2}+1),\qquad H(1)=\frac{13}6.
\]
Therefore
\[
 G'(0+)=\frac29\left(2k^2+\frac{13}6\right)=\frac{191}{378}.
\]
At $\lambda=1$, symmetry makes the scales for $x,z$ equal.
Their ratio $t$ to the $y$ scale solves
$3t^3-3t-1=0$, with $9/8<t<8/7$.
The endpoint norm is
\[
 G(1)=\frac29\left(t^2+2+t^{-2}+\frac1{6t^4}\right).
\]
The rational enclosure gives
\[
 t^2+2+t^{-2}+\frac1{6t^4}
 <2+\frac{64}{49}+\frac{64}{81}+\frac{2048}{19683}
 <\frac{359}{84}.
\]
Thus $G(1)<359/378=G(0)+G'(0+)$.
For a convex function, the right derivative at zero cannot exceed the
endpoint chord slope. This strict reverse inequality proves failure of
convexity.

Finally, support alone cannot give the required upper bound for a product
polynomial. Let $C=I+P_4$, where $P_4$ is a four-cycle permutation matrix.
Its product polynomial has central coefficient two and squared Fischer norm $36$, so
$N(p_C)/(p_C)_{\mathbf1}^2=9$.
On the same support $S$, the exponential-generating polynomial
$p_S=\sum_{\alpha\in S}x^\alpha/\alpha!$ is balanced by cyclic symmetry.
Its support consists of the center, twelve patterns with $\alpha!=2$, and
two with $\alpha!=4$. Hence
\[
 N(p_S)=p_S(\mathbf1)=1+\frac{12}2+\frac24=\frac{15}2<9.
\]
Both polynomials satisfy the norm inequality, but the proposed comparison between polynomials with the same support has the wrong direction.
Extending the support inequality~\eqref{eq:lp-mconvex-norm} to arbitrary matrix-product polynomials therefore requires control of the coefficients.


\section{Verification and reproduction}\label{sec:checks}

The principal order-five, order-six, all-order permutation-polynomial, all-order four-block, and exceptional-vector theorems, and rank-two orders two through six, have Lean proofs. The other results use analytic proofs or exact computational certificates.
The accompanying repository contains the programs, outputs, and reproduction commands in
\path{research-notes/gyires-complete-support}.
The table below identifies the formalized statements.

The order-six and order-seven primary and independent checks cover $5{,}022{,}480$ coefficient positions.
The rank-two calculation covers $33{,}607$ whole parameter intervals through order $15$.
Its certificates contain $3{,}524{,}206$ coefficient positions.
These are complete finite coefficient checks, not numerical samples of matrix entries.
The cubic deletion calculation was regenerated in three forms.
The full independent calculation covers all $720$ sorting orders.
The exact configuration identities were also checked by enumerating every labeled stub matching in seven small parameter cases.
Those small cases check the implementation; the counting proof establishes the general identities.

The analytic, scalar, closure, and local polynomial arguments are proved in the text.
Their computations are identified at the claims that use them.
An exact counterexample only refutes its stated auxiliary assertion.
It does not establish the unrestricted target inequality.

\subsection{Formal rank-two theorem in orders two through six}

The separate package \path{verification/gyires-rank-two} proves the conclusions of Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-two-finite} for every order $2\le n\le6$.
Its final declaration is \path{MathAndTheory.Gyires.RankTwo.rank_two_complete} in \path{src/RangeComplete.lean}.
The hypotheses use actual matrix rank and doubly stochastic membership.
No parameter factorization or coefficient-sign hypothesis remains in the final theorem.
Zero entries are permitted.

The proof derives the parameter representation, applies separate convexity, and checks every resulting profile interval in Lean's kernel.
It proves both Gram contractions, the square contraction, the nonnegative Gyires margin, the normalized left Gram bound, the combined inequality, and equality exactly at the uniform matrix.
The package contains 92 source modules.
Its source manifest records their hashes and import graph.
The command \texttt{python3 verify.py}, run inside the package after the pinned dependency cache is available, checks every source and the final axiom report.
The permitted axioms are \path{propext}, \path{Classical.choice}, and \path{Quot.sound}.
The formal range ends at order six; the written result in orders seven through fifteen still requires complete Lean certificates.

\subsection{Order-five coefficient checks}

The order-five exact checker reconstructs the permanent from its permutation definition.
It checks all $120$ weight orderings and the unique reduction to six affine representatives.
It verifies the polynomial identities by inverse binomial transforms.
Each ordering has $1001$ coefficient positions, with $996$ positive coefficients and five zeros.
The least positive coefficient is $484$.
The four pure-gap coefficients agree with Lemma~\ref{lem:certificate}.
The computation therefore covers $120{,}120$ positions.
The checking program is
\path{verification/gyires-circulant-five/check_coefficients.py}.

The Python computation and the Lean proof give separate checks of the coefficient identities.
Neither proof depends on random tests.
The repository also preserves a PARI/GP program from~\cite{repo}; its full set of checks was not rerun.

\subsection{Formal certificate for the order-six theorem}

The Lean certificate applies to every nonnegative real vector
$w\colon\operatorname{Fin}(6)\to\mathbb{R}$ and the matrix
$C_{ij}=w(j-i)$, with indices modulo six. It includes zero weights and
repeated weights. The declarations use the namespace
\path{MathAndTheory.Gyires.CirculantSix}.

In \texttt{SixPrincipal.lean}, \texttt{sorted\_margin} proves
\[
  324\operatorname{per}(C)^2-5\operatorname{per}(CC^{\mathsf T})
  \geq 296\sum_{j=1}^{5}(w_{(j+1)}-w_{(j)})^{12},
\]
where $w_{(1)}\leq\cdots\leq w_{(6)}$ is any sorted ordering of the
weights. The declaration \texttt{sorted\_gyires\_margin} proves the
corresponding combined deficit bound
\[
  1296\operatorname{per}(C)^2
  -5\bigl[\operatorname{per}(CC^{\mathsf T})
       +\operatorname{per}(C^{\mathsf T}C)
       +2\operatorname{per}(C^2)\bigr]
  \geq 1184\sum_{j=1}^{5}(w_{(j+1)}-w_{(j)})^{12}.
\]
The declarations \texttt{strong\_inequality} and \texttt{gyires} give
the two inequalities without the gap terms. The declarations
\texttt{strong\_equality} and \texttt{gyires\_equality} prove that
either of those inequalities has equality exactly when all six weights
are equal. The declaration \path{gyires_doubly_stochastic} gives
the normalized inequality and identifies its equality matrix as the
constant matrix with entries $1/6$.

The coefficient $296$ is sharp. In \texttt{SixSharpness.lean},
\texttt{margin\_coefficient\_le} uses the weights $(0,0,0,0,1,1)$
to prove that this coefficient cannot increase. In
\path{SixNormalizedSharpness.lean},
\path{normalized_margin_coefficient_le} proves the same statement
within the doubly stochastic class, using $(0,0,0,0,1/2,1/2)$.

The source package is \path{verification/gyires-circulant-six}.
This directory contains 3,091 Lean
source modules, a relative-path source manifest, and the pinned Lean
4.35.0 and package configuration. From that directory, run
\texttt{lake exe cache get}, then \texttt{python3 verify.py}.
The verifier rebuilds the local source set and checks its file hashes.
Success requires a zero exit code and \texttt{complete: true} in
\path{.verification/result.json}. The root source,
\texttt{SixTheoremChecks.lean}, restates five matrix conclusions and
reports the axioms of ten named theorems. The reported axioms are
\texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice}, and \texttt{Quot.sound}.
The package's \texttt{THEOREMS.md} maps the results to their source files.


\subsection{Permutation-polynomial Lean correspondence}

Theorem~\ref{thm:permutation-polynomial} has a complete Lean proof in every dimension.
The proof includes the actual cyclic permanent, both Gram products, the transfer identities, and the positive-root lower bound.
Explicit formulas cover orders zero through two.
Polynomial identities cover orders three through twenty-two, and the weighted trace bound covers every order at least twenty-three.
Cycle decomposition then proves the result for every finite permutation.
The final theorem has no assumed cycle inequality.

The source package is
\path{verification/gyires-permutation-polynomial}.
It contains 405 Lean modules and pins Lean~4.35.0 and Mathlib revision
\path{56a005855ea17e0b0958f73ec801ae140b0a1679}.
It is separate from the shared Comparator selection.
Its README gives the dependency, source-integrity, build, and axiom-check commands.

The following declarations use the namespace
\path{MathAndTheory.Gyires.PermutationComplete}.
Their source is \path{src/PermutationComplete.lean} within that package.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.37\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.57\linewidth}}
\toprule
Statement & Lean declaration\\
\midrule
Both Gram bounds for every cycle order & \path{cycle_all_gram_bounds}\\
Both Gram bounds for every permutation & \path{permutation_polynomial_gram_bounds}\\
The bound for the original square $A^2$ & \path{permutation_polynomial_square_bound}\\
The combined Gyires inequality & \path{permutation_polynomial_gyires_bound}\\
Strict square bound for a cycle, $n\ge23$ & \path{cycle_strict_square_bound}\\
Strict combined bound for a cycle, $n\ge23$ & \path{cycle_strict_gyires_bound}\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The last two statements require at least one positive weight.
The four expanded statements in \path{src/CompleteAudit.lean} write
$A=aI+bQ+cQ^2$, the Mathlib matrix products and permanent, and $n!/n^n$ directly.
The formal statements include zero weights, fixed points, and the empty dimension.

The finite proof uses three reflection-reduced gap regions for each order from three through twenty-two.
This gives 60 closed regions and 25,050 possible homogeneous coefficient positions.
The exact polynomial identities connect their nonnegative coefficients to the actual matrix permanents.
Lean checks these identities in its kernel.
No coefficient sign is an assumption, and numerical samples are not used as a proof.

The weighted trace argument proves the exact bound at order 23 and every later order by induction.
It includes both endpoints of the normalized parameter interval and the zero-weight matrix.
The permanent comparison for $A^2$ uses the original matrix indexing.
The ten final axiom reports contain only
\texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice}, and \texttt{Quot.sound}.
The proof uses no \texttt{native\_decide}.

The accompanying ordinary certificate record extends through order 47.
It contains 135 reflection-reduced regions and 224,550 coefficient positions.
Every coefficient is nonnegative, and every position is positive in orders four through forty-seven.
The program \path{independent_finite.mjs} in
\path{research-notes/gyires-extensions/certificates} reproduces that record with argument \texttt{47}.
The smaller formal certificate range and this wider ordinary computation are separate checks of the same deficit polynomials.
The programs \path{circ_small_check.py} and \path{sharp_bound_check.py} check the small-order transfer identities and the additional spectral estimate from order 48.
Those computational records remain supporting material for the stated ordinary arguments.

\subsection{The four-block argument}

Theorem~\ref{thm:four-block} uses no finite numerical certificate.
Its proof derives the likelihood bound for all admissible Bernoulli parameters.
The subsequent coefficient identities hold for arbitrary dimension.
The equality argument and the closed parameter interval include every boundary case in the stated family.
Finite examples can test an implementation, but they do not replace this argument.

\subsection{Four-block Lean correspondence}

Theorem~\ref{thm:four-block} has a complete Lean proof.
The formal statement uses Mathlib's matrix product, transpose, permanent, and doubly stochastic predicate.
Its hypotheses count the distinct row vectors and column vectors.
The proof permits unequal class sizes and zero entries.
It also covers one row type, one column type, and finite orders zero and one.
The four principal statements are in the namespace
\path{MathAndTheory.GyiresFourBlock}:
\path{contractions}, \path{margin}, \path{gyires}, and \path{gyires_eq_iff}.
The independent specifications are in
\path{lean/Challenges/GyiresCirculant5.lean}.
The matching proofs are in
\path{lean/Solutions/GyiresFourBlock.lean}.

The supporting library is
\path{lean/MathAndTheory/Mathematics/Gyires/FourBlock.lean}.
The probability proof establishes Lemmas~\ref{lem:fb-maximizer} and~\ref{lem:fb-adjacent}
and the bounds and equality case in Proposition~\ref{prop:fb-central}.
It maximizes a linear likelihood difference at a fixed mean.
A shifted-binomial coefficient recurrence gives the central bound.
The matrix proof then uses a permutation average to prove the contractions.
This route proves the full matrix statement without formalizing each intermediate coefficient identity in the ordinary proof.

\subsection{Exceptional-vector Lean correspondence}

The result for one exceptional column or row has a complete Lean proof for every $n\ge2$.
The predicate states directly that all columns except one are identical, or that all rows except one are identical.
The exceptional vector can have arbitrarily many distinct entries, and zero entries are permitted.
The formal theorem proves both strong Gram bounds in~\eqref{eq:e-1} and Gyires's inequality in~\eqref{eq:e-2}.
Equality in each Gram bound, and in Gyires's inequality, holds exactly at $J_n$.
The matrix square is the original product $A^2$.

The four statements use the namespace \path{MathAndTheory.GyiresExceptional}:
\path{strong}, \path{gram_eq_iff}, \path{gyires}, and \path{gyires_eq_iff}.
Their independent specifications are in \path{lean/Challenges/GyiresCirculant5.lean}.
Their proofs are in \path{lean/Solutions/GyiresExceptional.lean}.
The supporting library ends in \path{MathAndTheory.Mathematics.Gyires.ExceptionalVector}.
It proves the one-success probability bounds in both second-moment ranges.
It includes deterministic parameters and the exact coefficient identities for the matrix permanents.
The four-block theorem covers orders zero and one separately.

\subsection{Order-five Lean correspondence}
Lean~\cite{lean} is a \emph{proof assistant}. It checks a formal proof against
its statement using basic logical rules. The principal order-five results in
Section~\ref{sec:results} are proved with Lean 4.35.0 and
Mathlib~\cite{mathlib}, its library of formalized mathematics.

The collection library is in
\path{lean/MathAndTheory/Mathematics/GyiresCirculant5}.
Its namespace is \path{MathAndTheory.GyiresCirculant5}.
The formalization is based on~\cite{repo}.

In the library, \texttt{circ w} is the circulant with entries
\texttt{w (j - i)}. The definition \texttt{P5} is the polynomial in
Lemma~\ref{lem:P}. The definitions \texttt{gram} and \texttt{conv} give
the vectors $g$ and $h$. The type \texttt{Fin 5}, with arithmetic modulo
five, represents $\mathbb Z/5\mathbb Z$.

The principal statements are restated, using only Mathlib's definitions, in a \emph{Challenge} file, which states the
theorems with their proofs omitted, the file
\texttt{lean/\allowbreak{}Challenges/\allowbreak{}GyiresCirculant5.lean} (namespace \path{MathAndTheory.GyiresCirculant5}). There a circulant is a matrix of
the form \texttt{Matrix.circulant v}, whose entries are \texttt{v (i - j)}; this is the transpose of $C(v)$, which
describes the same set of matrices. Doubly stochastic matrices are Mathlib's \texttt{doublyStochastic}, the permanent is
Mathlib's \texttt{Matrix.permanent}, $5!/5^5$ is the real quotient \texttt{(Nat.factorial 5 :\ $\R$) / 5 \textasciicircum{} 5},
and $J_5$ is the constant matrix with entries $5^{-1}$. The \emph{Solution} file
\texttt{lean/\allowbreak{}Solutions/\allowbreak{}GyiresCirculant5.lean} proves the same theorems from the library. Lean's
Comparator checks that the Solution theorems match the Challenge statements.
The verification directory records the statement comparison and the kernel checks by Lean, NanoDa, and con-ron, each with its source revision.
The table lists the formalized order-five statements and their supporting declarations. All names use the namespace \path{MathAndTheory.GyiresCirculant5}.
\begin{center}\small\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.30\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.64\linewidth}}
\toprule
Statement in this paper & Lean declaration(s)\\
\midrule

Theorem~\ref{thm:margin} & \lean{strong\_margin}, \lean{strong\_margin\_attained}, \lean{strong\_margin\_sharp} \\

Lean's \texttt{\#print axioms} command lists the logical assumptions used by each theorem.
The recorded checks identify only three standard Lean axioms
(\texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice}, \texttt{Quot.sound}); no step is left unproved, and the proofs use neither
\texttt{native\_decide} nor any other evaluation by compiled code. The formal proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:certificate}
follows the organization described after it: the six chamber polynomials are expanded in $z_0$ and $z_1$, and each of
the resulting polynomials in $z_2,z_3,z_4$ is written with explicit nonnegative integer coefficients, proved equal to the
corresponding expansion coefficient by the \texttt{ring} tactic and nonnegative by the \texttt{positivity} tactic. These explicit
coefficients were produced by a computer program, but Lean does not rely on that program: it checks every identity and
every inequality itself. The formal proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:gram} is not the occupancy argument given here but a
sum-of-squares argument over permutations, adapted from the formalization of Ozols~\cite{ozols}.

Several supporting results are also formalized: the case $n=2$ discussed in Section~\ref{sec:discussion} (\lean{gyires\_two}); the characterization of
the doubly stochastic circulants of order five and their description as the combinations $\sum_kw_kQ^k$
(\lean{circulant\_mem\_doublyStochastic\_iff}, \lean{circulant\_eq\_sum\_pow}); and, for real square matrices of
every order, the reduction of Gyires's inequality to the strong form in Section~\ref{sec:general}
(\lean{gyires\_of\_strong}).

\subsection{General supporting Lean results}

The modules in \path{lean/MathAndTheory/Mathematics/Gyires} prove several comparisons used beyond order five.
The module \path{PermanentCauchySchwarz.lean} proves Lemma~\ref{lem:permanent-cs} for real square matrices of every finite size.
Its proof uses the nonnegative permutation bilinear form and a quadratic discriminant.

The module \path{PermanentPermutation.lean} proves that independent row and column permutations preserve both strong Gram bounds.
It then applies those bounds to the square of the permuted matrix.
It does not assert invariance of $\per(A^2)$ under independent row and column permutations.

The module \path{DimensionConstant.lean} proves the comparison of the constants $c_n$ for positive block sizes.
The comparison is strict for at least two blocks.
This supplies the constant comparison in the cycle and direct-sum arguments.
The module \path{BlockPermanent.lean} proves Lemma~\ref{lem:struct-direct-sum}.
It proves the permanent product identity and both strong bounds for finite block sums.
The block sizes can differ, and independent row and column permutations are permitted.
The bounds are strict for at least two positive-dimensional doubly stochastic blocks.
The proof also gives Gyires's inequality for the square of the resulting matrix.
These closure results do not prove the required block hypotheses for either all-order structured class.

\subsection{Limits of the formal verification}
The formal proof establishes $R_\kappa(z)\ge0$ for all $z\ge0$, which is the part of Lemma~\ref{lem:certificate} that
the theorems need. The finer statements of that lemma (the numbers $996$ and $5$ of positive and zero coefficients,
the positions of the zeros, the four pure-power coefficients, and the least positive coefficient $484$) are not stated
in Lean; the exact computation above verifies them.
The broader matrix and polynomial results are not fully formalized.
The Cauchy--Schwarz proof in Section~\ref{sec:general} uses the coefficient inner product.
The Lean proof uses the permutation bilinear form and a quadratic discriminant.
The formal verification therefore applies to the listed declarations, rather than to every argument in this paper.


\section{Comparison of the results and remaining questions}\label{sec:discussion}

The common reduction in Section~\ref{sec:general} explains how the matrix arguments address the same conjecture.
The circulant and permutation-polynomial proofs establish a strong Gram-permanent bound.
The four-block and finite rank-two proofs establish the sharper contractions $\per(AA^\T)\le\per(A)$ and $\per(A^\T A)\le\per(A)$ on their respective classes.
Those contractions are not valid for all matrices covered by the circulant results.

\subsection{A two-point example and a distinction between the bounds}

For the order-five weight vector $w=(t,t,0,0,0)$,
\[
g(w)=(2t^2,t^2,0,0,t^2),\qquad h(w)=(t^2,2t^2,t^2,0,0),
\]
and
\[
P(w)=2t^5,\qquad P(g(w))=P(h(w))=84t^{10},\qquad D_s(w)=D_G(w)=484t^{10}.
\]
This is the sharpness example in Theorem~\ref{thm:margin}.
At $t=1/2$, the matrix is doubly stochastic and
\[
\per(C)=\frac1{16},\qquad \per(CC^\T)=\per(C^2)=\frac{21}{256}.
\]
Thus $\per(CC^\T)>\per(C)$, although the strong bound and Gyires's inequality both hold.
The Gyires ratio is $1/21>24/625$.
The two-point example also belongs to the permutation-polynomial family.
It shows why the four-block contraction theorem must retain its row-type and column-type hypotheses.
An earlier order-four contraction counterexample is attributed to Newman in the account of Best and Wanless~\cite{bw}.

\subsection{The elementary order-two case}

For
$A=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}a&1-a\\1-a&a\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$ with $0\le a\le1$, put
$s=a^2+(1-a)^2$ and $t=2a(1-a)$.
Then $\per(A)=s$, and each permanent of a product in Gyires's denominator is $s^2+t^2$.
The ratio inequality reduces to $s\ge t$, which is $(2a-1)^2\ge0$.
Equality holds only at $a=1/2$.
This elementary case is also covered by the four-block theorem.

\subsection{What remains outside the proofs}

For a general nonnegative circulant, normality gives $CC^\T=C^\T C$.
The permanent comparison still gives $\per(C^2)\le\per(CC^\T)$.
The exact certificates for unrestricted circulant weights cover orders five through seven. The strong Gram bound for unrestricted circulant weights in arbitrary order remains unproved here.
The three-term transfer construction does not supply that step.

A general matrix of rank two can have more than two row types and more than two column types.
The four-block theorem does not cover that full class. The finite rank-two theorem covers orders through fifteen. The all-order rank-two assertion remains unproved here.
The unrestricted Gyires conjecture and the general strong Gram inequality remain outside this manuscript.

The order-seven result and rank-two orders 7 through 15 still require complete Lean proofs.
Each proof must connect its polynomial identities and coefficient bounds to the actual matrix theorem.
The permutation-polynomial proof includes this connection in every dimension.
